
\medterm{5-Alpha Reductase Inhibitor} A medicine that blocks conversion of testosterone to dihydrotestosterone. It is used mainly to treat
benign prostatic hyperplasia and androgen-related hair loss.

\medterm{Adaptive Design} A clinical-study design that permits planned changes in response to accumulating data while preserving validity.

\medterm{ADME} Absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion: the processes governing a substance’s movement through the body.

\synonyms
Pharmacokinetics

\medterm{Administration Procedure} A procedure used to deliver a medicine, fluid, vaccine, or other intervention to a patient.

\medterm{Adnexal Dysplasia} Abnormal precancerous or developmental cellular changes in tissue beside the uterus, such as the ovary or tube.

\medterm{ADP-Ribosylation} Addition of an ADP-ribose group to a protein or other molecule, altering its activity or interactions.

\medterm{Adrenomedullin} A peptide hormone that promotes blood-vessel dilation and helps regulate circulation and fluid balance.

\medterm{Adsorption} Attachment of molecules to the surface of a solid or liquid rather than absorption into its interior.

\medterm{Aerobiosis} A state or process requiring the presence of oxygen.

\medterm{Aerosol} A suspension of fine solid particles or liquid droplets in a gas.

\medterm{Affinity Chromatography} A laboratory separation method that isolates a molecule through its specific binding to an immobilized partner.

\medterm{Agammaglobulinemia} A severe deficiency or absence of immunoglobulins, causing recurrent infections.

\medterm{Aicardi's Syndrome} A rare developmental brain disorder characterized classically by absence of the corpus callosum, seizures, and distinctive retinal lesions.

\synonyms
Aicardi syndrome

\medterm{Ainhum} A rare disorder causing a constricting groove around a toe that may eventually lead to autoamputation.

\synonyms
Dactylolysis spontanea

\medterm{Alarmin} A molecule released by damaged or stressed cells that activates innate immune responses.

\medterm{Alcoholic Hepatitis} Inflammation and liver injury caused by heavy alcohol use.

\medterm{Alcoholic Intoxication} Impaired judgment, coordination, or consciousness caused by acute alcohol exposure.

\synonyms
Alcohol intoxication

\medterm{Alcoholism} A chronic disorder characterized by impaired control over alcohol use and continued use despite harm.

\synonyms
Alcohol use disorder

\medterm{Alexithymia} Difficulty identifying, describing, or distinguishing one’s emotional experiences.

\medterm{Alkaptonuria} An inherited disorder of tyrosine metabolism causing homogentisic-acid accumulation and darkening of urine or tissues.

\medterm{Anorectal Infection} Infection involving the anus, rectum, or surrounding tissues.

\medterm{Aortopulmonary Window} A congenital opening between the ascending aorta and pulmonary artery.

\medterm{Aponeurosis} A broad, flat sheet of connective tissue that attaches muscle to bone or other tissues.

\medterm{Apoptotic Body} A membrane-bound fragment of a cell undergoing programmed cell death.

\medterm{Arterial Embolization} Intentional blockage of an artery, often to control bleeding or reduce blood supply to a tumor.

\medterm{Arterial Injury} Damage to an artery that may cause bleeding, narrowing, clotting, or impaired blood flow.

\medterm{Arterial Stenosis} Abnormal narrowing of an artery.

\medterm{Arterial Thrombosis} Formation of a blood clot within an artery.

\medterm{Arteritis} Inflammation of an artery.

\medterm{Artery Occlusion} Complete or near-complete blockage of an artery.

\medterm{Arthropod Vector} An arthropod, such as a mosquito or tick, that transmits an infectious agent between hosts.

\synonyms
Disease vector

\medterm{Arthus Reaction} A localized immune-complex reaction causing inflammation at the site of antigen exposure.

\synonyms
AI

\medterm{Artificial Saliva} A product used to moisten the mouth and relieve symptoms of dry mouth.

\synonyms
Saliva substitute

\medterm{Assay} A laboratory procedure used to detect or measure a substance, activity, or biological response.

\medterm{Asthenozoospermia} Reduced sperm motility, which may contribute to difficulty conceiving.

\synonyms
Asthenospermia

\medterm{Asymptomatic Infections} Infections present without noticeable symptoms.

\synonyms
Subclinical infections

\medterm{Atopic Rhinitis} Allergic inflammation of the nasal lining causing sneezing, itching, congestion, or discharge.

\synonyms
Allergic rhinitis

\medterm{Atrial Ablation} A procedure that destroys selected heart tissue to prevent or control an atrial arrhythmia.

\synonyms
Cardiac ablation

\medterm{Atrophoderma Maculatum} A rare skin condition causing localized, depressed patches of altered pigmentation and tissue thinning.

\medterm{Atypia Morphology} Abnormal cellular appearance or architecture that may be benign, reactive, precancerous, or cancerous depending on context.

\synonyms
Cytologic atypia

\medterm{Augmentation Mammoplasty} Surgery to increase breast size or alter breast shape, commonly using implants or fat transfer.

\synonyms
Breast augmentation

\medterm{Avoidance Behavior} Deliberate behavior intended to prevent contact with a feared, distressing, or threatening situation.

\medterm{Axis Vertebra} The second cervical vertebra, which supports rotation of the first cervical vertebra and head.

\synonyms
C2; axis

\medterm{Axolemma} The cell membrane surrounding an axon.

\medterm{Adherens Junction} A cell-to-cell connection that uses cadherin proteins to link neighboring cells through actin filaments.

\medterm{Adipocyte} A cell specialized to store and release fat.

\synonyms
Fat cell

\medterm{Adipogenesis} The formation and maturation of fat cells.

\medterm{Adipokine} A signaling molecule released by adipose tissue that influences metabolism or inflammation.

\medterm{Adjustment Disorders} Emotional or behavioral symptoms occurring in response to an identifiable stressor and causing significant distress or impairment.

\synonyms
Adjustment disorder

\medterm{Adnexal Mass} A growth or enlargement involving structures beside the uterus, such as an ovary or fallopian tube.

\medterm{Adnexitis} Inflammation of an adnexal structure, usually the fallopian tube or ovary.

\synonyms
Salpingo-oophoritis

\medterm{Adoptive Immunotherapy} Treatment in which immune cells are collected, modified or activated, and returned to the patient.

\medterm{Adrenal Cancer} Cancer arising in the adrenal gland.

\synonyms
Adrenocortical cancer

\medterm{Adrenal Medulla} The inner part of the adrenal gland that produces epinephrine and norepinephrine.

\medterm{Adrenergic Agent} A substance that affects receptors responsive to adrenaline-like neurotransmitters.

\medterm{Adrenergic Agonist} A substance that activates adrenergic receptors.

\synonyms
Sympathomimetic

\medterm{Adrenergic Antagonists} Substances that block the effects of adrenaline-like signaling at adrenergic receptors.

\synonyms
Adrenergic blockers

\medterm{Adrenergic Receptor} A cell receptor activated by epinephrine or norepinephrine.

\medterm{Adrenoleukodystrophy} An inherited disorder causing abnormal buildup of very-long-chain fatty acids and progressive nervous-system or adrenal damage.

\synonyms
ALD

\medterm{Adult Rickets} Defective bone mineralization in adulthood, usually called osteomalacia.

\synonyms
Osteomalacia

\medterm{Adventitia} The outer connective-tissue layer of a blood vessel or hollow organ.

\synonyms
Tunica adventitia

\medterm{Adverse Reaction} An unintended, harmful response to a medicine, treatment, or other exposure.

\synonyms
Adverse drug reaction

\medterm{Aerobic Exercise} Physical activity that uses sustained oxygen-dependent energy production, such as walking or cycling.

\synonyms
Cardio exercise

\medterm{Afferent Pathway} A sensory nerve route carrying information from the body toward the brain or spinal cord.

\synonyms
Sensory pathway

\medterm{Afibrinogenemia} A rare inherited or acquired absence of fibrinogen, causing impaired blood clotting.

\medterm{African Trypanosomiasis} A parasitic infection transmitted by tsetse flies and affecting the blood and nervous system.

\synonyms
African sleeping sickness

\medterm{Agglutination Test} A laboratory test in which particles visibly clump when a specific antibody or antigen is present.

\medterm{Agglutinin} An antibody or other substance that causes cells or particles to clump together.

\medterm{AIDS Vaccine} A vaccine designed to prevent HIV infection or reduce its effects; no generally effective licensed vaccine currently exists.

\medterm{Air Sac} A cavity or thin-walled space that contains air, such as a pulmonary alveolus or bird air sac.

\synonyms
Air space

\medterm{Air Trapping} Retention of air in the lungs because it cannot be fully exhaled, often from airway obstruction.

\medterm{Alanine} An amino acid used in protein synthesis and glucose metabolism.

\medterm{Albumin Clearance} The removal rate of albumin from blood or another body compartment, used in selected physiologic studies.

\medterm{Alveolar Macrophage} An immune cell in the lung air sacs that removes particles, microbes, and cellular debris.

\synonyms
Dust cell

\medterm{Alzheimer's} A progressive neurodegenerative disease causing memory loss and decline in thinking and daily function.

\synonyms
Alzheimer disease

\medterm{Ambulance} A vehicle equipped to transport and provide emergency medical care to ill or injured people.

\medterm{Amebic Colitis} Inflammation of the colon caused by infection with Entamoeba histolytica.

\synonyms
Amoebic colitis

\medterm{Amnioscope} An instrument used to inspect amniotic fluid and the membranes through the cervix.

\medterm{Amyloid Protein} A normally soluble protein that has misfolded into insoluble fibrils and may accumulate in tissues.

\synonyms
Amyloid

\medterm{Anaerobic} Able to live, grow, or function without oxygen.

\medterm{Anaerobic Bacteria} Bacteria that grow without oxygen; some are harmed by oxygen.

\medterm{Anal Carcinoma} Cancer arising in the anus or anal canal.

\synonyms
Anal cancer

\medterm{Anal Mucositis} Inflammation and ulceration of the anal mucous membrane, often after radiation or chemotherapy.

\medterm{Anal Necrosis} Death of tissue in or around the anus.

\medterm{Anal Pain} Pain felt in or around the anus.

\synonyms
Proctalgia

\medterm{Angiogenesis Inhibitor} A treatment or substance that prevents or slows formation of new blood vessels.

\synonyms
Antiangiogenic agent

\medterm{Antinuclear Autoantibody} An autoantibody directed against components of a cell nucleus.

\synonyms
ANA

\medterm{Antisepsis} Use of chemical methods to reduce or eliminate harmful microorganisms on living tissue.

\medterm{Antiserum} Blood serum containing antibodies against a particular antigen or toxin.

\medterm{Antisocial Personality} A persistent pattern of disregard for other people’s rights, social rules, and responsibilities.

\synonyms
Antisocial personality disorder

\medterm{Antitoxin} An antibody or preparation that neutralizes a toxin.

\medterm{Alpha-Fetoprotein} A protein produced mainly by the fetal liver; maternal or blood levels can help assess certain fetal and adult conditions.

\synonyms
AFP

\medterm{Alpha-Globulin} A group of blood proteins that includes transport and acute-phase proteins.

\medterm{Alpha-Synuclein} A neuronal protein that can accumulate abnormally in disorders such as Parkinson disease and Lewy-body dementia.

\medterm{Alpha-Thalassemia} An inherited disorder in which reduced alpha-globin production impairs hemoglobin formation.

\medterm{Amniotic Band} A strand of amniotic tissue that can entangle or constrict developing fetal body parts.

\synonyms
Amniotic band sequence

\medterm{Aortic Body} A small group of chemoreceptor cells near the aortic arch that helps sense blood oxygen and carbon dioxide.

\medterm{Aortic Injury} Damage to the aorta, often caused by severe trauma or a medical procedure.

\medterm{Aortic Rupture} A life-threatening tear through the wall of the aorta causing internal bleeding.

\medterm{Anaplastic Astrocytoma} A high-grade astrocytic brain tumor with rapid growth and infiltrative behavior.

\medterm{Anaplastic Ependymoma} An aggressive, high-grade tumor arising from cells lining the brain’s ventricles or spinal canal.

\medterm{Anatomical Conduit} A natural or surgically created passage that carries fluid, air, tissue, or another structure.

\medterm{Anatomical Orifice} A natural opening into or through a body structure.

\synonyms
Body opening

\medterm{Anatomist} A specialist who studies the structure of the body.

\medterm{Androgen Excess} Abnormally high androgen activity or concentration, which can cause acne, excess hair, or menstrual changes.

\synonyms
Hyperandrogenism

\medterm{Androgen Insufficiency} Inadequate androgen production or activity for a person’s physiologic needs.

\synonyms
Androgen deficiency

\medterm{Androgen Receptor} A cellular protein that binds testosterone or related hormones and mediates their effects.

\medterm{Androgen Suppression} Reduction of androgen production or action, used in selected hormone-sensitive conditions.

\synonyms
Androgen deprivation

\medterm{Anesthesiologist} A physician who provides anesthesia and manages pain and vital functions during procedures.

\medterm{Anesthesiology} The medical specialty concerned with anesthesia, perioperative care, and pain management.

\medterm{Anesthetic} A medicine or technique that causes loss of sensation, with or without loss of consciousness.

\medterm{Angiocardiography} Imaging of the heart and major vessels after injection of contrast material.

\medterm{Angiofibroma} A benign tumor composed of blood vessels and fibrous tissue.

\medterm{Angioid Streak} Breaks in a tissue layer beneath the retina that appear as irregular dark lines.

\medterm{Ankle} The joint connecting the lower leg to the foot.

\medterm{Ankyloglossia} An abnormally short or tight lingual frenulum that restricts tongue movement.

\synonyms
Tongue-tie

\medterm{Ankyrin} A structural protein that links membrane proteins to the cell’s internal cytoskeleton.

\medterm{Anomia} Difficulty retrieving or naming familiar words.

\medterm{Anorectal Fistula} An abnormal channel connecting the anus or rectum with nearby skin or another organ.

\synonyms
Anal fistula

\medterm{Antigen Receptor} A receptor on an immune cell that specifically recognizes an antigen.

\medterm{Antiplatelet Agent} A medicine that reduces platelet clumping and lowers the risk of arterial clots.

\synonyms
Antiplatelet medicine

\medterm{Aortic Disease} Any disorder affecting the aorta, including aneurysm, dissection, narrowing, or inflammation.

\medterm{Arthroscope} A small illuminated instrument used to view the inside of a joint.

\medterm{Articular Ligament} A band of connective tissue that stabilizes a joint by connecting bone to bone.

\synonyms
Joint ligament

\medterm{Artificial Heart} A mechanical device that replaces or supports the pumping function of the heart.

\medterm{Artificial Skin} A biological or synthetic material used to cover wounds and support skin regeneration.

\synonyms
Skin substitute

\medterm{Ascitic Fluid} Excess fluid collected in the abdominal cavity.

\synonyms
Ascites

\medterm{Asphyxia Neonatorum} Failure of a newborn to establish adequate breathing at or shortly after birth.

\synonyms
Birth asphyxia

\medterm{Aspirate (Substance)} Material withdrawn from a body cavity, lesion, or airway by suction.

\synonyms
Aspirated material

\medterm{Assisted Circulation} Mechanical or procedural support used to maintain blood flow when the heart cannot pump adequately.

\synonyms
Mechanical circulatory support

\medterm{Association Cortex} Cerebral cortex involved in integrating information and supporting memory, language, judgment, and complex behavior.

\medterm{Astringent} A substance that contracts tissue and reduces secretions or minor bleeding.

\medterm{Astrocyte} A star-shaped glial cell that supports neurons, maintains the blood-brain barrier, and regulates the nervous-system environment.

\medterm{Asymptomatic} Having a disease or abnormality without noticeable symptoms.

\medterm{Ataxia Telangiectasia} An inherited disorder causing progressive coordination problems, visible small blood vessels, and immune deficiency.

\synonyms
A-T

\medterm{Atrial Arrhythmia} An abnormal heart rhythm originating in the atria.

\medterm{Atrial Standstill} Absence of normal electrical and mechanical activity in the atria.

\medterm{Atrial Thrombosis} Formation of a blood clot within an atrium of the heart.

\medterm{Atrioventricular Bundle} Conducting fibers that carry electrical impulses from the atrioventricular node to the ventricles.

\synonyms
Bundle of His

\medterm{Attenuated Vaccine} A vaccine made with a weakened microorganism that retains the ability to stimulate immunity.

\synonyms
Live attenuated vaccine

\medterm{Atypical Lymphocyte} A lymphocyte with an unusual appearance, often reflecting immune activation or infection.

\medterm{Audiology} The science and clinical evaluation of hearing, balance, and related disorders.

\medterm{Auditory Area} A region of the cerebral cortex that processes sound information.

\synonyms
Auditory cortex

\medterm{Auditory Fatigue} Temporary reduction in hearing sensitivity or listening performance after prolonged or intense sound exposure.

\medterm{Auditory Ossicle} One of the three small middle-ear bones that transmit sound vibrations.

\synonyms
Ear ossicle

\medterm{Auditory Pathway} The neural route carrying sound information from the inner ear to the auditory cortex.

\medterm{Auditory Threshold} The quietest sound intensity a person can detect at a specified frequency.

\synonyms
Hearing threshold

\medterm{Autism} A neurodevelopmental condition involving persistent differences in social communication and restricted or repetitive patterns of behavior.

\synonyms
Autism spectrum disorder

\medterm{Autoantigen} A normal body molecule mistakenly targeted by the immune system in an autoimmune disease.

\medterm{Autonomic Disorder} Dysfunction of the autonomic nervous system, which regulates involuntary cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, sudomotor, urinary, sexual, and pupillary functions. It may be primary or secondary to conditions such as diabetes, neurodegenerative disease, autoimmune neuropathy, infection, or medication effects, and may cause orthostatic hypotension, abnormal heart-rate responses, sweating abnormalities, gastrointestinal dysmotility, bladder dysfunction, or sexual dysfunction. Evaluation and treatment depend on the underlying cause and the organs affected.

\medterm{Autonomic Ganglion} A cluster of nerve-cell bodies in the autonomic nervous system that relays signals to organs.

\medterm{Autoradiography} An imaging technique that uses radioactive labels to show the location of substances in tissue.

\medterm{Autosome} Any chromosome other than a sex chromosome.

\synonyms
Non-sex chromosome

\medterm{Awareness} Conscious recognition of oneself, surroundings, thoughts, or sensations.

\synonyms
Consciousness

\medterm{Axon Fasciculation} Bundling of developing axons into organized nerve tracts.

\medterm{Axon Guidance} The developmental process directing growing axons toward their correct targets.

\medterm{Axon Hillock} The region of a neuron where action potentials are usually initiated.

\medterm{Axonal Transport} Movement of proteins, organelles, and other materials along an axon.

\medterm{Axoneme} The internal microtubule-based structure of a cilium or flagellum.

\medterm{Addictive Behavior} Repeated substance use or rewarding behavior that becomes difficult to control despite harmful consequences.

\medterm{AIDS-Associated Nephropathy} Kidney injury associated with advanced HIV infection.

\synonyms
HIV-associated nephropathy

\medterm{Akinetic Mutism} A state of profound lack of movement and speech despite wakefulness and preserved basic alertness.

\medterm{Allergic Reaction} An immune response to an otherwise harmless substance, ranging from mild symptoms to anaphylaxis.

\synonyms
Allergy

\medterm{Amnioinfusion} Infusion of sterile fluid into the amniotic sac, often to reduce umbilical-cord compression during labor.

\medterm{Amyloid Neuropathy} Peripheral nerve damage caused by deposition of abnormal amyloid protein.

\medterm{Angiogenesis Inhibition} Suppression of new blood-vessel formation, a strategy used in some cancer treatments.

\synonyms
Antiangiogenic therapy

\medterm{Ankle Fracture} A break in one or more bones forming the ankle joint.

\medterm{Anterior Chamber} The fluid-filled space between the cornea and iris of the eye.

\medterm{Anti-Anxiety Agents} Medicines or other treatments used to reduce clinically significant anxiety.

\synonyms
Anxiolytics

\medterm{Antibody Therapy} Treatment using antibodies to block, activate, or target a specific molecule or cell.

\synonyms
Immunotherapy

\medterm{Anticonvulsant} A medicine used to prevent or control seizures; some are also used for nerve pain or mood disorders.

\synonyms
Antiseizure medicine

\medterm{Antigen Presentation} Display of antigen fragments on cell surfaces for recognition by T cells.

\medterm{Antihypertensive Agent} A medicine or treatment that lowers elevated blood pressure.

\synonyms
Blood-pressure medicine

\medterm{Antipsychotic Agent} A medicine used to treat psychosis and, in some cases, bipolar disorder or severe behavioral symptoms.

\synonyms
Antipsychotic

\medterm{Apathy} Reduced motivation, emotional responsiveness, or interest in activities.

\medterm{Appetite} The desire or drive to eat.

\medterm{Arthropathy} Any disease or disorder affecting a joint.

\synonyms
Joint disease

\medterm{Artificial Insemination} Placement of sperm into the reproductive tract by a medical procedure to assist conception.

\synonyms
Intrauterine insemination

\medterm{Aseptic Meningitis} Inflammation of the meninges without evidence of typical bacterial meningitis, often caused by viruses or medicines.

\medterm{Autoimmune Thyroiditis} Immune-mediated inflammation and damage of the thyroid gland.

\synonyms
Hashimoto thyroiditis

\medterm{Axial Skeleton} The bones of the head, neck, and trunk, including the skull, spine, ribs, and sternum.

\medterm{Axillary Lymphadenectomy} Surgical removal of lymph nodes from the armpit.

\synonyms
Axillary node dissection

\medterm{Abandoned Lead} A disconnected or no-longer-used wire from an implanted cardiac or other electrical device that remains in the body.

\synonyms
Retained device lead

\medterm{Abdominal Fluid} Fluid within the abdominal cavity; excessive accumulation is called ascites.

\synonyms
Peritoneal fluid

\medterm{Abdominal Neoplasm} An abnormal tissue growth arising in an abdominal organ or structure; it may be benign or malignant.

\synonyms
Abdominal tumor

\medterm{Abdominoperineal Resection} Surgery removing the rectum and anus, usually with a permanent colostomy.

\synonyms
APR

\medterm{Ablepharon} A congenital absence or severe underdevelopment of an eyelid.

\medterm{Abnormal} Not typical of healthy structure, function, or measurement.

\medterm{Abnormal Color} A color change in skin, mucosa, secretions, or another tissue that differs from the expected appearance.

\synonyms
Discoloration

\medterm{Abnormal Fissure} An unusual crack, groove, or cleft in a tissue or organ.

\medterm{Abnormal Virilization} Development of masculinizing physical features in a person whose expected sex development does not include them, often from excess androgens.

\synonyms
Virilization

\medterm{Abnormality} A structural, functional, or developmental difference from the usual state.

\synonyms
Anomaly

\medterm{Abomasum} The fourth and glandular compartment of the stomach in ruminant animals.

\synonyms
True stomach

\medterm{Abrin} A highly toxic plant protein from rosary pea seeds that inhibits cellular protein synthesis.

\medterm{Absence Epilepsy} A generalized epilepsy characterized by brief episodes of impaired awareness, often with staring and subtle movements.

\synonyms
Absence seizures

\medterm{Absent Reflex} Failure of an expected tendon or neurologic reflex to occur when stimulated.

\medterm{Acalculous Cholecystitis} Inflammation of the gallbladder specifically without gallstones, usually acute and occurring in critically ill, injured, postoperative, fasting, or shocked patients. Bile stasis and gallbladder ischemia contribute to the disease, which may progress rapidly to gangrene or perforation. Fever, right-upper-quadrant pain, leukocytosis, or unexplained sepsis may occur; ultrasound is commonly used for evaluation.

\medterm{Acanthocyte} An abnormally shaped red blood cell with irregular thorn-like projections.

\synonyms
Spur cell

\medterm{Acantholysis} Loss of attachment between epidermal cells, causing cells to separate.

\medterm{Acanthoma} A benign growth composed of thickened squamous epidermal cells.

\medterm{Acatalasia} A rare inherited deficiency of catalase, an enzyme that breaks down hydrogen peroxide.

\synonyms
Catalase deficiency

\medterm{Accelerated Growth} Growth occurring faster than expected for a person’s age, tissue, or developmental stage.

\medterm{Accelerometry} Measurement of movement or acceleration, often used to assess activity, gait, or sleep.

\medterm{Accessory Nerve} The eleventh cranial nerve (CN XI), a primarily motor nerve whose spinal component supplies the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius muscles. It enables head rotation and shoulder elevation; injury may cause weak head rotation, impaired shoulder shrug, and shoulder droop.

\synonyms
Cranial nerve XI; spinal accessory nerve

\medterm{Accidental Death} Death resulting from an unintentional injury or event.

\synonyms
Unintentional death

\medterm{Accidental Fall} An unintentional descent to a lower level or the ground.

\synonyms
Fall injury

\medterm{Acephalostomia} A congenital anomaly involving absence of the head and mouth.

\medterm{Achondrogenesis} A group of severe inherited disorders in which cartilage and bone development are profoundly impaired.

\medterm{Achromatopsia} An inherited disorder causing absent or greatly reduced color vision, often with light sensitivity and poor visual acuity.

\synonyms
Complete color blindness

\medterm{Acid Phosphatase} An enzyme that works best in acidic conditions and is found in several tissues, including prostate and blood cells.

\synonyms
ACP

\medterm{Acinar Cell} A secretory cell arranged in a cluster, such as a pancreatic cell that releases digestive enzymes.

\medterm{Acne Keloid} A chronic inflammatory condition causing firm scar-like bumps around hair follicles, usually on the back of the neck.

\synonyms
Acne keloidalis nuchae

\medterm{Acquired Feminization} Development of typically female secondary sexual characteristics after birth in a person not expected to develop them, often from hormonal change.

\medterm{Acquisition Duration} The time required to obtain or record a measurement, image, or physiologic signal.

\medterm{Acrodermatitis} Inflammation affecting the skin of the hands, feet, or other distal body regions.

\medterm{Acro-Osteolysis} Resorption or destruction of the ends of the fingers or toes.

\medterm{Acrosin} An enzyme in sperm that helps it penetrate the egg’s surrounding layers.

\medterm{Acrosome} A cap-like structure on the head of a sperm cell containing enzymes needed for fertilization.

\medterm{Acrosome Reaction} Release of acrosomal enzymes that enables a sperm cell to penetrate the egg’s outer layers.

\medterm{Actigraphy} Monitoring of movement over time to estimate sleep-wake patterns and activity.

\synonyms
Actigraphic monitoring

\medterm{Actin Myofilament} A thin muscle filament made mainly of actin that interacts with myosin during contraction.

\synonyms
Thin filament

\medterm{Actin-Binding Protein} A protein that regulates, anchors, or cross-links actin filaments.

\medterm{Acting Out} Expressing emotional conflict or distress through behavior rather than words.

\medterm{Acute Disease} A condition that begins suddenly and generally lasts a limited time.

\synonyms
Acute illness

\medterm{Acute Onset Pain} Pain that begins suddenly, often over seconds, minutes, or hours.

\synonyms
Sudden-onset pain

\medterm{A-a Gradient} The difference between alveolar and arterial oxygen partial pressure. It helps assess gas exchange; an increased value
suggests ventilation-perfusion mismatch, shunt, or impaired diffusion.

\medterm{A1c} A measure of glycated hemoglobin that estimates average blood glucose over the previous two to three months. It is used to
diagnose and monitor diabetes, but some anemias and hemoglobin disorders can affect the result.

\medterm{AAA Repair} Surgical or endovascular treatment of an abdominal aortic aneurysm to prevent rupture or treat a rupture.

\medterm{Abasia} Inability to walk despite preserved strength and the ability to move the legs in other circumstances. It usually reflects a
neurologic or functional gait disorder rather than paralysis.

\medterm{ABCDE Assessment} A systematic emergency assessment of airway, breathing, circulation, disability, and exposure. Life-threatening
abnormalities are treated as they are identified, with repeated reassessment.

\medterm{ABCDE Rule for Melanoma} A screening mnemonic for suspicious pigmented lesions: asymmetry, border irregularity, color variation,
diameter greater than about 6 mm, and evolution. It does not diagnose melanoma; suspicious lesions require clinical
assessment.

\medterm{Abdomen} The body region between the diaphragm and pelvis. It contains most digestive organs, the kidneys, spleen, pancreas, and
major blood vessels and is described clinically by four quadrants or nine regions.

\medterm{Abdominal Abscess} A localized collection of pus within the abdomen, usually caused by bacterial infection, inflammation, surgery, or
organ perforation. It may cause fever and abdominal pain and often requires antibiotics and drainage.

\medterm{Abdominal Aorta} The portion of the aorta below the diaphragm that extends to its bifurcation into the common iliac arteries. It supplies
the abdominal organs, pelvic organs, and lower limbs through its branches.

\medterm{Abdominal Aortic Aneurysm} A localized enlargement of the abdominal aorta, usually defined as an aortic diameter of at least 3 cm or
more than 50 percent above normal. Enlargement can progress to rupture.

\medterm{Abdominal Aortic Aneurysm Screening} Ultrasound examination used to detect an abdominal aortic aneurysm before symptoms occur. Screening
recommendations depend on age, sex, smoking history, and clinical risk; follow-up intervals depend on aneurysm size.

\medterm{Abdominal Aortic Ultrasound} Ultrasound imaging of the abdominal aorta used to detect or monitor an abdominal aortic aneurysm.

\medterm{Abdominal Bloating} A sensation of fullness or visible enlargement of the abdomen, commonly caused by
intestinal gas, constipation, food intolerance, or functional gastrointestinal
disorders. Persistent or severe bloating may require evaluation for organic disease.

\medterm{Abdominal Bruit} An abnormal blowing or whooshing sound heard over the abdomen with a stethoscope,
usually caused by turbulent blood flow through a narrowed or enlarged artery. It may occur with
renal-artery stenosis or an abdominal aortic aneurysm. Further assessment depends on symptoms and
vascular risk factors.

\medterm{Abdominal Cavity} The cavity between the diaphragm and pelvis containing the stomach, liver, gallbladder,
spleen, pancreas, kidneys, and intestines.

\medterm{Abdominal Circumference} The circumference of the fetal abdomen measured at a standardized level that includes the
stomach, portal sinus, and spine. It is used with other ultrasound measurements to
estimate fetal size and assess growth.

\medterm{Abdominal Colic} Cramping abdominal pain caused by contractions or obstruction of a hollow organ,
such as the intestine, ureter, or bile duct. Pain often comes in waves and may be accompanied by
nausea, vomiting, sweating, or altered bowel or urinary function. Severe or persistent pain requires
prompt medical assessment.

\medterm{Abdominal Compartment Syndrome} Condition defined by sustained intra-abdominal pressure greater than twenty millimeters
of mercury associated with new organ dysfunction. Causes include massive ascites, bowel
distension from obstruction or ileus, hemorrhage, and post-surgical conditions
particularly after large abdominal wall hernia repairs.

\medterm{Abdominal CT} Computed tomography of the abdomen used to evaluate selected causes of abdominal pain, trauma,
bowel obstruction, inflammation, bleeding, and masses. The protocol and use of contrast
depend on the suspected condition and the patient's clinical status.

\medterm{Abdominal Distention} Visible or subjective enlargement, tightness, or fullness of the abdomen caused by gas,
fluid, stool, pregnancy, organ enlargement, mass, or bowel obstruction. Acute distention
with pain, vomiting, obstipation, fever, haemodynamic instability, or peritoneal signs
requires urgent evaluation.

\medterm{Abdominal Girth} The circumference of the abdomen measured at a standardized level. It is used to monitor
abdominal distention, fetal growth, obesity, ascites, and changes in intra-abdominal
volume.

\medterm{Abdominal Mass} A palpable or imaging-detected abnormal collection, enlargement, or lesion within the
abdomen. Causes include distended bowel or bladder, organ enlargement, hernia,
inflammatory collection, benign tumour, and malignancy; assessment depends on location,
mobility, tenderness, systemic features, and appropriate imaging.

\medterm{Abdominal Pain} Discomfort within the abdomen classified as visceral (dull, midline, poorly localized),
parietal (sharp, well localized, worsened by movement), or referred from a distant
source.

\medterm{Abdominal Pregnancy} An ectopic pregnancy implanted in the peritoneal cavity rather than the uterus, fallopian tubes, or ovaries.
It is rare and can cause severe internal bleeding. Diagnosis requires specialist imaging and management.

\medterm{Abdominal Radiography} Plain projectional X-ray imaging of the abdomen, sometimes obtained in supine, erect, or
decubitus positions.

\medterm{Abdominal Reflex} Contraction of the abdominal muscles after stimulation of the abdominal skin. An absent or asymmetric reflex may
indicate neurologic disease, although it can be absent normally in some people.

\medterm{Abdominal Rigidity} Involuntary tensing of the abdominal wall, usually caused by irritation of the peritoneum. It may occur with
peritonitis, perforation, severe infection, or intra-abdominal bleeding and requires clinical assessment.

\medterm{Abdominal Swelling} Visible or felt enlargement of the abdomen caused by gas, constipation, pregnancy, fluid accumulation, organ enlargement,
mass, or abdominal-wall changes. Persistent, progressive, painful, or sudden swelling—especially with
vomiting, fever, bleeding, or inability to pass stool or gas—requires medical evaluation.

\medterm{Abdominal Wall} The layered muscular, fascial, and skin-covered boundary surrounding the abdominal cavity.
It supports and protects abdominal organs and helps with posture, breathing, coughing, and straining.
Weakness or defects may produce abdominal or incisional hernias; trauma, infection, and tumors may
also affect the wall.

\medterm{Abdominal Wall Hernia} Protrusion of abdominal tissue through a weak area or opening in the abdominal wall. Common types include
inguinal, umbilical, femoral, and incisional hernias. A painful, firm, or non-reducible bulge with vomiting
or abdominal distension may indicate incarceration or strangulation and requires urgent assessment.

\medterm{Abdominal X-Ray} A plain radiograph of the abdomen used to assess selected causes of pain, obstruction, perforation, foreign bodies,
or urinary stones. It has limited sensitivity for many abdominal disorders.

\medterm{Abducens Nerve} The sixth cranial nerve, a motor nerve that supplies the lateral rectus muscle and
abducts the eye. Lesion of the nerve causes failure of lateral gaze and horizontal
diplopia.

\medterm{Abduction} Movement of a limb or other body part away from the median plane of the body.

\medterm{Abetalipoproteinemia} A rare inherited disorder in which the body cannot produce normal apolipoprotein-B-containing lipoproteins. It
causes fat malabsorption, deficiencies of fat-soluble vitamins, abnormal red cells, and possible neurologic or retinal disease.

\medterm{Abfraction} Loss of tooth structure near the gum line, possibly related to mechanical stress. It may cause tooth sensitivity and
may require dental treatment.

\medterm{ABI} The ankle-brachial index, calculated by dividing ankle systolic blood pressure by arm systolic blood pressure. A low value
suggests peripheral arterial disease.

\medterm{Abnormal Uterine Bleeding} Uterine bleeding that is abnormal in frequency, regularity, duration, or volume,
occurring outside of normal menstruation or in women who are not pregnant.

\medterm{ABO Blood Group} A blood classification based on whether red blood cells carry A antigen, B antigen, both, or neither. The four
groups are A, B, AB, and O.

\medterm{ABO Blood Typing} A laboratory test that determines a person's ABO and Rh blood groups. It is performed before transfusion,
transplantation, and some obstetric procedures.

\medterm{ABO Incompatibility} An immune reaction caused by transfusion or pregnancy involving incompatible A, B, or O
blood-group antigens. It can cause acute hemolysis, transfusion reactions, or hemolytic
disease of the newborn. See also Hemolytic Disease of the Newborn.

\medterm{Abortion} The deliberate termination of a pregnancy by medication or a procedure. The method and clinical definition of viability
vary by jurisdiction and circumstance.

\medterm{Abrasion} Superficial loss of tissue caused by friction or scraping. In dentistry, it commonly refers to mechanical loss of tooth
structure from external forces.

\medterm{Abruptio Placentae} Premature separation of the placenta from the uterine wall before delivery. It can cause vaginal bleeding,
abdominal pain, uterine tenderness, fetal distress, and maternal shock and requires urgent obstetric assessment.

\medterm{Abscess} A localized collection of pus within a tissue, organ, or cavity, typically caused by
bacterial infection. Abscesses form when bacteria (most commonly Staphylococcus aureus
including MRSA) invade tissue, triggering an intense inflammatory response with
neutrophil accumulation and tissue necrosis.

\medterm{Abscessed Tooth} A collection of pus caused by bacterial infection of the tooth or surrounding tissues, usually arising from
untreated dental decay, a cracked tooth, or periodontal disease. Pain, facial swelling, fever, and sensitivity may
occur. Dental treatment and drainage are usually required; swelling that affects swallowing or breathing is an
emergency.

\medterm{Absence Seizure} A brief generalized seizure causing sudden impaired awareness and behavioral arrest, usually followed by rapid recovery.
Electroencephalography commonly shows generalized three-hertz spike-and-wave activity.

\medterm{Absent Breath Sounds} Lack of audible air movement over part or all of a lung. Causes include pneumothorax, pleural effusion, or
airway obstruction; sudden onset requires urgent assessment.

\medterm{Absolute Neutrophil Count} The number of circulating neutrophils calculated from the white-cell count and differential. A low
ANC indicates neutropenia; severe neutropenia greatly increases the risk of serious infection.

\medterm{Absorption} The process by which a drug enters the bloodstream from its site of administration, such
as the digestive tract, skin, or muscle.

\medterm{Abusive Head Trauma} Injury to an infant or young child caused by inflicted blunt impact, violent shaking, or
both. Findings may include subdural hemorrhage, retinal hemorrhage, encephalopathy,
fractures, or other injuries; diagnosis requires careful multidisciplinary assessment
because no single finding is diagnostic.

\medterm{Acalculia} An acquired inability to perform previously learned numerical operations or understand
numerical relationships, commonly associated with dominant parietal or other cortical
dysfunction.

\medterm{Acanthamoeba Keratitis} A rare but potentially sight-threatening infection of the cornea caused by the free-living amoeba
Acanthamoeba. It is strongly associated with contaminated contact-lens water or poor lens hygiene and may cause
severe eye pain, redness, photophobia, blurred vision, and a ring-shaped corneal infiltrate. Prompt ophthalmic
assessment and prolonged antiamoebic treatment are required.

\medterm{Acanthocytes (Spur Cells)} Abnormal red blood cells with irregular, spiky, thorn-like membrane projections of
varying length and width. Caused by alterations in membrane lipid composition (increased
cholesterol-to-phospholipid ratio).

\synonyms
Spur Cells

\medterm{Acanthosis Nigricans} Hyperpigmented, velvety, thickened plaques typically in skin flexures (neck, axillae,
groin) and often associated with insulin resistance, obesity, type 2 diabetes mellitus,
and metabolic syndrome.

\medterm{Acapnia} An abnormally low partial pressure of carbon dioxide in arterial blood, usually caused
by excessive alveolar ventilation. It may accompany respiratory alkalosis,
light-headedness, or paresthesias.

\medterm{Acardia} Absence or severe malformation of the fetal heart, usually occurring in the setting of a
rare twin perfusion sequence. The affected fetus cannot sustain independent circulation.

\medterm{Acariasis} Infestation or disease caused by mites or ticks. Clinical manifestations vary with the
organism and may include dermatitis, pruritus, tissue invasion, or transmission of an
associated pathogen.

\medterm{Access Needle (Interventional)} A needle used to enter a vessel, cavity, or tissue under clinical or image guidance for
sampling, drainage, infusion, or placement of another device.

\synonyms
Interventional

\medterm{ACE Inhibitor} A drug that inhibits angiotensin-converting enzyme, reducing formation of angiotensin II
and degradation of bradykinin.

\medterm{Acetabular Labrum} A fibrocartilaginous rim attached to the margin of the acetabulum that deepens the hip
socket, helps maintain a fluid seal, and contributes to hip-joint stability.

\medterm{Acetabular Notch} The indentation in the inferior margin of the acetabulum, bridged by the transverse
ligament of the acetabulum and forming a passage for structures entering the hip joint.

\medterm{Acetabuloplasty} A surgical procedure that reshapes or augments the acetabular rim or socket to improve
coverage of the femoral head in selected structural hip disorders.

\medterm{Acetabulum} The cup-shaped socket of the hip formed by the fused ilium, ischium, and pubis. It articulates with the femoral head
and is deepened by the acetabular labrum.

\medterm{Acetaminophen (Paracetamol) Toxicity} Liver injury caused by an excessive dose of acetaminophen. Its toxic metabolite depletes
glutathione and causes hepatic necrosis. N-acetylcysteine is the antidote and should be given promptly when toxicity
is suspected.

\synonyms
Paracetamol

\medterm{Acetyl-CoA Carboxylase} An enzyme that converts acetyl-CoA to malonyl-CoA, the first committed step in fatty-acid synthesis. Its activity is
regulated by cellular energy status and hormones.

\medterm{Acetyl-Coenzyme A} A central metabolic intermediate consisting of an acetyl group linked to coenzyme A by a
high-energy thioester bond. It enters the citric acid cycle and provides carbon for
fatty-acid, cholesterol, and ketone-body synthesis.

\medterm{Acetylcholine} A major neurotransmitter in both the central and peripheral nervous systems. In the
peripheral nervous system, it is released at the neuromuscular junction (nicotinic
receptors) to excite skeletal muscle, at all autonomic ganglia (nicotinic receptors),
and at parasympathetic postganglionic terminals (muscarinic receptors).

\medterm{Achalasia} A disorder in which the lower esophageal sphincter fails to relax normally and esophageal peristalsis is
impaired, causing difficulty swallowing and regurgitation.

\medterm{Achenbach's syndrome} A benign condition of recurrent, painful, spontaneous hematoma formation in the pulp of
a finger or thumb, typically affecting middle-aged women. It presents with sudden onset
of throbbing pain and blue-black discoloration of the fingertip, resolving spontaneously
over one to two weeks without treatment.

\medterm{Achilles Tendinopathy} Pain and structural disorder of the Achilles tendon, usually caused by repeated loading. It causes posterior
heel pain and stiffness and is managed with activity modification, rehabilitation, and treatment of contributing factors.

\medterm{Achilles Tendon (Calcaneal Tendon)} The thickest and strongest tendon in the human body, connecting the gastrocnemius and
soleus (triceps surae) muscles to the calcaneus (heel bone). It plays a crucial role in
plantarflexion of the foot, enabling walking, running, and jumping.

\synonyms
Calcaneal Tendon

\medterm{Achilles Tendon Rupture} A partial or complete tear of the Achilles tendon, usually caused by sudden force. It causes posterior ankle
pain, weakness in plantar flexion, and difficulty walking or standing on tiptoe.

\medterm{Achilles Tendonitis} A commonly used term for pain and inflammation or degeneration of the Achilles tendon. It may affect the tendon
at its heel insertion or its mid-portion and is often related to repetitive loading.

\medterm{Achlorhydria} Absence of hydrochloric acid in gastric secretions. It may result from autoimmune gastritis, long-term acid suppression,
or loss of acid-producing stomach cells.

\medterm{Achondroplasia} Autosomal dominant skeletal dysplasia (FGFR3 gene). Most common form of dwarfism.
Features: short limbs, macrocephaly, frontal bossing, trident hand, spinal stenosis.
Homozygous form lethal. Normal intelligence.

\medterm{Acid} A substance that donates hydrogen ions or accepts an electron pair. In water, an acid increases hydrogen-ion concentration and
lowers pH.

\medterm{Acid Base Balance} The physiological regulation of hydrogen ion concentration in body fluids, maintaining
arterial pH between 7.35 and 7.45.

\medterm{Acid Reflux} Backward flow of stomach contents into the esophagus, which may cause heartburn, regurgitation, or irritation.

\medterm{Acid-Base Analysis} Evaluation of blood pH, bicarbonate, and carbon dioxide to identify metabolic or respiratory disturbances,
including those caused by poisoning.

\medterm{Acid-Base Disorder} Disturbance of the body's acid-base balance, classified by primary disturbance and
compensation. Respiratory acidosis: hypoventilation, high PaCO2, low pH. Respiratory
alkalosis: hyperventilation, low PaCO2, high pH.

\medterm{Acid-Fast Bacillus Smear} Microscopic examination of a specimen, often sputum, for acid-fast organisms using a
special stain. A positive result supports mycobacterial infection but does not by itself
identify the species or establish drug susceptibility; culture or molecular testing is
also required.

\medterm{Acidosis} An acid-base disturbance in which the concentration of hydrogen ions increases, tending
to lower arterial blood pH below 7.35.

\medterm{Acinetobacter baumannii resistance} Acinetobacter baumannii is a gram-negative coccobacillus that has emerged as a
significant cause of healthcare-associated infections, particularly in intensive care
units. The organism has intrinsic and acquired resistance mechanisms, including
beta-lactamases, efflux pumps, and porin mutations.

\medterm{ACL Injuries} Sprains or tears of the anterior cruciate ligament of the knee, usually caused by twisting, sudden deceleration, or
contact trauma. They may cause instability, swelling, and difficulty pivoting; examination and imaging guide management.

\medterm{ACL Reconstruction} Surgical reconstruction of the anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) following rupture, one
of the most common knee ligament injuries. The ACL is a primary restraint to anterior
tibial translation and rotatory instability, and its rupture frequently occurs during
non-contact pivoting sports activities ( skiing, soccer, basketball).

\medterm{Acne} A chronic inflammatory disorder of hair follicles and sebaceous glands. It causes comedones, papules, pustules, or nodules,
most often on the face, chest, and back.

\medterm{Acne Rosacea Subtypes} Rosacea may present predominantly with facial flushing and persistent redness, papules and pustules, skin
thickening, or eye involvement. A person may have more than one pattern.

\medterm{Acne Vulgaris} The common form of acne caused by follicular plugging, increased sebum production, microbial activity, and inflammation.
It usually affects the face, trunk, and upper arms.

\medterm{Acoustic Nerve} The acoustic nerve, more properly called the auditory or vestibulocochlear nerve
(cranial nerve VIII), carries hearing and balance information from the inner ear to the
brainstem.

\medterm{Acoustic Neuroma} A benign, slow-growing tumor of the vestibular portion of the vestibulocochlear nerve. It may cause hearing loss,
tinnitus, or balance problems.

\medterm{Acoustic Neuroma (Vestibular Schwannoma)} A usually benign, slow-growing tumor of Schwann cells involving the vestibular portion
of cranial nerve VIII. It may cause unilateral sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus,
imbalance, or facial sensory symptoms; MRI of the internal auditory canals is the
preferred diagnostic study.

\synonyms
Vestibular Schwannoma

\medterm{Acoustic Trauma} Acute damage to the auditory system from a brief, intense sound such as an explosion or
firearm. It may cause tinnitus, temporary or permanent sensorineural hearing loss, and
tympanic or ossicular injury; urgent audiologic assessment is indicated when hearing
loss is sudden.

\medterm{Acoustic Reflex} An involuntary contraction of the stapedius muscle in response to a loud sound. It can
be measured with immittance testing and helps assess the middle-ear muscles, auditory
pathways, and facial nerve.

\synonyms
Stapedial reflex; stapedius reflex; middle-ear muscle reflex.

\medterm{Acquisition Time} The time during which a medical imaging system collects data. The required time depends on the imaging method,
body region, protocol, and patient movement.

\medterm{Acral lentiginous melanoma} A subtype of melanoma arising on acral skin (palms, soles, or under nails), appearing as
a dark, irregularly pigmented patch or plaque. It is more common in people with darker
skin tones and often presents at a later stage due to less frequent surveillance of
these sites. Prognosis depends on thickness and stage at diagnosis.

\medterm{Acrodermatitis Enteropathica} A zinc-deficiency disorder causing periorificial and acral dermatitis, alopecia,
diarrhoea, poor wound healing, and increased susceptibility to infection. It may be
inherited from impaired intestinal zinc absorption or acquired through malnutrition,
malabsorption, or prolonged parenteral nutrition.

\medterm{Acromegaly} Chronic growth hormone (GH) excess in adulthood, typically from a pituitary somatotroph
adenoma.

\medterm{Acromegaly Complications} Long-term growth-hormone excess may cause diabetes, hypertension, heart disease, sleep apnea, joint disease,
nerve compression, visual problems, and other organ complications.

\medterm{Acromioclavicular Joint} A plane synovial joint between the acromion of the scapula and the lateral end of the
clavicle. It permits gliding movements and is stabilized primarily by the
acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments.

\medterm{Acromioclavicular Joint Injuries} Sprains or dislocations of the joint between the acromion and clavicle, usually caused by a fall or direct
shoulder impact. Severity ranges from ligament sprain to complete displacement and is assessed clinically and with imaging.

\medterm{Acromion} Bony process forming the shoulder's highest point, extending laterally from the scapular
spine. Articulates with the clavicle at the acromioclavicular joint and arches over the
glenohumeral joint. Attachment for deltoid and trapezius. The subacromial space contains
the supraspinatus tendon and subacromial bursa.

\medterm{ACTH Stimulation Test} A dynamic test of adrenal cortisol reserve using synthetic adrenocorticotropic hormone,
usually cosyntropin. An inadequate cortisol rise supports adrenal insufficiency,
although interpretation depends on timing, assay, recent glucocorticoid exposure, and
whether disease is acute or chronic.

\medterm{Actinic granuloma} A benign granulomatous skin condition arising on sun-damaged skin, presenting as annular
or ring-shaped plaques with central atrophy and an elevated, erythematous border. It is
thought to represent a foreign-body reaction to degenerated elastic tissue from chronic
ultraviolet exposure.

\medterm{Actinic Keratosis} A rough, scaly skin lesion caused by chronic ultraviolet exposure, usually on sun-exposed skin. It can progress to
squamous-cell carcinoma, so persistent, thickened, or changing lesions require clinical assessment.

\medterm{Actinic Keratosis Management} Actinic keratosis management requires field-directed and lesion-directed therapy.
Lesion-directed: cryotherapy (liquid nitrogen, 5 to 10 second freeze-thaw cycle),
curettage, electrodessication.

\medterm{Actinic Purpura} Bruising caused by fragile, sun-damaged skin and connective tissue, usually on the backs of the hands and
forearms in older adults. The purple patches may appear after minor trauma and are generally harmless, although
medicines such as anticoagulants can worsen them. New, widespread, or unexplained bruising warrants assessment.

\medterm{Actinomycetoma} A chronic, progressive granulomatous infection of the skin and subcutaneous tissue
caused by aerobic actinomycetes, characterized by swelling, draining sinuses, and dark
granules in the discharge. It primarily affects the feet and is endemic in tropical
regions. Diagnosis is by culture and histopathology.

\medterm{Actinomycosis} A chronic infection caused by branching, gram-positive anaerobic bacteria of the genus Actinomyces,
which normally colonize the mouth, gastrointestinal tract, and genital tract. It can form
infiltrative masses and draining sinuses containing sulfur granules.

\medterm{Action Potential} A rapid, all-or-nothing depolarization of the cell membrane that propagates along
excitable membranes (neurons, muscle, some endocrine cells).

\medterm{Activated Charcoal} A porous form of carbon that adsorbs some substances in the gastrointestinal tract and can reduce
their absorption after selected poisonings. It is not effective for every toxin and can
cause aspiration or other complications.

\medterm{Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time} A blood test that measures clotting through the intrinsic and common coagulation pathways.
It may be prolonged by unfractionated heparin, clotting-factor deficiencies, inhibitors,
or some liver and systemic disorders.

\medterm{Active Caries} Tooth decay that is continuing to progress. The affected surface is often soft, rough, or chalky and requires dental
assessment and management.

\medterm{Active Management of Third Stage} A set of measures used after birth to reduce postpartum hemorrhage, usually including a uterotonic
medicine, controlled cord traction when appropriate, and assessment of uterine tone and bleeding.

\medterm{Active Phase of Labor} The part of the first stage of labor in which the cervix dilates progressively, generally beginning at about 6 cm
and continuing to full dilation. Progress varies between individuals.

\medterm{Active Site} The specific three-dimensional region of an enzyme where substrate binding and catalysis
occur. Formed by amino acid residues that may be distant in primary sequence but brought
together by protein folding.

\medterm{Active Surveillance} Close, structured monitoring of a cancer or precancerous condition without immediate
treatment. It includes scheduled examinations, laboratory tests, imaging, or biopsies,
with treatment started if evidence of progression develops.

\medterm{Active Transport} Movement of substances across cell membranes against their concentration gradient,
requiring energy input, typically from ATP hydrolysis.

\medterm{Activities of Daily Living} Routine tasks required for personal care and independent living, including basic activities and
more complex instrumental activities. Performance is assessed to describe functional
status and support care planning.

\medterm{Acute} Beginning suddenly or developing over a short period. The term may describe symptoms, illnesses, or complications.

\medterm{Acute Agitation} A state of heightened motor activity, emotional tension, irritability, and impaired
behavioral control that may threaten the patient or others. Management begins with
de-escalation and assessment for delirium, intoxication, withdrawal, medical illness,
and psychosis.

\medterm{Acute Alcohol Intoxication} Neurobehavioral and physiologic impairment caused by recent ethanol exposure. Severity
varies with concentration, tolerance, co-ingestants, and comorbidity; dangerous effects
include respiratory depression, aspiration, hypoglycemia, hypothermia, trauma, and coma.

\medterm{Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma} An ophthalmic emergency in which sudden closure of the anterior-chamber angle causes a rapid
rise in intraocular pressure. Severe eye pain, blurred vision, halos, headache, nausea,
vomiting, and a red eye may occur; urgent ophthalmic treatment is required to protect
vision.

\medterm{Acute Aortic Syndrome} A spectrum of life-threatening conditions involving the thoracic aorta, including aortic
dissection, intramural hematoma (IMH), and penetrating aortic ulcer (PAU). See acute
aortic syndromes.

\medterm{Acute Bacterial Rhinosinusitis} Bacterial infection of the paranasal sinuses suspected when symptoms persist without
improvement for at least 10 days, become substantially worse after initial improvement,
or are severe with high fever and purulent discharge. Treatment decisions depend on
severity, complications, resistance risk, and local guidance.

\medterm{Acute blood loss} Rapid loss of circulating blood volume that can cause hypovolemic shock, tissue hypoxia,
tachycardia, hypotension, and acute anemia.

\medterm{Acute Bronchitis} Acute inflammation of the tracheobronchial tree, usually caused by a viral respiratory
infection and characterized by cough, with or without sputum production, lasting up to
several weeks.

\medterm{Acute Caries} Tooth decay that progresses rapidly, often producing a soft lesion or cavity. Risk is increased by factors such as frequent
sugar exposure, reduced saliva, or poor oral hygiene.

\medterm{Acute Cholecystitis} Acute inflammation of the gallbladder, most often caused by persistent cystic-duct
obstruction by a gallstone. It commonly causes sustained right-upper-quadrant pain,
fever, nausea, and a positive Murphy sign; ultrasound and timely antimicrobial and
surgical assessment are standard.

\medterm{Acute Acalculous Cholecystitis} Acute gallbladder inflammation without gallstones, usually occurring in critically ill, injured, postoperative, or fasting patients. Ischaemia, bile stasis, and infection may contribute; it can progress rapidly to necrosis or perforation and requires urgent assessment.

\medterm{Acute Necrotizing Ulcerative Gingivitis (ANUG)} A painful, rapidly developing infection of the gums characterized by punched-out gingival papillae, ulceration, spontaneous bleeding, gray necrotic tissue, and foul breath. It is associated with anaerobic oral bacteria and risk factors such as poor oral hygiene, smoking, stress, malnutrition, or immunosuppression; urgent dental evaluation, cleaning, and treatment are needed.

\medterm{Acute Coronary Syndrome} A clinical spectrum caused by acute myocardial ischemia from plaque disruption,
thrombosis, or less commonly coronary spasm. It includes unstable angina, NSTEMI, and
STEMI and requires rapid assessment with symptoms, serial ECGs, and cardiac troponin
testing.

\medterm{Acute Cough} A cough lasting less than three weeks. Common causes include viral respiratory infection, acute bronchitis, and pneumonia.

\medterm{Acute dacryocystitis} Acute infection and inflammation of the lacrimal sac, most commonly caused by
obstruction of the nasolacrimal duct leading to stasis and secondary bacterial
infection. It presents with painful swelling, redness, and tenderness at the medial
canthus with purulent discharge.

\medterm{Acute Diarrhea} Passage of loose or watery stools, usually three or more times daily, lasting less than two weeks. Infection, food-related
illness, medicines, and other intestinal disorders can cause it. Oral rehydration is important; blood in stool,
severe dehydration, persistent vomiting, or severe pain requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Acute Disseminated Encephalomyelitis} An acute, immune-mediated demyelinating disorder, usually occurring days to weeks after
an infection and less often after vaccination. It commonly causes encephalopathy with
multifocal neurologic deficits, ataxia, or seizures, particularly in children.

\medterm{Acute esophageal necrosis} Ischemic necrosis of the esophageal mucosa, most commonly caused by low-flow states such
as shock, sepsis, or thromboembolism, presenting with hematemesis and chest pain.
Endoscopy shows circumferential black necrotic mucosa, typically in the distal
esophagus.

\medterm{Acute Exacerbation of Asthma} A progressive increase in dyspnea, cough, wheezing, or chest tightness with reduced
expiratory airflow compared with the patient’s baseline. Severe attacks may cause
exhaustion, silent chest, hypercapnia, respiratory failure, and need for urgent
treatment.

\medterm{Acute Fatty Liver of Pregnancy} A rare, potentially life-threatening complication of late pregnancy involving liver dysfunction and
microvesicular fat accumulation. It may cause nausea, abdominal pain, jaundice, low glucose, coagulopathy, or organ failure.

\medterm{Acute Flaccid Myelitis} A rare but serious inflammatory disorder of the spinal cord, usually affecting the gray
matter and causing sudden flaccid limb weakness, reduced reflexes, and sometimes
cranial-nerve or respiratory involvement. MRI and cerebrospinal-fluid studies support
the diagnosis; urgent neurologic and respiratory monitoring is required.

\medterm{Acute Generalized Exanthematous Pustulosis} An acute drug reaction characterised by dozens to thousands of small, sterile,
nonfollicular pustules arising on an erythematous base, often with fever and
neutrophilia. It usually begins rapidly after exposure to an antibiotic or other
medication and resolves after withdrawal of the trigger.

\medterm{Acute glomerulonephritis} Acute inflammation of the renal glomeruli causing hematuria, proteinuria, reduced
glomerular filtration, edema, and often hypertension.

\medterm{Acute Hepatitis} Acute inflammation and injury of hepatocytes, usually developing over days to months and
characterized by elevated aminotransferases, with or without jaundice, nausea,
right-upper-quadrant discomfort, or constitutional symptoms.

\medterm{Acute HIV Syndrome} The acute retroviral syndrome occurring 2-6 weeks after primary HIV infection,
corresponding to high-level viremia and immune activation. Presents with fever (most
common), lymphadenopathy, pharyngitis, rash (maculopapular, trunk/face), myalgia,
arthralgia, headache, nausea, diarrhea, and oral ulcers.

\medterm{Acute Hypercapnic Respiratory Failure} Inability to eliminate carbon dioxide, producing elevated arterial PaCO2 and often
respiratory acidemia. Causes include COPD exacerbation, severe asthma, neuromuscular
weakness, central respiratory depression, and chest-wall restriction.

\medterm{Acute Hypoxemic Respiratory Failure} Inability of the respiratory system to maintain adequate arterial oxygenation, usually
manifested by low arterial oxygen or oxygen saturation despite appropriate inspired
oxygen. Causes include pneumonia, pulmonary edema, ARDS, atelectasis, and pulmonary
embolism.

\medterm{Acute Inflammation} A rapid, predominantly non-specific immune response to tissue injury or infection that
typically lasts hours to days and involves vascular and cellular reactions. The cardinal
signs are redness, heat, swelling, pain, and loss of function, resulting from increased
blood flow, vascular permeability, and cellular infiltration.

\medterm{Acute Interstitial Nephritis} Inflammatory infiltrate in the renal interstitium and tubules most commonly caused by
drug reactions including NSAIDs, antibiotics particularly penicillins and
cephalosporins, proton pump inhibitors, and diuretics. Also caused by infections and
autoimmune diseases.

\medterm{Acute Ischemic Stroke} Sudden neurologic dysfunction caused by focal cerebral, spinal, or retinal ischemia.
Emergency evaluation distinguishes hemorrhage from ischemia and determines eligibility
for reperfusion therapy while supporting airway, glucose, temperature, and blood
pressure management.

\medterm{Acute Kidney Injury} A sudden decline in kidney function over hours or days, causing reduced filtration and possible fluid, electrolyte,
or acid-base disturbances. Causes include reduced blood flow, direct kidney injury, and urinary obstruction.

\medterm{Acute Kidney Injury Staging} A standardized classification based on changes in serum creatinine and urine output.
KDIGO stages 1 through 3 quantify the severity of abrupt kidney-function decline and
support prognosis, monitoring, and treatment decisions.

\medterm{Acute Leukemia} A leukemia characterized by a rapid onset, clonal proliferation of immature blast cells
(>20\% of bone marrow cells), and rapid progression without treatment. Subtypes: acute
lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL, B-cell or T-cell, most common childhood cancer, peak age
2-5) and acute myeloid leukemia (AML, more common in adults, median age 65-70).

\medterm{Acute Limb Ischemia} Sudden reduction in arterial blood flow to a limb that threatens tissue viability,
usually caused by embolism, thrombosis, or graft occlusion. The six classic findings are
pain, pallor, pulselessness, paresthesia, paralysis, and poikilothermia; urgent vascular
assessment and revascularization are required.

\medterm{Acute Limb Ischemia Six Ps} The classic findings of acute arterial perfusion loss: pain, pallor, pulselessness,
paresthesia, paralysis, and poikilothermia. Motor or sensory deficit indicates
threatened limb and requires urgent vascular assessment and revascularization planning.

\medterm{Acute Liver Failure} Rapid deterioration of liver function in a previously healthy individual without prior
liver disease. Causes include acetaminophen toxicity, viral hepatitis, drug reactions,
autoimmune hepatitis, and Wilson disease. Features include coagulopathy, encephalopathy,
and multiorgan failure. Presents with jaundice, confusion, and bleeding.

\medterm{Acute lymphangitis} Acute inflammation of lymphatic vessels, typically resulting from streptococcal or
staphylococcal infection spreading from a distal wound. It presents with red, tender
streaks extending proximally from the site of infection, along with fever, malaise, and
regional lymphadenopathy. Treatment requires systemic antibiotics.

\medterm{Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia} A clonal malignancy of lymphoid progenitor cells, most common in children (peak age 2-5
years) but also occurring in adults with a worse prognosis with increasing age.

\medterm{Acute lymphocytic leukemia} A malignant proliferation of immature lymphoid precursors in the bone marrow and blood,
commonly presenting with cytopenias, fever, bone pain, or lymphadenopathy.

\medterm{Acute Megakaryoblastic Leukemia} A rare subtype of acute myeloid leukemia characterized by the proliferation of
megakaryoblasts in the bone marrow and peripheral blood.

\medterm{Acute mesenteric ischemia} Occlusion or reduced flow in the mesenteric arteries causing bowel ischemia, most
commonly from embolism or atherosclerosis. Presents with severe abdominal pain
disproportionate to physical findings. Delayed treatment leads to bowel necrosis,
perforation, and high mortality.

\medterm{Acute Mountain Sickness} A mild altitude illness caused by rapid ascent, usually producing headache with nausea,
fatigue, dizziness, or sleep disturbance. Prevention and treatment include gradual
acclimatization, descent when symptoms worsen, oxygen, and appropriate medical therapy.

\medterm{Acute Myeloid Leukemia} A clonal hematologic malignancy characterized by proliferation of immature myeloid
blasts that suppress normal hematopoiesis. Diagnosis requires the appropriate blast
burden or defining genetic abnormality, and presentation commonly includes anemia,
infection, and bleeding from marrow failure.

\medterm{Acute obstructive uropathy} Sudden impairment of urinary flow caused by obstruction of the urinary tract,
potentially producing hydronephrosis, pain, infection, and acute kidney injury.

\medterm{Acute Otitis Media} Infection of the middle ear space with acute onset of symptoms and signs of middle ear
inflammation. Diagnosis requires middle ear effusion plus acute symptoms (ear pain,
fever, irritability) or signs (bulging tympanic membrane, erythema). Most common
pathogen: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

\medterm{Acute Pancreatitis} Sudden inflammation of the pancreas, most often caused by gallstones or alcohol. It causes upper-abdominal pain
and may cause vomiting or systemic complications; severity ranges from mild to life-threatening.

\medterm{Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia} A distinct subtype of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) characterized by the
t(15;17)(q24;q21) translocation, which fuses the promyelocytic leukemia (PML) gene on
chromosome 15 with the retinoic acid receptor alpha (RARA) gene on chromosome 17.

\medterm{Acute Rehabilitation} Multidisciplinary rehabilitation provided during or soon after hospitalization for conditions such as stroke,
spinal-cord injury, traumatic brain injury, or major trauma. Treatment is tailored to functional needs.

\medterm{Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome} A severe inflammatory lung injury causing acute hypoxemic respiratory failure and diffuse lung
opacities not fully explained by heart failure. Common triggers include sepsis, pneumonia, aspiration, trauma, and pancreatitis.

\medterm{Acute Sinusitis} Acute inflammation of the mucosa of one or more paranasal sinuses, usually following a
viral upper-respiratory infection. It causes nasal obstruction, purulent or discoloured
discharge, facial pressure or pain, and reduced smell; persistent, severe, or worsening
symptoms may indicate acute bacterial rhinosinusitis or a complication.

\medterm{Acute Stress Disorder} A trauma-related mental-health condition beginning after exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury, or
sexual violence and lasting from three days to one month. Symptoms may include intrusive memories, avoidance,
negative mood, dissociation, and increased arousal; professional assessment and support are recommended.

\medterm{Acute Tubular Necrosis} Most common cause of intrinsic AKI resulting from ischemic or nephrotoxic injury to
tubular epithelial cells. Ischemic ATN develops from prolonged prerenal states.
Nephrotoxic ATN results from direct injury by aminoglycosides, amphotericin B,
cisplatin, contrast, and myoglobin.

\medterm{Acute viral illness} A short-duration illness caused by a virus. Symptoms depend on the affected organ system and may
include fever, fatigue, cough, vomiting, diarrhea, or rash.

\medterm{Acute-on-Chronic Liver Failure} Acute deterioration of pre-existing chronic liver disease associated with organ failure
and high short-term mortality. It is commonly precipitated by infection, alcohol-related
hepatitis, bleeding, or other acute insults and requires urgent multidisciplinary
management.

\medterm{Adam's Apple} The common name for the laryngeal prominence, the visible projection at the front of the
neck formed by the angle where the two laminae of the thyroid cartilage meet.

\medterm{Adaptive Equipment} Devices that help individuals with disabilities perform daily activities independently.
Examples: reachers, dressing sticks, button hooks, ergonomic utensils, grab bars, shower
chairs, wheelchairs, walkers, canes. Selection based on individual needs and goals.
Occupational therapy assessment recommended.

\medterm{Adaptive Immunity} Antigen-specific immunity mediated principally by B and T lymphocytes. It develops after
antigen exposure, generates immunologic memory, and includes humoral responses from
B-cell antibodies and cellular responses from T cells.

\medterm{Addiction} A chronic, relapsing brain disorder characterized by compulsive substance use or
behavior despite harmful consequences. It involves changes in brain reward, motivation,
and memory circuits, leading to impaired control over the behavior. Treatment often
combines medication, behavioral therapy, and social support.

\medterm{Addison Disease} Primary adrenal insufficiency resulting from destruction or dysfunction of the adrenal
cortex, leading to deficiency of cortisol, aldosterone, and adrenal androgens.

\medterm{Addisonian Crisis} Life-threatening emergency resulting from acute cortisol deficiency in patients with
adrenal insufficiency. Precipitated by infection, trauma, surgery, or abrupt
discontinuation of chronic glucocorticoid therapy.

\medterm{Adduction} Movement of a body part toward the body's midline.

\medterm{Adductor Muscles} A group of muscles on the medial thigh that adduct (pull toward the midline) the hip:
adductor longus, adductor brevis, adductor magnus, gracilis, and pectineus. Innervated
by the obturator nerve (L2-L4); adductor magnus also receives tibial nerve (sciatic).
Adductor longus is a common site of athletic injury ('groin strain').

\medterm{Adenocarcinoma} A malignant tumor arising from glandular epithelial tissue, forming gland-like
structures. The most common type of carcinoma, occurring in organs such as the colon,
breast, lung, pancreas, and prostate.

\medterm{Adenocarcinoma of the colon} A malignant gland-forming epithelial tumor of the colon, commonly presenting with
altered bowel habits, occult bleeding, anemia, or an obstructing mass.

\medterm{Adenoid Cystic Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor of glandular tissue, most commonly of the salivary glands,
characterized by slow growth, perineural spread, and late recurrence.

\medterm{Adenoidectomy} The surgical removal of the pharyngeal tonsils (adenoids), which are lymphoid tissue
structures located in the nasopharynx, and it is commonly performed in children for
obstructive sleep apnea, chronic nasal obstruction, recurrent otitis media, and chronic
rhinosinusitis that is refractory to medical management.

\medterm{Adenoids} Lymphoid tissue in the posterior wall and roof of the nasopharynx, part of Waldeyer ring
of pharyngeal lymphoid tissue. Enlarged adenoids (adenoidal hypertrophy) in children
cause nasal obstruction, mouth breathing, snoring, obstructive sleep apnea, serous
otitis media (eustachian tube obstruction), and facial changes ('adenoid facies').

\medterm{Adenoma} A benign tumour arising from glandular or gland-like epithelial tissue. Some adenomas,
particularly certain colorectal adenomas, can develop dysplasia and progress to
carcinoma.

\medterm{Adenoma-Carcinoma Sequence} Established model of stepwise colorectal carcinogenesis progressing from normal
epithelium through adenomas to carcinoma over ten to fifteen years.

\medterm{Adenomatous Polyp} A benign gland-forming polyp, most commonly in the colon, that may contain dysplasia and
can progress through the adenoma--carcinoma sequence. Tubular, villous, and
tubulovillous types are recognized.

\medterm{Adenomyosis} A condition in which endometrial glands and stroma are present within the myometrium
(uterine muscle). Most common in multiparous women in their 40s and 50s. Presents with
heavy menstrual bleeding (menorrhagia), severe dysmenorrhea, chronic pelvic pain, and
uterine enlargement.

\medterm{Adenovirus} A non-enveloped DNA virus that causes respiratory infections, conjunctivitis,
gastroenteritis, and cystitis. Adenoviruses are highly stable in the environment and can
cause outbreaks in closed populations. The virus has over 50 serotypes with different
tissue tropisms. Diagnosis is made by PCR, antigen detection, or viral culture.

\medterm{Adenovirus Infection} Infection with an adenovirus, commonly causing sore throat, cough, fever,
conjunctivitis, diarrhea, or vomiting. Most infections are self-limited, but severe respiratory or
disseminated disease can occur in infants and people with weakened immunity. Treatment is usually
supportive, with medical assessment for breathing difficulty, dehydration, or severe illness.

\medterm{Adenovirus Vaccine} A vaccine used primarily to prevent adenovirus types 4 and 7 among selected
military personnel. It is an oral, live vaccine and is not part of routine vaccination for the general
public. Vaccine use and eligibility depend on current public-health recommendations.

\medterm{ADHD} Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder is a neurodevelopmental disorder involving persistent inattention and/or
hyperactivity and impulsivity that impair functioning. Symptoms begin in childhood,
occur in more than one setting, and are classified as predominantly inattentive,
predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, or combined presentation.

\medterm{Adherence} The extent to which a person follows an agreed treatment plan, including prescribed medicines and other care.
Adherence may be affected by cost, adverse effects, treatment complexity, health beliefs,
or difficulty accessing care.

\medterm{Adherence (Geriatric)} Adherence to an agreed treatment plan in an older adult, including medicine use, appointments, and
recommended self-care. Cognitive impairment, polypharmacy, adverse effects, cost, and
support needs may affect adherence.

\synonyms
Geriatric

\medterm{Adhesive Capsulitis} A condition causing progressive shoulder pain and stiffness due to inflammation and fibrosis of
the glenohumeral joint capsule. See Frozen Shoulder.

\medterm{Adhesive Tape} A material used to secure dressings, devices, or tubing to the skin. It is available in paper, cloth, plastic, and other forms.

\medterm{Adie Tonic Pupil} Uncommon cause of anisocoria, a benign parasympathetic denervation of the iris sphincter
and ciliary muscle, typically unilateral in young women, idiopathic or associated with
diabetes, herpes zoster, or autoimmune neuropathy.

\medterm{Adipocere (Grave Wax)} Waxy, grayish-white substance formed postmortem in warm, moist, anaerobic environments
(water, damp soil) by the hydrolysis and hydrogenation of body fat into free fatty
acids; retards putrefaction and preserves anatomical details for months to years.

\synonyms
Grave Wax

\medterm{Adiponectin} A hormone produced mainly by adipose tissue that influences fatty-acid metabolism, insulin sensitivity, and inflammatory responses.

\medterm{Adipose Tissue} A specialized connective tissue composed mainly of adipocytes that store energy as
triglycerides. It provides thermal insulation, cushioning for organs, and endocrine
regulation through hormones and signaling molecules such as leptin and adiponectin.

\medterm{Adjustment Disorder} Emotional or behavioral symptoms in response to an identifiable stressor occurring
within three months of the onset of the stressor. The symptoms are disproportionate to
