the severity of the stressor and cause significant distress or impairment. It resolves
within six months of the termination of the stressor.

\medterm{Adjustment Disorder with Anxiety} An emotional or behavioral response to an identifiable stressor that causes distress or
impairment but does not meet criteria for another mental disorder.

\medterm{Adjuvant Therapy} Additional treatment after primary treatment to reduce recurrence risk. Examples:
chemotherapy after surgery.

\medterm{ADL Training} Training for activities of daily living: dressing, bathing, grooming, feeding,
toileting. Adaptive techniques, equipment.

\medterm{Administered Activity} The measured amount of radioactive material given to a patient for imaging or therapy,
usually expressed in becquerels. It should be prescribed and recorded with the
radionuclide, radiopharmaceutical, route, date, and time of administration.

\medterm{Adolescent Health} The health and wellbeing of young people (10-19 years), addressing the physical,
psychological, and social transitions of puberty and adolescence.

\medterm{Adolescent Confidentiality and Consent} The principles governing private healthcare discussions with adolescents and their ability to participate in medical decisions. The exact rules for confidentiality, consent, and parental involvement depend on age, capacity, local law, and safeguarding concerns.

\medterm{Adoptive Cell Therapy} A personalized cellular immunotherapy in which a patient's own lymphocytes are expanded
or engineered ex vivo and reinfused to attack the tumor; examples include
tumor-infiltrating lymphocyte (TIL) therapy and chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell
therapy.

\medterm{ADP (Adenosine Diphosphate)} A nucleotide composed of adenine, ribose, and two phosphate groups. Formed by hydrolysis
of ATP ($\mathrm{ATP} \rightarrow \mathrm{ADP} + \mathrm{P_i}$, $\Delta G^\circ = -30.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$). Serves as the immediate precursor for ATP
synthesis via oxidative phosphorylation (mitochondrial ATP synthase), substrate-level
phosphorylation (glycolysis, TCA cycle), and photophosphorylation.

\synonyms
Adenosine Diphosphate

\medterm{Adrenal Cortex Physiology} The adrenal cortex produces steroid hormones in three zones: the zona glomerulosa produces
mineralocorticoids, mainly aldosterone; the zona fasciculata produces glucocorticoids,
mainly cortisol; and the zona reticularis produces adrenal androgens. Secretion is
regulated by the renin-angiotensin system, plasma potassium, and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis.

\medterm{Adrenal Cortical Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor arising from the adrenal cortex. It may be functional,
producing excess adrenal hormones, or nonfunctional.

\medterm{Adrenal Crisis} An acute, life-threatening manifestation of severe adrenal insufficiency. Causes:
primary (Addison disease), secondary (pituitary failure), or tertiary (exogenous
corticosteroid withdrawal). Precipitants: systemic infection, trauma, surgery, or abrupt
steroid cessation.

\medterm{Adrenal Gland} Paired endocrine glands atop each kidney (suprarenal), composed of cortex and medulla.
Cortex (three zones): zona glomerulosa (aldosterone, mineralocorticoids), zona
fasciculata (cortisol, glucocorticoids), zona reticularis (androgens). Medulla:
chromaffin cells secrete epinephrine and norepinephrine (catecholamines).

\medterm{Adrenal Hemorrhage} Bleeding into the adrenal gland most commonly occurring in critically ill patients with
sepsis, coagulopathy, or anticoagulation therapy. Bilateral adrenal hemorrhage may cause
acute adrenal insufficiency with circulatory collapse. Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome:
adrenal hemorrhage associated with meningococcal sepsis.

\medterm{Adrenal Hyperplasia} Enlargement or overgrowth of adrenal tissue that may increase production of adrenal
hormones. Causes include congenital adrenal hyperplasia, chronic stimulation by ACTH, and some
adrenal tumors. Symptoms depend on the hormone affected and may include abnormal genital development,
early puberty, excess body hair, high blood pressure, or electrolyte disturbances.

\medterm{Adrenal Insufficiency} Deficient production of cortisol by the adrenal cortex classified as primary adrenal
insufficiency or Addison disease from adrenal gland destruction, secondary from
pituitary ACTH deficiency, and tertiary from chronic exogenous glucocorticoid
suppression of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis.

\medterm{Adrenal Medulla Physiology} The adrenal medulla produces catecholamines primarily epinephrine and norepinephrine in
response to sympathetic nervous system activation. Epinephrine accounts for
approximately eighty percent and norepinephrine approximately twenty percent of
catecholamine output.

\medterm{Adrenal Myelolipoma} A usually benign adrenal tumor composed of mature fat and blood-forming tissue. It is often
found incidentally on imaging and causes no symptoms, but larger lesions may produce mass
effect or bleeding. Management depends on size, symptoms, growth, and diagnostic
certainty.

\medterm{Adrenal Tumors} Tumors arising in the adrenal cortex or medulla may be benign or malignant and may or may not produce
hormones. Evaluation uses clinical assessment, hormone testing when indicated, and
imaging to assess size and appearance; treatment depends on the diagnosis, hormone
secretion, symptoms, growth, and risk of malignancy.

\medterm{Adrenal Venous Sampling} Interventional radiology procedure for lateralizing aldosterone secretion in primary
hyperaldosteronism. Catheterization of both adrenal veins allows measurement of
aldosterone and cortisol levels to calculate selectivity index confirming successful
cannulation and lateralization ratio.

\medterm{Adrenalectomy} Surgical removal of one or both adrenal glands, performed for selected adrenal tumors,
severe hormone excess, or other localized disease. Removal of one gland may leave adequate hormone
production; bilateral removal causes permanent adrenal insufficiency and requires lifelong hormone
replacement and emergency-steroid planning.

\medterm{Adrenocortical Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor of the adrenal cortex that may secrete cortisol, androgens, or
aldosterone or be non-functional. Often large at diagnosis; treatment is en bloc
resection, with mitotane for advanced disease.

\medterm{Adrenocorticotropic Hormone} ACTH (corticotropin): a peptide hormone from the anterior pituitary (cleaved from POMC)
that stimulates the adrenal cortex to produce cortisol (zona fasciculata) and adrenal
androgens; regulated by hypothalamic CRH and cortisol negative feedback (circadian).

\medterm{Adult Spinal Deformity} Abnormal curvature or alignment of the spine in an adult, including scoliosis, kyphosis, or loss of
normal sagittal balance. It may cause back pain, nerve compression, difficulty walking,
or functional disability.

\medterm{Adult Still's Disease} A systemic inflammatory disorder characterized by recurrent high fevers, arthritis or joint pain, a transient
salmon-colored rash, sore throat, and possible involvement of other organs.

\medterm{Adult-Onset Still Disease} A systemic inflammatory disorder beginning in adulthood, characterized by high-spiking fevers, arthritis or
joint pain, a transient salmon-pink rash, sore throat, and possible liver, spleen, heart, or blood-clotting complications.

\medterm{Advance Care Planning} A process of discussing and documenting a person's healthcare values and preferences for possible future illness,
including designation of a surrogate decision-maker when appropriate.

\medterm{Advance Directive} Legal document expressing healthcare wishes. Components: living will (treatment
preferences), durable power of attorney for healthcare (surrogate decision-maker).
Should be reviewed periodically, discussed with surrogate and healthcare team.

\medterm{Advanced Cancer} Cancer that has grown beyond its original site. It may be locally advanced when it has
invaded nearby tissues or metastatic when it has spread to distant organs.

\medterm{Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support} A standardized set of advanced resuscitation measures for cardiac arrest and other
life-threatening cardiovascular emergencies. It includes high-quality CPR, defibrillation
when indicated, airway management, medicines, and treatment of reversible causes.

\medterm{Advanced Maternal Age} Maternal age of 35 years or older at the estimated time of delivery. It is associated with increased risks of
some chromosomal conditions and pregnancy complications.

\medterm{Adverse Drug Event} Harm related to the use of a medication, including harm caused by an adverse drug reaction, medication error,
overdose, or inappropriate use.

\medterm{Adverse Drug Reaction} An adverse drug reaction is any noxious or unintended response to a drug that occurs at
doses used for prophylaxis, diagnosis, or therapy.

\medterm{Adverse Event} Any untoward medical occurrence during or after a treatment or intervention, whether or
not it is considered causally related to that treatment. Severity, seriousness,
expectedness, and relatedness are assessed separately.

\medterm{Aerobe} A microorganism that requires molecular oxygen for growth or uses oxygen efficiently for
energy production. Obligate aerobes are unable to grow without oxygen, whereas
facultative organisms can grow with or without it.

\medterm{Aerobic} Requiring or involving molecular oxygen. In microbiology, aerobic organisms use oxygen
for growth or energy production, either obligatorily or as one option among several; in
physiology, aerobic metabolism uses oxidative phosphorylation in mitochondria to
generate ATP from nutrients.

\medterm{Aerophagy} Excessive swallowing of air, also called aerophagia, which can lead to frequent
belching, abdominal bloating, distension, and discomfort.

\synonyms
aerophagia, which can lead to frequent.

\medterm{Aesthetic Dentistry} Dental care intended to improve the appearance of the teeth, gums, or smile. Procedures may include
tooth-colored restorations, veneers, whitening, or selected crowns.

\medterm{Affective Flattening} Markedly reduced outward expression of emotion, including limited facial expression, voice modulation, and gestures.
It may occur in schizophrenia and other psychiatric or neurologic conditions.

\medterm{AFP (Alpha-Fetoprotein)} A protein produced mainly by the fetal liver. Maternal blood AFP is used in prenatal screening, and serum AFP
may help evaluate selected liver and germ-cell tumors. Results are not diagnostic by themselves.

\synonyms
Alpha-Fetoprotein

\medterm{Afterload} The resistance against which the ventricles must eject blood during systole. Left
ventricular afterload is primarily determined by systemic vascular resistance (SVR);
right ventricular afterload is determined by pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR).
Afterload is estimated by blood pressure or calculated as wall stress.

\medterm{Agar} A gelatinous polysaccharide from red algae used to solidify culture media. Melting near
85°C and solidifying at 35--40°C, it remains solid at incubation temperatures and is
largely undegraded by bacteria, enabling isolation of pure colonies on media such as
blood, MacConkey, and Sabouraud agars.

\medterm{Age-Related Macular Degeneration} A degenerative disorder of the macula that can impair central vision in older adults.
The dry form involves retinal and pigment-cell changes and may progress gradually; the
neovascular form involves abnormal blood-vessel growth and may cause more rapid vision
loss. Risk is influenced by age, smoking, family history, and other factors.

\medterm{Age-Standardized Rate} A rate adjusted to a specified standard population age distribution so that populations
with different age structures can be compared more fairly. It is commonly used for
mortality, cancer, and disease-incidence comparisons.

\medterm{Ageism} Stereotyping, prejudice, or discrimination directed at people because of their age,
which can impair access to appropriate medical care.

\medterm{Agenesis of the Corpus Callosum} Complete or partial absence of the corpus callosum, the major commissural fiber bundle
connecting the cerebral hemispheres. May be isolated or associated with other CNS
anomalies (Dandy-Walker, Chiari, heterotopias) and genetic syndromes (Aicardi,
Andermann, acrocallosal syndromes).

\medterm{Ageusia} Complete loss of the sense of taste. Causes include viral infections, oral or nasal
disease, head injury, damage to the facial or glossopharyngeal nerves, medications,
chemotherapy, zinc deficiency, and neurologic disorders.

\medterm{Agglutination} The visible clumping of cells or particles caused by antibodies or other binding
molecules linking antigens on their surfaces. It is used in blood-grouping and
crossmatching, infectious-disease serology, and some laboratory identification tests.

\medterm{Aggregation, Platelet} The process by which activated platelets adhere to one another to form a primary
platelet plug at a site of vascular injury. Platelet agonists such as ADP, thromboxane
A2, and thrombin activate glycoprotein IIb/IIIa, which binds fibrinogen and links
adjacent platelets.

\medterm{Aggression} Verbal or physical behavior intended to threaten, harm, or dominate another person or
object. It may occur with mania, psychosis, personality disorders, dementia, delirium,
substance intoxication or withdrawal, traumatic brain injury, or severe frustration.

\medterm{Aggressive Cancer} A cancer that grows, invades, or spreads rapidly and may require prompt diagnosis and
treatment. Aggressiveness depends on tumour biology, grade, stage, and response to
therapy.

\medterm{Agility} The ability to move or change direction quickly while maintaining control and balance.

\medterm{Aging} Biological process of growing older, involving cumulative changes at molecular,
cellular, tissue, organ levels.

\medterm{Agitation} A state of excessive motor activity, emotional excitement, and restlessness. In
psychiatric settings, it may be a symptom of mania, psychosis, delirium, substance
intoxication, or withdrawal.

\medterm{Agnathia} A rare congenital absence or severe underdevelopment of the mandible, maxilla, or both.
It is usually part of a craniofacial developmental disorder and may cause profound
facial deformity, airway obstruction, feeding difficulty, and impaired dentition.

\medterm{Agnosia} A higher-order neurological deficit characterized by the inability to recognize or
process sensory stimuli (visual, auditory, tactile) despite intact primary sensory
organs and pathways. Caused by focal lesions in specific cortical association areas
(parietal, temporal, or occipital lobes).

\medterm{Agonist} A substance that binds to a receptor and activates it, producing a biological response.
A full agonist can produce the receptor's maximum response, whereas a partial agonist
produces a lower maximum response even when it occupies all available receptors.

\medterm{Agonist-Antagonist} A drug that has mixed pharmacological activity, acting as an agonist at one receptor
type and an antagonist at another, or as a partial agonist at a single receptor.

\medterm{Agoraphobia} Anxiety disorder characterized by fear and avoidance of situations where escape might be
difficult or help unavailable during a panic-like episode. Common feared situations
include public transportation, open spaces, enclosed spaces, standing in line, being in
crowds, and being outside the home alone.

\medterm{Agranulocytosis} A severe reduction or near-absence of circulating neutrophils, usually defined as an
absolute neutrophil count below $0.5\times10^9/\mathrm{L}$. It may be caused by medications,
chemotherapy, autoimmune disease, infections, or bone-marrow disorders and presents with
fever, sore throat, oral ulcers, or recurrent serious infections.

\medterm{Agraphesthesia} Inability to recognize numbers or letters traced on the skin, indicating a lesion of the
contralateral parietal lobe (somatosensory association cortex, postcentral gyrus).
Tested with a blunt object on the palm or fingertip while the patient's eyes are closed.

\medterm{Agraphia} An acquired inability to write or express language in written form despite adequate
education and motor ability. It may result from dominant-parietal or language-network
lesions, especially stroke, traumatic brain injury, dementia, or brain tumors, and may
occur with aphasia, alexia, or acalculia.

\medterm{Aicardi-Goutieres Syndrome} Rare autosomal recessive inflammatory disorder mimicking congenital viral infection.
Characterized by cerebral calcifications, white matter disease, cerebral atrophy, and
cerebrospinal fluid lymphocytosis. Presents with severe neurodevelopmental impairment,
seizures, and spasticity.

\medterm{AIDS} Acquired immunodeficiency syndrome: the advanced stage of HIV infection, defined by CD4
count <200 cells/µL or the presence of AIDS-defining illnesses.

\medterm{Air Abrasion} Minimally invasive caries removal using abrasive particles. Alternative to drilling for
small lesions.

\medterm{Air bronchogram} A radiographic sign showing air-filled bronchi visible against opacified lung
parenchyma, indicating that the airways remain patent while the surrounding alveoli are
filled with fluid, pus, or cells.

\medterm{Air Contaminants} Solid, liquid, or gaseous substances in air that may harm health, such as particulate matter, fumes, gases, allergens,
or microorganisms. Effects depend on the contaminant, concentration, and duration of exposure.

\medterm{Air Embolism} Entry of gas into the venous or arterial circulation, where it can obstruct blood flow and injure tissues. It may
occur during medical procedures, trauma, or rapid changes in environmental pressure.
Symptoms and treatment depend on the amount and location of gas and require urgent
clinical assessment when significant embolism is suspected.

\medterm{Air Polishing} Removal of stains using air-abrasive device. Glycine or erythritol powder.

\medterm{Air Quality} The condition of air as determined by the concentration of pollutants such as particulate matter, ozone, nitrogen dioxide, and sulfur dioxide.

\medterm{Airborne Disease} An infection that can spread through small suspended particles or aerosols under suitable conditions. Transmission depends on
the organism, environment, ventilation, and duration of exposure.

\medterm{Airborne precautions} Infection control measures used for pathogens transmitted through airborne droplet
nuclei. Precautions include negative pressure rooms, N95 respirators, and restricted
access. Airborne precautions are used for tuberculosis, measles, varicella, and
disseminated herpes zoster.

\medterm{Airway Clearance Techniques} Physical or mechanical methods used to mobilize and remove airway secretions. Examples include
directed coughing, breathing exercises, postural drainage, positive expiratory pressure,
and high-frequency chest-wall oscillation. Selection depends on the disease, secretion
burden, strength, and ability to cooperate.

\medterm{Airway Management} Airway management encompasses techniques and devices used to maintain ventilation and
oxygenation in patients with compromised airways.

\medterm{Airway Obstruction} Partial or complete blockage of airflow through the upper or lower respiratory tract. It
may result from a foreign body, secretions, swelling, trauma, tumor, or loss of airway
muscle tone and can cause stridor, wheeze, hypoxaemia, respiratory distress, or arrest.

\medterm{Airway Resistance} The opposition to airflow through the conducting airways, determined largely by airway
radius and flow pattern. Bronchoconstriction, mucus, edema, and reduced elastic traction
increase resistance; because resistance varies strongly with radius, small changes can
markedly affect work of breathing.

\medterm{Akathisia} Subjective feeling of inner restlessness accompanied by a compulsive need to move, most
commonly an extrapyramidal side effect of antipsychotic drugs (typical and atypical
neuroleptics), also seen with dopamine antagonists (metoclopramide), selective serotonin
reuptake inhibitor withdrawal, and in Parkinson disease.

\medterm{Akinesia} Impairment or absence of voluntary movement, classically a hypokinetic feature of basal
ganglia disorders such as Parkinson disease and related parkinsonian syndromes.

\medterm{Alagille Syndrome} An inherited disorder of Notch signaling that commonly affects the liver, heart, skeleton,
eyes, and characteristic facial features. It is usually caused by a pathogenic variant in JAG1 or, less often, NOTCH2.

\medterm{ALARA Principle} Radiation-protection principle requiring exposure to be kept as low as reasonably
achievable while obtaining the necessary diagnostic or therapeutic information.
Justification, optimization, collimation, shielding, time reduction, and increased
distance support ALARA.

\medterm{Albinism (Oculocutaneous Albinism)} A group of inherited disorders that reduce melanin production in the skin, hair, and eyes. It may
cause light pigmentation, reduced visual acuity, nystagmus, and increased sensitivity
to sunlight; the severity depends on the genetic subtype.

\synonyms
Oculocutaneous Albinism

\medterm{Albright's hereditary osteodystrophy} A group of inherited features associated with abnormalities of GNAS signaling, including short
metacarpal bones, a round facial appearance, short stature, and subcutaneous
ossification. Some associated disorders also cause resistance to parathyroid hormone
or other hormones.

\medterm{Alanine Aminotransferase (ALT)} An enzyme found mainly in liver cells that is released into the blood when hepatocytes are injured. Serum ALT is widely used to screen for and monitor liver disease and is interpreted together with aspartate aminotransferase, alkaline phosphatase, bilirubin, and the clinical context.

\synonyms
ALT

\medterm{Albumin} The main protein in blood plasma, produced by the liver. It helps maintain oncotic pressure and
transports various substances, including hormones, fatty acids, and medications.

\medterm{Albumin-to-Creatinine Ratio} The urinary albumin concentration standardized to urinary creatinine concentration,
usually measured in a spot urine sample. Persistent albuminuria is a marker of kidney
damage and is categorized as A1 (<30 mg/g), A2 (30--300 mg/g), or A3 (>300 mg/g) in
commonly used CKD classification.

\medterm{Albuminuria} Abnormally increased excretion of albumin in the urine, indicating disruption of the
glomerular filtration barrier or another form of kidney disease. It is commonly detected
with a urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio and classified as moderately or severely
increased according to the quantity and persistence of excretion.

\medterm{Alcohol} Ethanol, a psychoactive substance that can impair judgment and coordination and cause intoxication or dependence.

\medterm{Alcohol dependency} A chronic relapsing disorder characterized by compulsive ethanol use, loss of control
over intake, and negative emotional state during withdrawal, leading to tolerance,
dependence, and organ damage over time.

\medterm{Alcohol Intoxication} Acute impairment caused by ethanol, which may produce disinhibition, impaired coordination, slurred speech,
vomiting, reduced consciousness, or respiratory depression. Severe intoxication can be
life-threatening and requires assessment of breathing, circulation, glucose, trauma, and
other substances.

\medterm{Alcohol Swab} A single-use pad containing alcohol for cleaning intact skin before selected procedures. It should be used according to the product
instructions and allowed to dry.

\medterm{Alcohol Use} Consumption of alcoholic beverages. Health effects depend on amount, frequency, pattern, and individual risk.

\medterm{Alcohol Use Disorder} A problematic pattern of alcohol use leading to significant impairment or distress.
Severity is classified as mild (2--3 criteria), moderate (4--5 criteria), or severe (6
or more criteria).

\medterm{Alcohol Withdrawal} A syndrome that may occur after abrupt cessation or substantial reduction of prolonged heavy alcohol use.
Symptoms can include tremor, anxiety, sweating, nausea, hallucinations, seizures, or
delirium; onset and severity vary, and severe withdrawal requires urgent medical care.

\medterm{Alcoholic Cardiomyopathy} Dilated cardiomyopathy caused by chronic excessive alcohol consumption, one of the most
common reversible causes of dilated cardiomyopathy. Direct toxic effect of ethanol and
its metabolite acetaldehyde on cardiomyocytes. Presentation is identical to other
dilated cardiomyopathies.

\medterm{Alcoholic Ketoacidosis} A high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis caused by prolonged heavy alcohol use, poor
nutritional intake, and vomiting. It typically presents with abdominal pain, nausea,
vomiting, dehydration, and tachypnea, often with a normal or low blood glucose
concentration.

\medterm{Alcoholic Neuropathy} A length-dependent peripheral neuropathy associated with chronic excessive alcohol use
and nutritional deficiency, especially thiamine deficiency. It causes burning pain,
numbness, paraesthesia, and weakness, most prominently in the feet and lower legs.

\medterm{Aldosterone} A mineralocorticoid hormone produced by the zona glomerulosa of the adrenal cortex, the
final effector of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS).

\medterm{Alexia} An acquired inability to comprehend written language despite intact vision and otherwise preserved language function, usually caused by stroke or other lesions affecting the dominant-hemisphere reading networks. When reading is impaired but writing remains possible, the pattern is called alexia without agraphia.

\medterm{Algor Mortis} Post-mortem cooling of the body following death until it reaches ambient environmental
temperature. Serves as one of the physical estimators of the post-mortem interval (time
since death).

\medterm{Aliasing Artifact} An ultrasound display artifact in which blood flow exceeds the selected velocity scale and appears to reverse direction.
It can be reduced by increasing the scale or changing the imaging settings.

\medterm{Alkaline phosphatase} A group of enzymes that catalyze the hydrolysis of phosphate esters at alkaline pH,
found in high concentrations in the liver, bone, placenta, and intestinal mucosa.
Elevated serum levels occur in obstructive liver disease, bone disorders including Paget
disease and fractures, pregnancy, and certain malignancies.

\medterm{Alkalosis} A metabolic (elevated bicarbonate) or respiratory (low PaCO2) disturbance that raises
arterial pH above 7.45. Metabolic alkalosis: vomiting/NG suction (HCl loss), diuretics,
mineralocorticoid excess, hypokalemia, and alkali ingestion—compensated by
hypoventilation; chloride-responsive vs. chloride-resistant distinguishes causes.

\medterm{Allele} Alternative form of a gene at a particular locus. Individuals inherit two alleles for
each gene (one from each parent). Alleles can be dominant, recessive, or codominant.

\medterm{Allergen} A substance capable of causing an allergic immune response in a susceptible person. Common allergens include
proteins in foods, pollens, medicines, animal dander, insect venom, and natural rubber.

\medterm{Allergen Immunotherapy} Treatment that gives gradually increasing doses of a specific allergen by injection or under the tongue to
reduce allergic sensitivity. It may be used for selected allergic rhinitis, conjunctivitis, asthma, or insect-venom allergy.

\medterm{Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis} A hypersensitivity reaction to Aspergillus fumigatus in asthma or CF patients. Causes
central bronchiectasis, elevated total IgE, and eosinophilia. Treatment includes
corticosteroids and itraconazole.

\medterm{Allergic Conjunctivitis} An inflammatory condition of the conjunctiva mediated by IgE-dependent mast cell
degranulation in response to environmental allergens, characterized by bilateral ocular
itching, tearing, conjunctival injection, and eyelid edema.

\medterm{Allergic Dermatitis} An inflammatory skin reaction caused by an immune response to an external allergen, most
commonly allergic contact dermatitis. It is a delayed, T-cell-mediated hypersensitivity
reaction that typically develops after prior sensitization.

\medterm{Allergic Reactions} Immune reactions to substances that are usually harmless, such as foods, medicines, pollens, or insect venom.
They may be immediate or delayed and can range from localized symptoms to anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergic Rhinitis} An IgE-mediated inflammatory response of the nasal mucosa to inhaled allergens, causing
sneezing, nasal congestion, clear rhinorrhea, nasal itching, and often conjunctival
symptoms. Management may include allergen avoidance, intranasal corticosteroids,
antihistamines, and allergen immunotherapy when indicated.

\medterm{Allergic Rhinitis (Hay Fever)} Allergies occur when the immune system overreacts to harmless environmental substances
such as pollen, dust mites, mold, or pet dander. Hay fever (allergic rhinitis)
specifically involves nasal symptoms like sneezing, congestion, and itchy eyes triggered
by airborne allergens.

\synonyms
Hay Fever

\medterm{Allergies} Immune reactions to normally harmless substances such as foods, pollens, medicines,
insect venom, or animal dander. Reactions range from localized rhinitis or dermatitis to
life-threatening anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergy Testing} Identification of IgE-mediated allergic sensitization. Skin prick test (SPT): allergen
extract applied to skin via lancet, read after 15 min (wheal >3mm = positive).
Intradermal test: more sensitive, used when SPT negative but suspicion remains.

\medterm{Allodynia} Pain caused by a stimulus that is normally not painful, such as light touch or gentle pressure. It may
occur with neuropathic, nociplastic, or other pain conditions.

\medterm{Allograft} Tissue transplanted between genetically different individuals of the same species.
Allografts may provoke immune rejection and require appropriate donor screening,
processing, and, in some settings, immunosuppression.

\medterm{Allosteric Regulation} The regulation of enzyme activity by the binding of an effector molecule at a site other
than the active site (allosteric site), causing a conformational change that alters the
enzyme's activity. Allosteric enzymes typically have multiple subunits with cooperative
binding kinetics (sigmoidal rather than hyperbolic velocity curves).

\medterm{Alogia} Poverty of speech or poverty of content of speech, a negative symptom of schizophrenia.
It manifests as brief, empty replies to questions, reduced spontaneous speech, and lack
of elaboration. It reflects dysfunction in the mesocortical dopamine pathway and is less
responsive to antipsychotic medications than positive symptoms.

\medterm{Alopecia} The general medical term for loss of hair from the scalp or body, rather than a single specific disease. Causes include disorders of the hair follicle, immune disease, hormones, medicines, systemic illness, or physical injury. Androgenetic alopecia (male- or female-pattern hair loss) is common and is associated with genetic susceptibility and androgen, particularly dihydrotestosterone (DHT), effects. Alopecia areata is an autoimmune, usually non-scarring, patchy hair-loss disorder that may progress to alopecia totalis (entire scalp) or universalis (whole body).

\medterm{Alopecia Areata} A common, autoimmune, non-scarring hair loss disorder affecting approximately 1 to 2
percent of the population worldwide, with a lifetime risk of 2 percent, occurring
equally across sexes and ethnicities, with peak onset between 15 and 29 years.

\medterm{Alpha-1 Antitrypsin Deficiency} An autosomal codominant genetic disorder caused by mutations in the SERPINA1 gene. It
predisposes to early-onset panlobular emphysema (lower lobes), liver disease, and
panniculitis. PiZZ genotype is most severe. Treatment includes AAT augmentation therapy
and smoking avoidance.

\medterm{Alpha-Agonists} Medicines that stimulate alpha-adrenergic receptors. Depending on the receptor and tissue, they can constrict
blood vessels, alter autonomic activity, or reduce aqueous-humor production in the eye.
Their uses and adverse effects depend on the specific medicine and route.

\medterm{Alpha-Glucosidase Inhibitors} Oral glucose-lowering medications including acarbose, miglitol, and voglibose that delay
carbohydrate digestion and glucose absorption in the small intestine by inhibiting brush
border alpha-glucoside enzymes. Reduce postprandial glucose excursions without affecting
fasting glucose significantly.

\medterm{Alpha-Ketoglutarate} A five-carbon intermediate of the citric acid cycle formed from isocitrate and converted
to succinyl-CoA. It also serves as a carbon skeleton for amino-acid metabolism and
participates in oxidative deamination and cellular signaling.

\medterm{Alport Syndrome} Hereditary nephritis caused by mutations in type four collagen genes leading to
progressive sensorineural hearing loss, ocular anomalies, and renal failure. X-linked
form from COL4A5 mutations is the most common. Autosomal recessive and dominant forms
exist.

\medterm{ALS} Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (motor neuron disease; Lou Gehrig disease) is the progressive neurodegenerative disease characterized by degeneration of upper and lower motor neurons controlling voluntary movement. It causes progressive weakness, muscle wasting, fasciculations, spasticity, dysarthria, or dysphagia; sensation is usually relatively preserved, but respiratory muscles may eventually be affected.

\medterm{Altitude Illness} Acute mountain sickness (AMS): headache + 1 symptom (nausea, fatigue, dizziness,
insomnia) at >2500m. HACE: AMS + ataxia/altered mental status (cerebral edema). HAPE:
dyspnea at rest, cough, frothy sputum, crackles, hypoxemia (noncardiogenic pulmonary
edema).

\medterm{Aluminum Phosphide} A highly toxic fumigant that releases phosphine gas when exposed to moisture or stomach acid. Poisoning can
cause severe cardiovascular, respiratory, gastrointestinal, and metabolic effects and
requires immediate emergency treatment and supportive intensive care.

\medterm{Alvarado Score} Clinical scoring system for acute appendicitis. Components (MANTRELS): Migration of pain
(1), Anorexia (1), Nausea/vomiting (1), Tenderness in RLQ (2), Rebound pain (1),
Elevated temperature (1), Leukocytosis (2), Left shift (1).

\medterm{Alveolar Bone} Bone surrounding and supporting tooth roots. Resorbs with tooth loss. Maintained by
function. Bone grafting may be needed for implants.

\medterm{Alveolar Gas Equation} PAO2 = (FiO2 x (Patm - PH2O)) - (PaCO2 / R). Patm = 760 mmHg, PH2O = 47 mmHg, R = 0.8.
Allows calculation of A-a gradient.

\medterm{Alveolar Ventilation} The volume of fresh air reaching the alveoli per minute, representing the portion of
minute ventilation that participates in gas exchange. Calculated as (tidal volume - dead
space volume) x respiratory rate. Normal alveolar ventilation is approximately 4-5
L/min.

\medterm{Alveolar Ventilation Equation} The relationship between alveolar ventilation and arterial carbon-dioxide tension,
approximately PaCO2 = 0.863 times carbon-dioxide production divided by alveolar
ventilation. It explains why reduced effective ventilation causes hypercapnia.

\medterm{Alveolus} The terminal air sac (200-300 microns) where gas exchange occurs. Approximately 300-500
million per lung, providing ~70 m2 surface area. Wall: type I pneumocytes (95\% surface,
thin for diffusion), type II pneumocytes (5\%, secrete pulmonary surfactant reducing
surface tension), alveolar macrophages (dust cells, phagocytosis).

\medterm{Alzheimer Disease} A progressive neurodegenerative disorder characterized pathologically by amyloid-beta
plaques, tau neurofibrillary pathology, and neuronal loss. It most commonly presents
with insidious impairment of episodic memory followed by broader cognitive and
functional decline.

\medterm{Amalgam} Restorative material composed of mercury alloyed with silver, tin, copper, and other
metals. Properties: strong, durable, cost-effective. Concerns: mercury toxicity debates,
aesthetic limitations. Placement: requires cavity preparation, condensation, carving.
Removal: sectioning, cooling to prevent mercury vapor.

\medterm{Amaurosis Fugax} Transient monocular vision loss lasting seconds to minutes, classically described as a
curtain descending over the field, caused by retinal or optic nerve ischemia.

\medterm{Ambiguous Genitalia} External genital anatomy that does not have typical male or female features, usually
because of a difference in sex development. Evaluation includes examination, chromosome
and hormone testing, pelvic or abdominal imaging, and assessment for congenital adrenal
hyperplasia.

\medterm{Ambulatory Blood Pressure Monitoring} Measurement of blood pressure at regular intervals, usually over twenty-four hours, with a portable cuff worn during normal activities and sleep. It detects white-coat and masked hypertension, evaluates nighttime blood-pressure patterns, and helps confirm hypertension diagnosed in the clinic.

\medterm{Amblyopia} A developmental reduction in vision caused by abnormal visual input during early childhood, without a sufficient structural explanation.
It commonly results from misalignment, unequal focusing power, or obstruction of vision
in one eye.

\medterm{Ambu Bag} A self-inflating manual resuscitation bag used with a mask or airway device to provide positive-pressure ventilation.

\medterm{Amebiasis} Infection with the protozoan parasite Entamoeba histolytica, acquired mainly by
ingestion of faecally contaminated food or water. It may be asymptomatic or cause
amoebic colitis with abdominal pain, diarrhoea, or dysentery, and can spread through the
portal circulation to produce a liver abscess.

\medterm{Amebic Liver Abscess} A focal collection of necrotic material in the liver caused by Entamoeba histolytica,
usually following intestinal infection. It commonly causes fever, right-upper-quadrant
pain, and tender hepatomegaly; diagnosis is supported by imaging and serology.

\medterm{Amelanotic melanoma} A variant of melanoma that lacks significant melanin production, presenting as a pink,
red, or flesh-coloured papule or nodule rather than a typical dark lesion. Its paucity
of pigment can delay diagnosis, making it a diagnostic challenge. Histopathology with
immunohistochemical markers confirms the diagnosis.

\medterm{Amelia} A rare congenital anomaly characterized by complete absence of one or more limbs. It may occur alone or as part of a
genetic or developmental syndrome. Prenatal imaging can identify the anomaly and help
assess associated abnormalities.

\medterm{Ameloblastoma} Locally aggressive odontogenic tumor. Multicystic radiolucency in posterior mandible.
High recurrence rate. Treatment: surgical resection with margins.

\medterm{Amelogenesis} The formation and maturation of tooth enamel by ameloblasts during tooth development.

\medterm{Amelogenesis Imperfecta} A group of inherited disorders in which enamel formation is abnormal, causing enamel that may be thin, poorly mineralized, weak, or easily worn on most or all teeth.

\medterm{Amenorrhea} Absence of menstruation. Primary amenorrhea: no menarche by age 15 with normal secondary
sexual development, or by age 13 without. Secondary amenorrhea: absence of menses for
three cycles or six months in women with prior menstruation.

\medterm{Amino Acid} An organic molecule containing an amino group, a carboxyl group, a hydrogen atom, and a variable side chain attached
to a central carbon. Amino acids are the building blocks of proteins; cells use some
also in energy metabolism and other biochemical pathways.

\medterm{Amino Acid Catabolism} The breakdown of amino acids after protein digestion, with the amino group removed and
the carbon skeleton metabolized for energy or gluconeogenesis/ketogenesis.
Transamination: amino groups are transferred to alpha-ketoglutarate, forming glutamate,
by aminotransferases requiring pyridoxal phosphate (PLP, vitamin B6) as a cofactor.

\medterm{Aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity} Kidney damage caused by aminoglycoside antibiotics, characterized by acute tubular
necrosis. Nephrotoxicity is caused by accumulation of aminoglycosides in the proximal
tubular cells. The risk is increased with prolonged therapy, high doses, concurrent
nephrotoxic drugs, and renal impairment.

\medterm{Aminoglycoside ototoxicity} Damage to the inner ear caused by aminoglycoside antibiotics, resulting in hearing loss
and vestibular dysfunction. Ototoxicity is caused by accumulation of aminoglycosides in
the inner ear, damaging hair cells. The risk is increased with prolonged therapy, high
doses, concurrent ototoxic drugs, and renal impairment.

\medterm{Aminoglycosides} A class of bactericidal antibiotics that bind irreversibly to the 30S ribosomal subunit,
causing misreading of messenger RNA and inhibiting protein synthesis. They include
gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin, and streptomycin.

\medterm{Amiodarone-induced skin discoloration} A slate-grey to bluish hyperpigmentation of sun-exposed skin caused by long-term
amiodarone therapy, due to deposition of the drug and its metabolites in dermal
macrophages. It is dose-related, more pronounced in photodistributed areas, and may be
partially reversible upon drug cessation.

\medterm{Amiodarone-Induced Thyroid Dysfunction} Thyroid abnormalities occurring in approximately fifteen to twenty percent of patients
on amiodarone due to the high iodine content of the molecule. Type one results from
iodine-induced increased thyroid hormone synthesis in patients with underlying thyroid
disease. Type two results from destructive thyroiditis.

\medterm{Amiodarone-induced vortex keratopathy} Whorl-like corneal epithelial deposits caused by amiodarone accumulation, appearing as
golden-brown verticillata on slit-lamp examination, usually asymptomatic but reversible
upon drug discontinuation.

\medterm{Ammonia} A nitrogen-containing waste product produced during protein metabolism and normally converted to urea by the liver. Elevated
blood ammonia may occur with liver failure, urea-cycle disorders, some medicines, or other metabolic conditions.

\medterm{Amnesia} Loss or impairment of memory that is greater than ordinary forgetting. It may affect the
ability to form new memories (anterograde amnesia), recall past events (retrograde
amnesia), or both.

\medterm{Amniocentesis} A procedure in which a needle is used to obtain amniotic fluid for genetic, chromosomal, infectious, or other
prenatal testing. It is usually performed after the first trimester when clinically
indicated; risks and benefits depend on the reason for testing and gestational age.

\medterm{Amnion} The innermost fetal membrane, lining the amniotic cavity and containing the amniotic
fluid. A thin, avascular membrane composed of a single layer of cuboidal epithelium
overlying a basement membrane and connective tissue. The amnion produces and resorbs
amniotic fluid. It fuses with the chorion by 12-14 weeks.

\medterm{Amniotic Band Syndrome} A spectrum of congenital anomalies caused by strands of a ruptured amniotic membrane that constrict or entangle developing fetal parts. Effects range from ring-like limb constrictions, swelling, or loss of digits to facial and body-wall disruptions; severity varies widely, and management depends on the structures involved.

\medterm{Amniotic Fluid} The clear to pale-yellow fluid surrounding the fetus within the amniotic sac. Early in
pregnancy it is derived largely from maternal plasma; later, fetal urine and lung fluid
are major sources, and the fetus continuously swallows and recycles it.

\medterm{Amniotic Fluid Embolism} A rare obstetric emergency involving sudden respiratory or cardiovascular collapse and often severe coagulopathy
during labor, delivery, or shortly afterward. It requires immediate supportive and multidisciplinary treatment.

\medterm{Amniotic Fluid Index} An ultrasound estimate of amniotic-fluid volume obtained by dividing the uterus into four quadrants,
measuring the deepest unobstructed vertical pocket in each quadrant, and adding the
measurements. Interpretation uses gestational-age and practice-specific thresholds to
identify unusually low or high fluid volume.

\medterm{Amoebiasis} Infection caused by the protozoan parasite Entamoeba histolytica, transmitted via the
fecal-oral route (contaminated food or water).

\medterm{Amoxicillin rash in infectious mononucleosis} A maculopapular, non-allergic rash that develops in up to 90 percent of patients with
Epstein-Barr virus infectious mononucleosis who receive amoxicillin or ampicillin. The
rash is often widespread and pruritic, and its presence does not indicate true
penicillin allergy.

\medterm{AMP (Adenosine Monophosphate)} A nucleotide composed of adenine, ribose, and a single phosphate group. Formed by
hydrolysis of ADP (ADP -> AMP + Pi) or directly from ATP via adenylate kinase (2 ADP <->
ATP + AMP).

\synonyms
Adenosine Monophosphate

\medterm{AMPK} AMP-activated protein kinase. Energy sensor. Activated by AMP. Inhibits anabolism,
stimulates catabolism.

\medterm{Amputation} Surgical removal of a limb or part of a limb. The level may involve a digit, foot, lower leg,
thigh, or hip, or a corresponding part of the upper limb. The level is selected according to tissue viability,
function, and potential for rehabilitation.

\medterm{Amputation Stump} The remaining part of a limb after amputation. It must be monitored for wound healing,
infection, poor circulation, neuroma pain, phantom-limb pain, and skin problems caused by prosthetic use.
Care includes hygiene, compression or shaping when advised, progressive strengthening, and professional
prosthetic and rehabilitation assessment.

\medterm{AMR (Antimicrobial Resistance)} The ability of a microorganism to survive or grow despite an antimicrobial medicine that would normally inhibit or
kill it.

\synonyms
Antimicrobial Resistance

\medterm{Amygdala} Almond-shaped nucleus cluster in the anterior medial temporal lobe, part of the limbic
system, primarily processing emotions (especially fear/anxiety). Receives
thalamic/cortical sensory input; projects to hypothalamus, brainstem, and cortex for
autonomic/behavioral fear responses.

\medterm{Amylase} An enzyme that breaks down starch and is produced mainly by the salivary glands and pancreas. Blood levels may rise in several
conditions, including some pancreatic disorders.

\medterm{Amylase and Lipase} Digestive enzymes measured in blood when pancreatic disease is suspected. Lipase is generally more specific
for pancreatic injury and remains elevated longer than amylase, but neither test alone
diagnoses acute pancreatitis; results must be interpreted with symptoms and imaging.

\medterm{Amylin Analogues} Medicines that mimic amylin, a hormone released with insulin by pancreatic beta cells. Pramlintide slows gastric
emptying, reduces post-meal glucagon secretion, and may be used with insulin in selected patients with diabetes.

\medterm{Amyloid PET} A positron-emission tomography scan using a tracer that binds to amyloid deposits in the brain. It can support evaluation of selected
cognitive disorders but is interpreted with clinical assessment and other tests.

\medterm{Amyloid purpura} Purpuric discoloration of the eyelids and periorbital skin (raccoon eyes) caused by
cutaneous amyloid deposition, which weakens vessel walls and leads to extravasation of
erythrocytes. It may be a sign of systemic amyloidosis, particularly AL amyloidosis, and
warrants further workup including serum free light chains and tissue biopsy.

\medterm{Amyloidosis} A group of disorders characterized by the extracellular deposition of misfolded proteins
that polymerize into insoluble fibrils with a distinctive beta-pleated sheet
configuration. These fibrils bind Congo red dye and show characteristic apple-green
birefringence under polarized light microscopy.

\medterm{Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis} Progressive neurodegenerative disorder affecting both upper and lower motor neurons,
causing weakness, atrophy, fasciculations, and eventually respiratory failure. Most
cases are sporadic (90 percent); 10 percent are familial. Average age of onset 55-60
years.

\medterm{Anabolism} The set of metabolic pathways that construct complex molecules from simpler precursors,
requiring energy input (ATP, reducing equivalents NADPH). Examples: protein synthesis
from amino acids, glycogen synthesis (glycogenesis), fatty acid synthesis (lipogenesis),
cholesterol synthesis, nucleotide synthesis, gluconeogenesis.

\medterm{Anaemia} A condition in which the number of red blood cells or the hemoglobin concentration is
below the normal range, reducing the oxygen-carrying capacity of the blood.

\medterm{Anabolic Steroids} Synthetic derivatives of testosterone used in selected medical conditions and misused for muscle building or performance enhancement. Nonmedical use can cause acne, infertility, testicular shrinkage, breast enlargement, mood disturbance, and cardiovascular disease, and sudden cessation may produce symptoms of hormone deficiency.

\medterm{Anaerobe} A microorganism that grows without molecular oxygen. Obligate anaerobes may be injured
or killed by oxygen, while aerotolerant anaerobes tolerate oxygen but do not use it for
energy production.

\medterm{Anal Abscess} Collection of pus in the perianal or ischiorectal space usually from infected anal
glands. Presents with throbbing anal pain, swelling, fever, and difficulty sitting.
Diagnosed clinically with tenderness and fluctuance. Treatment is incision and drainage
under local anesthesia. Antibiotics are not routinely required for simple abscesses.

\medterm{Anal Canal} The terminal 3-4 cm of the gastrointestinal tract, extending from the anorectal junction
(puborectalis/pectinate line) to the anal verge. Above the pectinate line: lined by
columnar epithelium, innervated by autonomic nerves, draining to portal system, supplied
by superior rectal artery.

\medterm{Anal Cancer} Malignant tumor of the anal canal or margin, most commonly squamous cell carcinoma
associated with high-risk human papillomavirus infection. Presents with bleeding, pain,
or a mass; primary treatment is chemoradiation, with surgery reserved for residual or
recurrent disease.

\medterm{Anal Fissure} Linear tear in the distal anal mucosa usually posterior in the midline. Presents with
painful defecation and bright red blood on wiping or in the toilet. Associated with
constipation, hard stools, and chronic diarrhea. The internal anal sphincter spasm
perpetuates the fissure by reducing blood flow to the posterior midline.

\medterm{Anal Fistula} Abnormal tract connecting the anal canal to the perianal skin usually following a
drained abscess. Presents with recurrent drainage, pain, and pruritus. Parks
classification categorizes fistulae by relationship to the sphincter muscles. Diagnosed
by examination under anesthesia and MRI.

\medterm{Anal Intercourse} The insertion of the penis into the anus and rectum for sexual pleasure. The anal canal
is rich in nerve endings, making it a sensitive erogenous zone. The rectum lacks natural
lubrication, so artificial lubricants are essential to prevent mucosal trauma.

\medterm{Anal Itching} Pruritus around the anus. Common causes include irritation, moisture, dermatitis, hemorrhoids, infections, and
occasionally pinworms or other disease. Gentle cleansing, keeping the area dry, and avoiding irritants may help;
persistent, severe, bleeding, or painful symptoms require medical evaluation.

\medterm{Analgesic} A drug that relieves pain without producing loss of consciousness.

\medterm{Analgesic Nephropathy} Chronic tubulointerstitial renal injury associated with prolonged or excessive use of
analgesic medicines. It may cause impaired urinary concentration, sterile pyuria,
haematuria, papillary necrosis, hypertension, and progressive chronic kidney disease.

\medterm{Analysis of Urine} Laboratory examination of urine for cells, blood, protein, glucose, microorganisms, and other
substances.

\medterm{Analytic Sensitivity} The smallest amount or concentration of an analyte that a laboratory method can reliably
detect. It concerns measurement performance and differs from clinical sensitivity, which
describes detection of disease in affected patients.

\medterm{Anaphylactic Shock} A severe, potentially life-threatening systemic hypersensitivity reaction characterized
by rapid onset of multisystem involvement, primarily affecting the skin, respiratory
tract, cardiovascular system, and gastrointestinal tract. See Anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Anaphylaxis} A severe, potentially life-threatening systemic hypersensitivity reaction characterized
by rapid onset of multisystem involvement, primarily affecting the skin, respiratory
tract, cardiovascular system, and gastrointestinal tract.

\medterm{Anaphylaxis During Anesthesia} A life-threatening systemic hypersensitivity reaction that may cause sudden hypotension,
bronchospasm, cardiovascular collapse, and sometimes urticaria or flushing. Common
perioperative triggers include neuromuscular-blocking agents, antibiotics,
chlorhexidine, and latex.

\medterm{Anaplasia} A lack of differentiation, describing cells that have reverted to a more primitive,
embryonic phenotype with loss of the specialized structural and functional
characteristics of their tissue of origin.

\medterm{Anaplastic Thyroid Cancer} A rare, rapidly growing, and highly aggressive thyroid cancer that often presents as a
large fixed neck mass with dysphagia or airway compromise. Prognosis is poor; management
is usually palliative.

\medterm{Anastomosis (Vascular)} A surgical or natural connection between blood vessels that permits blood to flow from one vessel
to another.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Anastomotic Leak} Breakdown of a surgical anastomosis in the gastrointestinal tract causing spillage of
luminal contents into the peritoneal cavity. Presents with fever, tachycardia, abdominal
pain, leukocytosis, and peritonitis. Diagnosed by CT with oral or rectal contrast
showing extraluminal contrast or fluid collection.

\medterm{Anatomical Axis} An imaginary straight line around which a body part moves or rotates.

\medterm{Anatomical Planes} Hypothetical flat surfaces that pass through the body in anatomical position, used to
slice the body for anatomical descriptions or medical imaging.

\medterm{Anatomical Position} The standard reference position of the body used to describe anatomy, defined as
standing erect, facing forward, with arms at the sides and palms facing forward.

\medterm{Anatomical Snuffbox} Triangular depression on the dorsal wrist, bounded by extensor pollicis longus medially
and extensor pollicis brevis/abductor pollicis longus laterally. Floor contains the
scaphoid, trapezium, and radial artery. Tenderness after a fall on an outstretched hand
suggests scaphoid fracture. The radial artery pulse is palpable here.

\medterm{Anatomy} Study of body structure. Regions: head, neck, thorax, abdomen, pelvis, extremities.
Systems: skeletal, muscular, nervous, circulatory, respiratory, digestive, urinary,
reproductive, endocrine, immune.

\medterm{Anomalous Anatomy} A structural variation or congenital abnormality in the expected arrangement, number, origin, or course of an organ, vessel, duct, or other body structure. It may be incidental or clinically important during diagnosis, surgery, or interventional procedures.

\medterm{Anatomy Survey (Level II Ultrasound)} A comprehensive fetal ultrasound performed at 18-22 weeks to evaluate fetal anatomy and
screen for structural anomalies.

\synonyms
Level II ultrasound; anatomy scan.

\medterm{Anemia in Pregnancy} Anemia occurring during pregnancy, commonly because of iron deficiency or the normal plasma-volume expansion of pregnancy.
Assessment considers hemoglobin, mean corpuscular volume, iron status, folate, vitamin B12, bleeding, and other causes.

\synonyms
Gestational anemia; pregnancy-associated anemia.

\medterm{Apgar Score} A structured assessment of a newborn's appearance, pulse, grimace, activity, and respiration, usually recorded at 1 and 5 minutes after birth.
It describes immediate adaptation after delivery and helps guide resuscitation; it is not a standalone diagnosis or long-term prognostic test.

\synonyms
Apgar assessment; newborn Apgar score.

\medterm{ANCA-Associated Vasculitis} A group of small-vessel vasculitides associated with antineutrophil cytoplasmic
antibodies. Includes granulomatosis with polyangiitis (c-ANCA/PR3), microscopic
polyangiitis (p-ANCA/MPO), and eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis (p-ANCA/MPO
with asthma/eosinophilia).

\medterm{Ancillary Test (Pathology)} A test performed in addition to routine morphology to refine a pathologic diagnosis.
Examples include IHC, flow cytometry, cytogenetics, molecular testing, special stains,
and electron microscopy.

\synonyms
Pathology

\medterm{Ancylostoma (Hookworm)} Nematode infestation by Ancylostoma duodenale (Old World) or Necator americanus (New
World), common in tropical and subtropical regions where sanitation is poor.

\synonyms
Hookworm

\medterm{Androgen Deficiency} Hypogonadism in males: reduced testosterone production causing symptoms and signs.
Causes: primary (testicular—Klinefelter, mumps orchitis, chemotherapy, trauma) and
secondary (pituitary/hypothalamic—prolactinoma, iron overload, pituitary lesions,
opioids, obesity, aging).

\medterm{Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome} A difference of sex development in which the cells of a genetically male fetus cannot respond to androgens because of a defect in the androgen receptor. Affected individuals have typical female external genitalia and breast development but no uterus, and testes that may lie in the groin or abdomen; diagnosis and care require specialist endocrine and genetic assessment.

\medterm{Androgenetic Alopecia} The most common cause of hair loss, caused by progressive miniaturization of genetically
susceptible hair follicles under androgen influence. Male pattern commonly affects the
frontal hairline and vertex; female pattern commonly causes diffuse crown thinning with
relative preservation of the frontal hairline.

\medterm{Andropause} Age-related testosterone decline in men. Symptoms: fatigue, decreased libido,
depression. Treatment controversial.

\medterm{Anejaculation} Absence of ejaculation. Causes: neurological, surgical, pharmacological, psychological.

\medterm{Anemia} A reduction in the oxygen-carrying capacity of the blood, defined by a hemoglobin
concentration below the normal range for age and sex: less than 13.5 g/dL in adult
males, less than 12.0 g/dL in adult females.

\medterm{Anemia (Cancer)} Anemia occurring in a person with cancer, caused by the cancer itself, bleeding, inflammation, nutritional deficiency,
or treatment. Evaluation identifies the cause; management may include treating the
underlying problem, iron or other replacement, transfusion, or selected medicines.

\synonyms
Cancer

\medterm{Anemia of Chronic Disease} Anemia of inflammation. Normocytic or microcytic. Low iron, low TIBC, normal/high
ferritin.

\medterm{Anencephaly} A fatal neural tube defect where the cerebral hemispheres and cranial vault fail to
develop due to failed closure of the rostral neuropore during the fourth week of
gestation. The brainstem and spinal cord are present, and reflex movements may occur,
but there is no cognitive function.

\medterm{Anergy} State of T-lymphocyte non-responsiveness to nominal antigen despite prior sensitization,
reflecting functional inactivation rather than cell death.

\medterm{Anesthesia} The use of medications to prevent sensation, particularly pain, during medical
procedures or surgery.

\medterm{Anesthesia for Coagulopathy} Coagulopathy can increase bleeding risk during an operation or invasive procedure. Anesthetic planning
considers the cause, bleeding history, platelet count, coagulation tests, fibrinogen,
the procedure, and available correction or replacement therapies.

\medterm{Anesthesia for Epilepsy} Patients with epilepsy generally require continuation of antiseizure medication
perioperatively because missed doses increase seizure risk. Propofol and benzodiazepines
are anticonvulsant; high concentrations of some volatile agents may produce epileptiform
activity.

\medterm{Anesthesia for Hemophilia} Hemophilia A results from factor VIII deficiency and hemophilia B from factor IX
deficiency. Perioperative care is coordinated with hematology and uses factor
replacement or, in selected mild hemophilia A cases, desmopressin.

\medterm{Anesthesia for Neurosurgery} Neuroanesthesia aims to maintain cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP = mean arterial
pressure minus intracranial pressure), optimize brain relaxation, and minimize secondary
brain injury. Induction agents (propofol, etomidate) reduce cerebral metabolic rate and
intracranial pressure.

\medterm{Anesthesia for Pheochromocytoma} Anesthetic care for pheochromocytoma requires control of catecholamine effects before surgery,
careful management of blood pressure and heart rate, and preparation for rapid hemodynamic changes.

\medterm{Anesthesia for Porphyria} Anesthetic care in acute porphyria requires avoidance of medicines known to precipitate attacks and attention to
nutrition, pain, and metabolic stability. The specific drug plan should be checked against a current porphyria-safety reference.

\medterm{Anesthesia Gas Analyzers} Anesthesia gas analyzers measure delivered and exhaled concentrations of oxygen, carbon
dioxide, and volatile anesthetic agents. The oxygen analyzer measures inspired oxygen
concentration; pulse oximetry measures peripheral hemoglobin oxygen saturation.

\medterm{Anesthesia Informatics} Anesthesia informatics integrates electronic health records, decision support systems,
and data analytics into clinical practice. Automated documentation reduces charting
time. Clinical decision support alerts for drug interactions and dosing errors. Big data
analytics identify patterns in anesthesia outcomes.

\medterm{Anesthesia Machine} The anesthesia machine delivers precise mixtures of oxygen, air, and volatile
anesthetics to the patient.

\medterm{Anesthesia Monitoring} Continuous assessment of vital functions during anesthesia, typically including oxygenation, ventilation, circulation,
temperature, and depth of anesthesia as clinically indicated.

\medterm{Anesthesia Pharmacogenomics} Anesthesia pharmacogenomics studies genetic variations affecting drug response.
Pseudocholinesterase deficiency causes prolonged succinylcholine paralysis. CYP2D6
polymorphisms affect codeine metabolism to morphine. Malignant hyperthermia
susceptibility is linked to RYR1 gene mutations.

\medterm{Aneuploidy} The presence of an abnormal number of chromosomes in a cell, with either extra or missing copies of whole chromosomes, such as trisomy 21. It results from errors in cell division and is a major cause of miscarriage, congenital syndromes, and chromosomal disorders identified by prenatal testing.

\medterm{Aneurysm} A localized weakening and enlargement of a blood vessel. Aneurysms may occur in arteries or, rarely, veins and may rupture,
dissect, or form emboli depending on their location and cause.

\medterm{Aneurysm Clip} Surgical clip occluding aneurysm neck. Materials: titanium, stainless steel.
Indications: intracranial aneurysm. Placed via craniotomy. Complications: clip
migration, vessel occlusion, rebleeding.

\medterm{Aneurysm Coils} Flexible coils, often made of platinum, placed inside an aneurysm to promote clotting and reduce blood flow into the aneurysm.

\medterm{Aneurysm Repair} Surgical or catheter-based treatment that excludes or replaces a weakened, dilated section of a blood vessel when the risk of rupture or other complications justifies intervention.

\medterm{Angelman Syndrome} A genetic disorder caused by loss of function of the UBE3A gene on chromosome 15
(maternal deletion or paternal uniparental disomy). Features: severe intellectual
disability, absent speech, seizures, ataxia, and frequent laughter. Characteristic happy
demeanor with hand-flapping movements.

\medterm{Angina Pectoris} Chest discomfort caused by myocardial ischemia without infarction. Stable angina occurs
predictably with exertion and resolves with rest. Unstable angina occurs at rest or with
minimal exertion and is an acute coronary syndrome. Prinzmetal (variant) angina occurs
at rest due to coronary vasospasm.

\medterm{Angiodysplasia} Dilated, fragile blood vessels in the gastrointestinal mucosa or submucosa, often occurring in the right colon of older
adults. They may cause intermittent or recurrent gastrointestinal bleeding and anemia.

\medterm{Angioedema} Rapid swelling of the deep dermis/subcutaneous and submucosal tissues (lips, eyelids,
tongue, throat, hands, GI tract) due to mast cell mediator release
(histamine—allergic/idiopathic, urticaria associated) or bradykinin-mediated (hereditary
angioedema—C1 esterase inhibitor deficiency; ACE-inhibitor-induced).

\medterm{Angiogenesis} Formation of new blood vessels. Tumors induce angiogenesis for growth. Targeted by
anti-angiogenic drugs (bevacizumab).

\medterm{Angiography} Invasive imaging of blood vessels by injecting contrast material under fluoroscopy.
Coronary angiography (cardiac catheterization): assesses coronary artery stenosis,
guides PCI. Cerebral angiography: evaluates aneurysms, AVMs, and vasculitis. Peripheral
angiography: evaluates PAD and guides intervention.

\medterm{Angiomyolipoma} A benign renal tumor composed of blood vessels, smooth muscle, and fat; it is associated
with tuberous sclerosis and may cause retroperitoneal hemorrhage.

\medterm{Angioplasty} A procedure that widens a narrowed blood vessel, usually with a balloon and sometimes a stent. It is used most often for
coronary or peripheral arterial disease.

\medterm{Angioplasty Balloon (Vascular)} An inflatable catheter balloon used to widen a narrowed blood vessel or expand a stent.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Angiosarcoma} A rare, aggressive malignant tumor of blood vessel or lymphatic endothelial cells.
Common sites include skin, breast, liver, and bone. Treatment typically involves wide
surgical excision with chemotherapy and radiation.

\medterm{Angiotensin converting-enzyme inhibitor} A class of antihypertensive medications that block the conversion of angiotensin I to
angiotensin II by inhibiting the angiotensin-converting enzyme, thereby reducing
vasoconstriction and aldosterone secretion. Used to treat hypertension, heart failure,
and diabetic nephropathy.

\medterm{Angiotensin Receptor Blockers} A class of cardiovascular drugs that selectively block the angiotensin II type 1
receptor, preventing the binding of angiotensin II and its effects including
vasoconstriction, aldosterone secretion, and sympathetic activation.

\medterm{Anhedonia} The inability to experience pleasure from activities that were previously enjoyable. It
is a core symptom of major depressive disorder and a negative symptom of schizophrenia.
It is mediated by dysfunction in the brain's reward circuitry, particularly the
mesolimbic dopamine system.

\medterm{Anhidrosis} Absent or greatly reduced sweating. It may result from nerve damage, skin disease, dehydration, medications,
or inherited conditions. Inability to sweat can cause dangerous overheating, especially during
exercise or hot weather.

\medterm{Animal-Assisted Therapy} A structured intervention in which trained animals are incorporated into care to support defined therapeutic
goals. It may be used to support engagement, mood, communication, or activity when
appropriate safeguards are in place.

\medterm{Anion Gap} A calculated estimate of unmeasured serum anions, commonly sodium minus chloride and
bicarbonate. An increased anion gap supports the presence of unmeasured acids in
metabolic acidosis, but interpretation must account for albumin concentration and
laboratory method.

\medterm{Anisocoria} Unequal pupil size. It may be physiologic or caused by disorders of the eye, autonomic nerves, or brain; sudden anisocoria
with headache, eye pain, or neurologic symptoms requires urgent assessment.

\medterm{Anisocytosis} Increased variation in red blood cell size on a peripheral blood film (e.g., macrocytes
with microcytes together), not a diagnosis in itself. Derived from the Greek
"anisokytosis" (unequal cells). Quantified by an elevated red cell distribution width
(RDW).

\medterm{Ankle Arthroplasty} Surgical replacement of the ankle joint with a prosthesis, usually for severe symptomatic
ankle arthritis. Suitability depends on deformity, bone quality, infection risk, activity level, and the condition
of the surrounding joints.

\medterm{Ankle Dislocation} Complete displacement of the ankle joint surfaces, usually resulting from a high-energy injury and often
accompanied by a fracture, ligament damage, or neurovascular compromise. It causes severe pain, deformity, and
inability to bear weight. The joint requires urgent reduction, imaging, and immobilization, with surgical management
when instability or associated fractures demand it.

\medterm{Ankle Fractures} Ankle fractures: Weber classification (fibular fracture relative to syndesmosis): A
(below syndesmosis, stable), B (at syndesmosis, variable stability), C (above
syndesmosis, unstable). Lauge-Hansen: supination-external rotation (SER, most common),
supination-adduction, pronation-external rotation, pronation-abduction.

\medterm{Ankle Joint} Uniaxial hinge synovial joint between distal tibia, lateral malleolus, and superior
talus, allowing dorsiflexion/plantarflexion. Medial (deltoid) ligament resists eversion;
lateral complex resists inversion. The ankle mortise provides bony stability.

\medterm{Ankle Pain} Pain in or around the ankle, commonly caused by sprain, fracture, tendinitis, arthritis,
bursitis, or infection. Inability to bear weight, deformity, marked swelling, or fever
requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Ankle Sprain} An injury to the ligaments surrounding the ankle joint, most commonly involving the
lateral ligament complex (anterior talofibular ligament [ATFL, most commonly injured],
calcaneofibular ligament [CFL], and posterior talofibular ligament [PTFL]) from an
inversion mechanism.

\medterm{Ankle Swelling} Enlargement around the ankle caused by injury, inflammation, infection, venous disease, lymphatic problems, or fluid retention.
One-sided sudden swelling with pain, warmth, or shortness of breath may indicate a blood clot or infection and
requires urgent assessment; swelling after trauma may require evaluation for fracture.

\medterm{Ankle-Brachial Index} The ratio of ankle systolic blood pressure to brachial systolic blood pressure. A low result supports peripheral
arterial disease, while a very high result may indicate noncompressible arteries.

\medterm{Ankyloglossia (Tongue-Tie)} Short or thick lingual frenulum restricting tongue movement. May cause breastfeeding
difficulties, speech problems, oral hygiene issues. Treatment: frenotomy (simple
release) or frenuloplasty.

\synonyms
Tongue-Tie

\medterm{Ankylosing Spondylitis} A chronic, progressive inflammatory disease primarily affecting the axial skeleton
(sacroiliac joints and spine), characterized by inflammation at entheses (where tendons,
ligaments, and joint capsules insert into bone), leading to new bone formation,
syndesmophyte development, and eventual vertebral fusion (bamboo spine).

\medterm{Ankylosis} Abnormal stiffness or fusion of a joint. In dentistry, it may refer to fusion of a tooth root to the surrounding bone.

\medterm{Annihilation} Positron-electron annihilation producing two 511 keV photons in opposite directions.

\medterm{Annular Pancreas} A congenital anomaly where a ring of pancreatic tissue completely or partially encircles
the second portion of the duodenum, causing duodenal obstruction. It results from
failure of the ventral pancreatic bud to completely rotate around the duodenum during
embryogenesis.

\medterm{Anodontia} Complete absence of teeth. Rare, associated with ectodermal dysplasia.

\medterm{Anophthalmia} Congenital absence of the eye due to failure of optic vesicle development, often
associated with syndromes (e.g., SOX2 mutations). Diagnosis by absence of globe on
imaging or clinical examination; management requires early socket expansion and
prosthetic fitting for orbital and facial development.

\medterm{Anorectal Abscess} A localized collection of pus in the tissues around the anus or rectum, usually caused
by infection of an anal gland. It commonly causes severe anal pain, swelling, fever, and
difficulty sitting. Prompt incision and drainage is the standard treatment.

\medterm{Anorectal Malformation} A congenital abnormality in which the anus or rectum does not develop normally or does not connect appropriately
to the perineum. Types vary in anatomy and severity and usually require specialist surgical assessment.

\medterm{Anorectal Manometry} Measurement of pressures in the anal canal and rectum to assess sphincter function and
rectal sensation. A pressure-sensing catheter is inserted into the rectum and slowly
withdrawn through the anal canal. Measures resting and squeeze pressures reflecting
internal and external sphincter function respectively.

\medterm{Anorexia} Loss of appetite, distinct from anorexia nervosa (the eating disorder).

\medterm{Anorexia Nervosa} An eating disorder characterized by restriction of energy intake, intense fear of weight
gain, and distorted body image. Highest mortality of any psychiatric disorder. Features:
emaciation, lanugo, bradycardia, hypotension, amenorrhea, and electrolyte abnormalities.
Diagnosis by DSM-5 criteria.

\medterm{Anorgasmia} Persistent or recurrent delay in, or absence of, orgasm following a normal sexual
excitement phase.

\medterm{Anoscopy} Examination of the anal canal with a short lighted instrument called an anoscope. It is
used to evaluate rectal bleeding, hemorrhoids, fissures, fistulas, and other anorectal
disorders.

\medterm{Anosmia} Complete loss of the sense of smell. Causes include post-viral olfactory dysfunction,
chronic rhinosinusitis with polyps, head trauma, neurodegenerative disease, congenital
disorders such as Kallmann syndrome, medications, and toxic exposure.

\medterm{Anosognosia} Denial or unawareness of one's own neurologic deficit, classically hemiplegia following
a nondominant (right) parietal lobe lesion. Associated with right middle cerebral artery
strokes and sometimes with neglect of the left side of space.

\medterm{Anovulation} Failure to release an egg from the ovary during the menstrual cycle. It may cause irregular or absent periods,
infertility, or abnormal bleeding and can result from hormonal disorders, excessive exercise,
low body weight, stress, or polycystic ovary syndrome.

\medterm{Anoxia} Complete absence of oxygen supply to tissues, distinguished from hypoxia (reduced
oxygen). Anoxia causes rapid cell death, particularly in highly aerobic tissues (brain,
heart, kidneys).

\medterm{Antacids} Medications that neutralise gastric acid, providing rapid symptomatic relief in
dyspepsia, gastro-esophageal reflux, and peptic ulcer disease. Commonly contain
aluminium hydroxide, magnesium hydroxide, calcium carbonate, or sodium bicarbonate.

\medterm{Antecubital Fossa} Triangular depression on the anterior elbow, bounded laterally by brachioradialis,
medially by pronator teres, and superiorly by a line connecting the epicondyles. Floor
formed by brachialis and supinator. Contains biceps tendon, brachial artery, and median
nerve. Primary site for venipuncture and blood pressure measurement.

\medterm{Antemortem Injury} Injury inflicted upon a body prior to death, characterized microscopically and
macroscopically by physiological tissue responses including hemorrhage, blood clotting,
inflammatory cell infiltration, tissue edema, and active healing reactions
(differentiating it from postmortem artifact).

\medterm{Antenatal} The period before birth, also called prenatal. The medical supervision and screening
provided to pregnant women to monitor the health of the mother and fetus, detect
complications early, and provide health education.

\synonyms
prenatal.

\medterm{Antepartum Fetal Surveillance} The monitoring of fetal well-being in the third trimester for high-risk pregnancies.
Methods: fetal kick counts (daily maternal counting of fetal movements), non-stress test
(NST), biophysical profile (BPP, AFI, fetal tone, movement, breathing), and Doppler
(umbilical artery, MCA).

\medterm{Antepartum Hemorrhage} Bleeding from the genital tract after the threshold of fetal viability and before birth.
Important causes include placenta previa, placental abruption, vasa previa, and local
cervical or vaginal lesions; maternal stabilization and fetal assessment are priorities.

\medterm{Anterior} Relating to the front of the body or positioned closer to the front.

\medterm{Anterior Chamber Angle} The anterior chamber angle is the anatomical region where the cornea and iris meet, and
it contains the trabecular meshwork, the primary drainage structure for aqueous humor
from the eye.

\medterm{Anterior Cruciate Ligament Injury} Tear of the ACL, a common sports injury (non-contact deceleration, pivoting, or
hyperextension). Presents with a pop, immediate swelling (hemarthrosis), and giving way
of the knee. Positive Lachman test (most sensitive), anterior drawer test, and pivot
shift test.

\medterm{Anterior Cruciate Ligament Tear} ACL tear: common knee injury, often non-contact pivoting sports. Symptoms: popping
sound, swelling, instability, inability to continue play. Diagnosis: Lachman test,
anterior drawer, MRI. Treatment: surgical reconstruction (autograft/allograft),
rehabilitation.

\medterm{Anterior Pituitary} The glandular portion of the pituitary that secretes ACTH, TSH, growth hormone,
prolactin, FSH, and LH in response to hypothalamic releasing hormones. Its hormones
regulate adrenal, thyroid, growth, lactation, and reproductive function.

\medterm{Anterior Tibial Artery} Arises from the popliteal artery, passes through the anterior intermuscular septum to
the anterior compartment, continuing to the ankle as the dorsalis pedis artery. Supplies
anterior leg and dorsal foot muscles. Palpable anterior to the lateral malleolus as the
dorsalis pedis pulse.

\medterm{Anterior Uveitis} Anterior uveitis, also known as iritis or iridocyclitis when the ciliary body is
involved, is inflammation of the anterior segment of the eye involving the iris and/or
ciliary body, and it is the most common form of uveitis.

\synonyms
iritis or iridocyclitis when the ciliary body is.

\medterm{Anterior/Posterior Drawer Test} A clinical test of knee stability. Anterior displacement assesses the anterior cruciate ligament, and posterior
displacement assesses the posterior cruciate ligament.

\medterm{Anthrax} A serious infectious disease caused by the gram-positive bacterium Bacillus anthracis,
which forms highly resistant spores that can persist in the environment for decades, and
it is primarily a zoonotic disease of herbivores (cattle, sheep, goats) that can be
transmitted to humans through contact with infected animals or their products.

\medterm{Anthropometry} Body measurements: weight, height, BMI, waist, hip, skinfold thickness. Assessment of
nutritional status and body composition.

\medterm{Anti-CCP} An autoantibody against citrullinated proteins. It supports the diagnosis of rheumatoid arthritis and
may be associated with a more erosive disease course, but it must be interpreted with the clinical findings.
