\medterm{Anti-CCP Antibody} A blood antibody directed against citrullinated proteins. It can support assessment of
rheumatoid arthritis and may be detectable before joint symptoms, but a positive or negative result is not diagnostic by
itself.

\medterm{Anti-citrullinated peptide antibody} An autoantibody against citrullinated proteins that may support a diagnosis
of rheumatoid arthritis. Its significance depends on the clinical context.

\medterm{Anti-dsDNA Antibody} An autoantibody directed against double-stranded DNA. It supports the diagnosis of
systemic lupus erythematosus and may be associated with disease activity, particularly kidney involvement; results are
interpreted with other findings.

\medterm{Anti-GBM Disease} Autoimmune disease with antibodies against alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in
glomerular and alveolar basement membranes. Presents with crescentic GN and pulmonary
hemorrhage (Goodpasture syndrome). Linear IgG deposits on immunofluorescence. Treatment
requires emergent plasmapheresis, cyclophosphamide, and corticosteroids.

\medterm{Anti-IgE Therapy} Omalizumab is a monoclonal antibody that binds free IgE, preventing it from binding to
mast cells and basophils. It reduces allergic inflammation and is approved for
moderate-to-severe persistent allergic asthma. It reduces exacerbations and the need for
oral corticosteroids. It is administered by subcutaneous injection every 2--4 weeks.

\medterm{Anti-IL4 Receptor Therapy} Dupilumab is a monoclonal antibody blocking the IL-4 receptor alpha subunit, inhibiting
both IL-4 and IL-13 signaling. It is approved for moderate-to-severe asthma with
eosinophilic phenotype or oral corticosteroid dependence. It reduces exacerbations and
improves lung function.

\medterm{Anti-IL5 Therapy} Treatment with monoclonal antibodies that block interleukin-5 signaling or its receptor,
thereby reducing eosinophil production or survival. It is used for selected eosinophilic diseases, including some
forms of severe asthma.

\medterm{Anti-inflammatory} A medicine or other treatment that reduces inflammation.

\medterm{Anti-MDA5 dermatomyositis} A clinically distinct subtype of dermatomyositis associated with anti-melanoma
differentiation-associated gene 5 antibodies, characterised by skin-predominant disease,
mechanic's hands, and a high risk of rapidly progressive interstitial lung disease.

\medterm{Anti-Mullerian Hormone} A hormone produced by granulosa cells of ovarian follicles. It is measured in selected
fertility and reproductive evaluations, but it does not by itself determine fertility.

\medterm{Anti-Reflux Surgery} An operation intended to reduce reflux of stomach contents into the esophagus, usually by
reinforcing the gastroesophageal junction. It may be considered for selected patients
with gastroesophageal reflux disease or a hiatal hernia.

\medterm{Antianginal} A class of medications used to prevent or relieve angina pectoris (chest pain from
myocardial ischemia).

\medterm{Antiarrhythmic} A drug used to treat or prevent abnormal cardiac rhythms. Drug classes act mainly on sodium
channels, sympathetic stimulation, potassium channels, or calcium channels; selection
depends on the rhythm and the patient's clinical condition.

\medterm{Antibiogram} A periodic summary of antimicrobial susceptibility patterns of bacterial isolates from a
hospital or laboratory. Guides empiric antibiotic therapy by showing local resistance
patterns (e.g., \% of E. coli susceptible to TMP-SMX). Updates annually.

\medterm{Antibiotic} A substance that kills or slows the growth of bacteria.

\medterm{Antibiotic cycling} A strategy in which the preferred antibiotic for a particular clinical use is changed at
planned intervals. It is intended to limit selection of resistant organisms, although its
benefit depends on local conditions and implementation.

\medterm{Antibiotic de-escalation} The process of narrowing antimicrobial spectrum based on microbiological results and
clinical response. De-escalation aims to reduce selective pressure for resistance while
maintaining therapeutic efficacy.

\medterm{Antibiotic mixing} A strategy in which different antibiotics are assigned concurrently to different patients
with the same indication. It is intended to reduce uniform selection pressure for
resistance in a patient population.

\medterm{Antibiotic Resistance} Bacteria resisting antibiotic effects. Mechanisms: enzymatic degradation, target
modification, efflux pumps, membrane permeability. Driven by antibiotic overuse.

\medterm{Antibiotic resistance genes} Genes that encode mechanisms conferring resistance to antibiotics. These genes can be
located on chromosomes, plasmids, or transposons. Major resistance genes include mecA
(methicillin resistance in S. aureus), vanA/vanB (vancomycin resistance in
Enterococcus), and blaKPC (carbapenem resistance).

\medterm{Antibiotic Stewardship} Coordinated efforts to use antibiotics appropriately by selecting the right medicine, dose, route, and duration while reducing unnecessary treatment and resistance.

\medterm{Antibodies} Immunoglobulin proteins produced by B cells that bind to specific antigens. There are
five classes: IgG (most abundant), IgM (first produced), IgA (mucosal immunity), IgE
(allergic responses), and IgD. Antibodies neutralise pathogens, opsonise for
phagocytosis, activate complement, and mediate antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity.

\medterm{Anticholinergic Burden} The cumulative effect of taking multiple medications with anticholinergic properties. It
can cause dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, blurred vision, confusion, and
delirium, especially in elderly patients. Many psychiatric medications (TCAs,
first-generation antipsychotics, some antihistamines) have anticholinergic effects.

\medterm{Anticholinergic Cognitive Burden Scale} A medication-review tool that estimates the total anticholinergic load of a patient's
medicines. It supports risk assessment and deprescribing but does not replace
individualized clinical judgment.

\medterm{Anticoagulant Monitoring} Laboratory assessment used to measure the effect or safety of an anticoagulant. The
appropriate test depends on the drug and may include aPTT, anti-Xa activity, INR, or
measurements of kidney function and blood counts.

\medterm{Anticoagulation} Treatment that reduces blood clot formation by inhibiting the coagulation system. It is used
for conditions such as venous thromboembolism and selected cardiac or vascular disorders.

\medterm{Anticodon} A three-nucleotide sequence at one end of transfer RNA that base-pairs with a
complementary messenger RNA codon during translation, ensuring the correct amino acid is
incorporated into the growing polypeptide.

\medterm{Anticonvulsant Mood Stabilizers} Antiepileptic drugs used as mood stabilizers in bipolar disorder. Valproate (divalproex)
is effective for acute mania and rapid cycling. Carbamazepine is effective for acute
mania but has numerous drug interactions via CYP3A4 induction. Lamotrigine is effective
for bipolar depression prevention but not acute mania.

\medterm{Anticonvulsants} Medications used to prevent or control seizures in epilepsy and other seizure disorders.

\medterm{Antidiabetic} A class of medications used to manage diabetes mellitus by lowering blood glucose levels
through various mechanisms.

\medterm{Antidiarrheal} A drug that reduces the frequency or volume of diarrhea. Some antidiarrheals should be avoided
when there is bloody diarrhea, high fever, suspected invasive infection, or ileus.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone} A hypothalamic hormone released from the posterior pituitary in response to increased
plasma osmolality or reduced effective circulating volume. It increases collecting-duct
water permeability through V2 receptors and promotes vasoconstriction through V1
receptors.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone Physiology} Antidiuretic hormone also known as vasopressin is synthesized in the supraoptic and
paraventricular nuclei of the hypothalamus and stored in the posterior pituitary.
Released in response to increased serum osmolality detected by hypothalamic
osmoreceptors and decreased blood volume detected by baroreceptors.

\synonyms
vasopressin is synthesized in the supraoptic and.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone Resistance} Impaired renal response to vasopressin, causing excessive production of dilute urine and
polydipsia. Causes include lithium, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, chronic kidney disease,
and inherited defects of the vasopressin receptor or aquaporin pathway.

\medterm{Antidote} Substance that counteracts poison toxicity. Specific: naloxone (opioids), flumazenil
(benzodiazepines), N-acetylcysteine (acetaminophen), digoxin immune Fab (digoxin),
chelators (EDTA, deferoxamine, dimercaprol), atropine + pralidoxime (organophosphates),
methylene blue (methemoglobinemia), cyanide antidote kit (nitrite + thiosulfate).

\medterm{Antiemetic} A medication that prevents or treats nausea and vomiting.

\medterm{Antiepileptic Drugs} Medications used to prevent and control seizures by modulating neuronal excitability
through various mechanisms. They can be classified by their primary mechanism of action.

\medterm{Antifungal} A drug used to treat fungal infections.

\medterm{Antifungal drug classes} Major classes of antifungal agents include polyenes (amphotericin B), azoles
(fluconazole, voriconazole), echinocandins (caspofungin, micafungin), and allylamines
(terbinafine). Polyenes bind to ergosterol in fungal cell membranes. Azoles inhibit
ergosterol synthesis. Echinocandins inhibit cell wall synthesis.

\medterm{Antifungal prophylaxis} The use of antifungal agents to prevent invasive fungal infections in high-risk
patients. Common indications include prolonged neutropenia, hematopoietic stem cell
transplantation, and advanced HIV infection. Fluconazole, posaconazole, and micafungin
are used for prophylaxis.

\medterm{Antifungal resistance} The ability of fungi to withstand the effects of antifungal agents. Resistance can be
intrinsic or acquired. Azole resistance occurs through efflux pumps and target
mutations. Echinocandin resistance occurs through FKS gene mutations. Amphotericin B
resistance is rare.

\medterm{Antigen} A molecule or molecular structure specifically recognized by an antibody, B-cell
receptor, or T-cell receptor. An immunogen is an antigen capable of inducing an immune
response; antigenicity and immunogenicity are related but not synonymous properties.

\medterm{Antigen Detection Test} A diagnostic assay that identifies a pathogen-derived antigen in a clinical specimen
using a specific antibody or related binding reagent. It is generally rapid but may be
less sensitive than nucleic-acid amplification, especially when organism burden is low.

\medterm{Antigen-Presenting Cell} Specialized immune cells that process protein antigens and display peptide fragments
bound to Major Histocompatibility Complex (MHC) molecules on their surface for
recognition by T-cell receptors. Professional APCs: dendritic cells (most potent for
naive T cells), macrophages, and B cells.

\medterm{Antigenic Drift} The gradual accumulation of point mutations in viral surface antigens (hemagglutinin,
neuraminidase) during replication, allowing influenza viruses to evade pre-existing
antibodies and cause recurrent seasonal epidemics. Drift is continuous and selected by
host immunity; it is why the seasonal vaccine is updated annually.

\medterm{Antigenic Shift} A major, abrupt change in the antigenic structure of a virus (especially influenza A)
resulting from genetic reassortment between different strains (e.g., human and avian
viruses) infecting the same cell.

\medterm{Antigenic variation} The process by which pathogens change their surface proteins to evade immune
recognition. Antigenic drift: gradual changes in surface proteins. Antigenic shift:
major changes through reassortment of genome segments. Influenza virus demonstrates both
mechanisms. Trypanosomes and Plasmodium also use antigenic variation.

\medterm{Antigout} A class of medications used to treat gout by reducing uric acid levels or controlling
acute inflammation.

\medterm{Antihistamine} A drug that blocks histamine receptors. H1 antihistamines treat allergic rhinitis,
urticaria, pruritus, and (older sedating agents) insomnia and motion sickness.
First-generation (diphenhydramine, chlorpheniramine): CNS-penetrant—sedating,
anticholinergic (dry mouth, urinary retention, confusion in elderly).

\medterm{Antihypertensive} A medicine used to lower blood pressure. Classes include ACE inhibitors, angiotensin-receptor
blockers, calcium-channel blockers, thiazide-type diuretics, beta-blockers, and other
agents. Selection depends on blood pressure, kidney function, cardiovascular disease,
pregnancy, and other clinical factors.

\medterm{Antihypertensives in Pregnancy} Medications used to treat hypertension in pregnancy.

\medterm{Antimicrobial} A general term for antibiotics and other drugs that fight microscopic organisms in the
body, such as bacteria, viruses, fungi, and parasites.

\medterm{Antimicrobial adverse effects} Side effects associated with antimicrobial agents. Beta-lactams can cause allergic
reactions. Fluoroquinolones can cause tendon rupture and QT prolongation.
Aminoglycosides can cause nephrotoxicity and ototoxicity. Azole antifungals can cause
hepatotoxicity.

\medterm{Antimicrobial desensitization} A procedure that administers gradually increasing doses of an antimicrobial to
temporarily induce tolerance in a person with a documented or strongly suspected immediate allergy. It may be considered
when that antimicrobial is clinically necessary and suitable alternatives are not available.

\medterm{Antimicrobial dosing optimization} Strategies to ensure antimicrobial agents achieve therapeutic concentrations at the site
of infection. Optimization includes appropriate drug selection, dosing interval, route
of administration, and duration of therapy. Extended or continuous infusion of
beta-lactams may improve outcomes. Weight-based dosing is important for many agents.

\medterm{Antimicrobial drug interactions} Interactions between antimicrobial agents and other medications. Fluoroquinolones chelate divalent cations. Macrolides inhibit CYP3A4. Rifampin is a potent enzyme inducer. Azole antifungals inhibit CYP3A4.

\medterm{Antimicrobial hypersensitivity reactions} Immune-mediated reactions to antimicrobial agents that may range from a mild rash to
life-threatening anaphylaxis. The reaction depends on the drug, the patient, and the
type of immune response.

\medterm{Antimicrobial pharmacodynamics} The relationship between antimicrobial drug concentrations and their effect on
microorganisms. Time-dependent antibiotics (beta-lactams) are most effective when
concentrations remain above the MIC. Concentration-dependent antibiotics
(aminoglycosides) are most effective with high peak concentrations.

\medterm{Antimicrobial pharmacokinetics} The absorption, distribution, metabolism, and elimination of antimicrobial agents.
Pharmacokinetics varies by drug class, patient factors, and disease state. Renal
impairment affects elimination of many antibiotics. Hepatic impairment affects
metabolism of many antifungals.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Resistance} Microorganisms becoming resistant to antibiotics. Major global health threat.
Prevention: appropriate antibiotic use, infection control.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Resistance Mechanisms} Biological processes that allow microorganisms to survive antimicrobial exposure, including
drug inactivation, modification of the drug target, reduced permeability, and active
drug efflux.

\medterm{Antimicrobial resistance surveillance} The systematic monitoring of antimicrobial resistance patterns in clinical isolates.
Surveillance programmes track resistance rates over time and guide empiric therapy.
National and international networks share resistance data.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Stewardship} Coordinated actions that promote appropriate antimicrobial selection, dose, route, and duration
to improve patient outcomes and limit resistance, adverse effects, and unnecessary cost.

\medterm{Antimicrobial susceptibility testing} Laboratory methods used to estimate how susceptible a microorganism is to antimicrobial
agents. Methods include disk diffusion, broth microdilution, and automated systems. Results
are interpreted using standardized breakpoints and can help guide antimicrobial therapy.

\medterm{Antineoplastic} A medicine or other treatment that inhibits the growth or survival of abnormal cells, especially cancer cells.

\medterm{Antinuclear Antibody} ANA: autoantibodies against nuclear components (DNA, histones, ribonucleoproteins,
etc.), detected by immunofluorescence (HEp-2 cells), reported with pattern (homogeneous,
speckled, nucleolar, centromere) and titer.

\medterm{Antioxidant} A substance that limits oxidation by reacting with oxidizing molecules or free radicals.

\medterm{Antiparasitic} A drug used to treat infections caused by parasites (protozoa and helminths).
Antiprotozoals: metronidazole (Giardia, amebiasis, trichomoniasis), tinidazole,
chloroquine and artemisinin combinations (malaria), pyrimethamine (toxoplasmosis),
pentamidine, and nifurtimox (Chagas).

\medterm{Antiparkinson} A class of medications used to manage Parkinson disease symptoms by restoring
dopaminergic activity or modulating other neurotransmitter systems.

\medterm{Antiparkinsonian} A drug used to treat Parkinson disease and drug-induced parkinsonism.

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Antibodies} Include lupus anticoagulant (prolongs phospholipid-dependent coagulation tests),
anticardiolipin antibodies (IgG/IgM), and anti-beta-2-glycoprotein I antibodies.
Detected on >=2 occasions 12 weeks apart. Clinical criteria: vascular thrombosis or
pregnancy morbidity. Can be transient (infection, drugs) or persistent.

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Antibody} An autoantibody directed against phospholipid-binding proteins. Persistent antibodies may be associated with thrombosis or pregnancy complications, particularly in antiphospholipid syndrome.

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Syndrome} A systemic autoimmune thrombophilia characterized by arterial and venous thrombosis,
pregnancy morbidity, and persistent antiphospholipid antibody positivity.

\medterm{Antiplatelet Therapy} Treatment with medicines that reduce platelet activation or aggregation, such as aspirin or clopidogrel, when clinically indicated to reduce arterial clotting.

\medterm{Antipruritic} A medication that relieves itching (pruritus).

\medterm{Antipsychotic} A drug used to treat psychotic disorders (schizophrenia, schizoaffective disorder,
psychosis in bipolar/manic states and delirium). Typical (first-generation):
haloperidol, chlorpromazine—D2 blockade, high EPS/tardive dyskinesia risk,
hyperprolactinemia.

\medterm{Antipsychotic-Induced Parkinsonism} Drug-induced bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, or postural instability caused by
dopamine-receptor blockade, usually from antipsychotic treatment. Management involves
reviewing the medication, dose, alternatives, and symptomatic treatment when
appropriate.

\medterm{Antipyretic} A drug that reduces fever by lowering the hypothalamic set point, typically by
inhibiting prostaglandin synthesis in the preoptic hypothalamus. Acetaminophen and
NSAIDs (ibuprofen, aspirin) are the principal antipyretics.

\medterm{Antiretroviral therapy} Treatment of HIV infection with combinations of antiretroviral agents. ART targets
multiple steps in the viral life cycle including entry, reverse transcription,
integration, and maturation. Combination ART suppresses viral replication and restores
immune function. ART is lifelong therapy.

\medterm{Antiseptic} Substances used on wounds to prevent or treat infection; they kill or slow the growth of
diseasecausing organisms, such as bacteria, on the surface of the body.

\medterm{Antisocial Personality Disorder} A pattern of disregard for and violation of the rights of others occurring since age 15
years. At least three of seven criteria: failure to conform to social norms,
deceitfulness, impulsivity, irritability and aggressiveness, reckless disregard for
safety, consistent irresponsibility, and lack of remorse.

\medterm{Antispasmodic} A medication that reduces smooth muscle spasm, primarily in the gastrointestinal tract.
Anticholinergic agents: hyoscyamine, dicyclomine, atropine — block muscarinic M3
receptors on GI smooth muscle, reducing contractions and peristalsis, used for irritable
bowel syndrome and functional dyspepsia.

\medterm{Antistreptolysin O Titer} A blood test that measures antibodies against streptolysin O, a toxin produced by group A streptococci. Rising or elevated titers support recent streptococcal infection and help evaluate suspected rheumatic fever or poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis, although a single value does not prove active infection.

\medterm{Antisynthetase Syndrome} An autoimmune disorder associated with antibodies against aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, especially anti-Jo-1.
It may cause interstitial lung disease, inflammatory muscle disease, arthritis, fever,
Raynaud phenomenon, or mechanic's hands.

\medterm{Antithrombin} Natural anticoagulant. Inhibits thrombin, factor Xa. Deficiency causes thrombosis.

\medterm{Antithrombin Deficiency} A rare (1 in 2,000-5,000), strongly thrombophilic inherited disorder (autosomal
dominant). Antithrombin (previously antithrombin III) is a serine protease inhibitor
that inactivates thrombin and factors Xa, IXa, XIa, and XIIa. Heterozygous: 10-50 fold
increased VTE risk, more severe than factor V Leiden.

\medterm{Antithrombin III deficiency} An inherited or acquired thrombophilia caused by reduced antithrombin activity, leading
to increased risk of venous thromboembolism.

\medterm{Antithyroid} A medicine or treatment that reduces thyroid-hormone production or activity. Examples include thioamides,
radioactive iodine, and medicines that reduce the effects of thyroid hormone; the choice
depends on the cause and clinical situation.

\medterm{Antitubercular Agents} Antimicrobial drugs used to treat Mycobacterium tuberculosis infections.

\medterm{Antitussive} A medication that suppresses the cough reflex, used for dry, non-productive cough.

\medterm{Antiviral} A class of drugs that inhibit viral replication, used to treat viral infections.

\medterm{Antiviral drug classes} Major classes of antiviral agents include nucleoside analogues (acyclovir, tenofovir),
protease inhibitors (ritonavir, atazanavir), reverse transcriptase inhibitors
(efavirenz, sofosbuvir), and neuraminidase inhibitors (oseltamivir). Each class targets
a specific step in the viral replication cycle.

\medterm{Antrochoanal polyp} A benign, single nasal polyp originating from the maxillary sinus mucosa and extending
through the accessory ostium into the choana and nasopharynx. It is most common in
children and young adults, presenting with unilateral nasal obstruction and rhinorrhea.
Treatment is endoscopic surgical removal.

\medterm{Anuria} Near absence of urine production, often defined in adults as less than about 100 milliliters in 24 hours.

\medterm{Anus} The terminal opening of the gastrointestinal tract through which feces are expelled. It
is surrounded by the internal smooth-muscle sphincter and external skeletal-muscle
sphincter.

\medterm{Anxiety} A normal emotional response to perceived danger, but anxiety disorders involve
excessive, persistent worry that interferes with daily life. Symptoms can include
restlessness, rapid heart rate, muscle tension, and avoidance behaviors.

\medterm{Anxiety Disorder} A group of psychiatric conditions characterized by excessive fear, anxiety, and related
behavioral disturbances. Includes generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), panic disorder,
social anxiety disorder, specific phobias, and agoraphobia.

\medterm{Anxiolytic} A medicine or other treatment that reduces anxiety. Choice depends on the anxiety disorder, urgency, coexisting
conditions, adverse effects, and patient preference; psychological therapies and, when
appropriate, antidepressant medicines are commonly used for persistent anxiety.

\medterm{Aorta} Largest artery, from the left ventricle distributing oxygenated blood systemically.
Approximately 30 cm long, 2.5 cm diameter. Four segments: ascending aorta, aortic arch
(brachiocephalic trunk, left common carotid, left subclavian), descending thoracic
aorta, descending abdominal aorta (bifurcating into common iliacs at L4).

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm} A permanent, localized enlargement of the aorta caused by weakening of its wall. It may occur in the thoracic or
abdominal aorta and can enlarge, dissect, or rupture; management depends on its site,
size, growth, symptoms, and the patient's risk.

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm Rupture} Full-thickness failure of an aortic aneurysm with bleeding into a body cavity or
surrounding tissue. Ruptured aneurysm commonly causes sudden pain, shock, and collapse
and requires immediate resuscitation and operative or endovascular repair.

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm Screening} Evaluation of selected people for an aortic aneurysm before symptoms develop. Screening
may use abdominal ultrasonography or other imaging according to the person's risk.

\medterm{Aortic Arch} Curves from second right sternocostal joint superiorly and left, then posteriorly and
inferiorly to T4. From right to left: brachiocephalic trunk, left common carotid, left
subclavian. Related to left recurrent laryngeal nerve (hooking under it), trachea,
esophagus, left phrenic nerve. Arch aneurysms may cause hoarseness and SVC syndrome.

\medterm{Aortic Coarctation} Congenital narrowing of the aorta, usually near the insertion of the ductus arteriosus.
It causes higher blood pressure and stronger pulses in the arms than in the legs, with a
radio-femoral delay; severe neonatal disease may present with shock when the ductus
closes.

\medterm{Aortic Dissection} A tear in the inner layer of the aorta that allows blood to enter the wall and form a false channel. Stanford type A
involves the ascending aorta; type B does not. Treatment depends on the type, symptoms,
complications, and overall clinical condition.

\medterm{Aortic embolism} Obstruction of a systemic artery by material originating in the aorta, such as thrombus
or atheromatous debris, causing distal ischemia.

\medterm{Aortic Graft Infection} Infection involving a prosthetic or endovascular aortic graft, potentially causing
sepsis, pseudoaneurysm, hemorrhage, or aortoenteric fistula. Management usually requires
prolonged antimicrobial treatment and individualized surgical or endovascular source
control.

\medterm{Aortic Regurgitation} Incomplete closure of the aortic valve allowing blood to flow backward from the aorta to
the left ventricle during diastole. Causes include bicuspid aortic valve, endocarditis,
aortic root dilation, hypertension, and connective tissue disorders. Murmur: early
diastolic decrescendo murmur at the left sternal border.

\medterm{Aortic Regurgitation Murmur} Early diastolic decrescendo murmur heard best at the left sternal border with the
patient sitting up and leaning forward in end-expiration. Wide pulse pressure,
water-hammer pulses (Corrigan pulse), de Musset sign (head bobbing with each pulse), and
Duroziez sign (femoral bruit with compression).

\medterm{Aortic Stenosis} Narrowing of the aortic valve opening, obstructing left ventricular outflow. It is the
most common valvular disease in developed countries, usually caused by calcific
degeneration. Symptoms include angina, syncope, and heart failure (SAD triad).

\medterm{Aortic Stenosis Grading} Severity grading: mild (valve area >1.5 cm2, mean gradient <20 mmHg), moderate (valve
area 1.0-1.5 cm2, mean gradient 20-40 mmHg), severe (valve area <1.0 cm2, mean gradient
>40 mmHg). Very severe (valve area <0.6 cm2, mean gradient >60 mmHg) indicates critical
stenosis. Grading guides timing of intervention.

\medterm{Aortic Valve} A semilunar valve between the left ventricle and aorta, normally composed of right, left, and
noncoronary cusps. It opens during systole and closes during diastole to direct blood flow.
Each cusp forms a sinus of Valsalva from which coronary arteries originate. Opens during
systole; closes during diastole.

\medterm{Aortic Valve Disease} Abnormal narrowing, leakage, or structural change of the valve between the left ventricle and aorta. It may cause
chest pain, breathlessness, fatigue, fainting, or no symptoms initially. Evaluation commonly includes examination
and echocardiography; severe disease may require specialist treatment.

\medterm{Aortography} Contrast imaging of aorta. Evaluates aneurysm, dissection, occlusion. Largely replaced
by CTA/MRA.

\medterm{APACHE II Score} Severity scoring system for critically ill patients incorporating physiological
variables, age, and chronic health status to generate scores from zero to seventy-one.
Higher scores indicate greater severity and higher mortality. In acute pancreatitis,
scores of eight or greater indicate severe disease.

\medterm{APC Tumor Suppressor} The adenomatous polyposis coli protein is a large multidomain tumor suppressor that
functions as a critical negative regulator of the canonical Wnt signaling pathway.

\medterm{Apert Syndrome} An autosomal dominant craniosynostosis syndrome caused by FGFR2 mutations (most commonly
Ser252Trp or Pro253Arg).

\medterm{APGAR Score} A scoring system developed by Virginia Apgar in 1952 to assess the newborn's immediate
condition at 1 and 5 minutes after birth. Five components (each scored 0, 1, or 2):
Appearance (skin color), Pulse (heart rate), Grimace (reflex irritability), Activity
(muscle tone), and Respiration (breathing effort).

\medterm{Aphasia} Impairment of language comprehension or production resulting from brain damage, most
commonly from stroke affecting the left hemisphere (dominant) language areas.

\medterm{Apheresis} A procedure that removes a selected blood component, such as plasma, platelets, or
abnormal cells, and returns the remaining components to the patient or donor.

\medterm{Aphonia} Complete loss of the ability to phonate, distinct from dysphonia in which voice is
present but abnormal. Causes are organic (bilateral vocal cord paralysis, severe
laryngitis, laryngeal trauma, structural lesions) or psychogenic (conversion disorder,
muscle tension dysphonia). Whispered speech and cough are usually preserved.

\medterm{Aphthous Stomatitis} Recurrent painful ulcers of the oral mucosa with a shallow base and erythematous border, commonly
known as canker sores.

\medterm{Aphthous Ulcer} A recurrent, painful ulcer of the oral mucosa. See Canker Sore.

\medterm{Apicoectomy} Surgical removal of root tip and periapical lesion. Indication: failed root canal
therapy.

\medterm{Aplastic Anemia} A bone marrow failure syndrome characterized by pancytopenia (anemia, leukopenia,
thrombocytopenia) and a hypocellular bone marrow with replacement by fat cells,
resulting from immune-mediated destruction of hematopoietic stem cells.

\medterm{Apnea} Temporary cessation of breathing. Clinically important apnea may cause low oxygen, carbon-dioxide retention, or
bradycardia.

\medterm{Apnea of Prematurity} Recurrent pauses in breathing in a premature infant, sometimes accompanied by bradycardia
or oxygen desaturation. It may be central, obstructive, or mixed. Management includes monitoring and, when needed,
respiratory stimulation or support.

\medterm{Apnea-Hypopnea Index} Number of apneas plus hypopneas per hour of sleep. Apnea = airflow cessation > 10
seconds. Hypopnea = partial obstruction with desaturation or arousal. AHI < 5 normal,
5--14 mild, 15--29 moderate, >= 30 severe.

\medterm{Apneustic Breathing} A breathing pattern characterized by a prolonged inspiratory pause, seen in brainstem
lesions (particularly the pons). It indicates damage to the apneustic center and is a
poor prognostic sign in neurological injury. It may require mechanical ventilation.

\medterm{Apnoea} Temporary cessation of breathing, defined as complete cessation of airflow for at least 10
seconds. Obstructive apnea: airway collapse despite respiratory effort (common in
obstructive sleep apnea).

\medterm{Apoenzyme} The protein component of an enzyme without its required cofactor or coenzyme. Inactive
or minimally active until the cofactor binds. The complete, catalytically active complex
of apoenzyme + cofactor is called the holoenzyme.

\medterm{Apoptosis} Programmed cell death. Dysregulated in cancer (avoiding death). Pro-apoptotic genes:
BAX, P53. Anti-apoptotic: BCL-2.

\medterm{Apoptosis Extrinsic Pathway} The extrinsic pathway of apoptosis is triggered by extracellular ligands binding to
death receptors belonging to the tumor necrosis factor receptor superfamily, including
Fas (CD95), TNFR1, and TRAIL receptors DR4 and DR5.

\medterm{Apoptosis Intrinsic Pathway} The intrinsic, or mitochondrial, pathway of apoptosis is initiated by intracellular
stress signals including DNA damage, growth factor withdrawal, endoplasmic reticulum
stress, and hypoxia.

\medterm{Apparent Mineralocorticoid Excess} Rare autosomal recessive disorder caused by deficiency of 11-beta-hydroxysteroid
dehydrogenase type 2 normally converting cortisol to cortisone in the collecting duct.
Cortisol activates mineralocorticoid receptors causing severe hypertension, hypokalemia,
metabolic alkalosis, and low renin with low aldosterone.

\medterm{Appendectomy} Surgical removal of the appendix, the most common emergency surgical procedure performed
worldwide. Appendectomy is indicated for acute appendicitis, the most common surgical
emergency with a lifetime risk of 7-8 percent and peak incidence between 10 and 30
years.

\medterm{Appendiceal Tumors} Neoplasms arising in the appendix, including neuroendocrine tumors, mucinous neoplasms, and adenocarcinoma.
Some are found incidentally, whereas others cause pain, obstruction, or abdominal
distention. Evaluation and treatment depend on the tumor type, size, spread, and
pathologic features.

\medterm{Appendicitis} Inflammation of the vermiform appendix, often caused by obstruction of its lumen. It may cause abdominal pain that moves to
the right lower quadrant, nausea, loss of appetite, and fever; diagnosis and treatment
depend on clinical assessment, laboratory tests, imaging, and complications.

\medterm{Appendix} A blind-ended tubular diverticulum of the cecum, typically 6-10 cm long, rich in
lymphoid tissue. Its position is variable (retrocecal, pelvic, or retroileal); the base
usually projects near McBurney's point, one-third of the distance from the anterior
superior iliac spine to the umbilicus.

\medterm{Appetite Regulation} Complex control by hormones (leptin, ghrelin, insulin, CCK), neural signals,
psychological factors. Leptin signals satiety, ghrelin signals hunger.

\medterm{Applied Behavior Analysis} A behavioral intervention based on learning principles that uses observation, measurement, and reinforcement
to increase useful behaviors and reduce behaviors that interfere with functioning. It
may be used in autism care and other settings, with goals individualized to the person.

\medterm{Apraxia} Inability to perform learned, skilled motor movements despite intact motor function,
sensation, coordination, and comprehension.

\medterm{Aquagenic keratoderma} Transient, painful whitish thickening and wrinkling of the palms and soles occurring
within minutes of water immersion, accompanied by a prickling or burning sensation. It
is often associated with cystic fibrosis or other sweat gland disorders but can also be
idiopathic or drug-induced. Symptoms resolve shortly after drying the skin.

\medterm{Aquatic Therapy} Exercise or rehabilitation performed in water, using buoyancy and resistance to support movement and
conditioning.

\medterm{Aqueous Humor} A clear, watery fluid that fills the anterior and posterior chambers of the eye,
maintaining intraocular pressure and providing nutrients and oxygen to the avascular
cornea and lens while removing metabolic waste products.

\medterm{Arachnoid cyst} A congenital, benign, cerebrospinal fluid-filled cyst within the arachnoid membrane,
most commonly found in the middle cranial fossa. Most are asymptomatic and discovered
incidentally on imaging, but large cysts may cause headache, seizures, or focal
neurological deficits.

\medterm{Arachnoid Mater} The middle of the three meninges, a delicate avascular membrane lying between the dura
mater and pia mater. The subarachnoid space between arachnoid and pia contains
cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) and major vessels. Arachnoid granulations (villi) project into
the dural venous sinuses and mediate CSF resorption.

\medterm{ARB-Neprilysin Inhibitor} Sacubitril/valsartan combines an ARB with a neprilysin inhibitor, increasing natriuretic
peptide levels while blocking the renin-angiotensin system. The PARADIGM-HF trial
demonstrated superior reduction in cardiovascular death and heart failure
hospitalization compared to enalapril.

\medterm{Arbovirus} A virus transmitted by mosquitoes or other member of the arthropod phylum.

\medterm{ARBs} Block angiotensin II AT1 receptors providing renoprotection similar to ACE inhibitors.
Alternative for ACE inhibitor cough. Do not combine ACE inhibitor and ARB due to
increased adverse effects without benefit. Similar renoprotective effects to ACE
inhibitors.

\medterm{Arcus Senilis (Corneal Arcus)} A white, gray, or bluish opaque ring in the peripheral corneal stroma separated from the
limbus by a clear zone (lucid interval of Vogt). Caused by lipid (cholesterol)
deposition in the peripheral cornea. Common and benign in elderly individuals (>60
years).

\synonyms
Corneal Arcus

\medterm{ARDS} Acute respiratory distress syndrome is a severe inflammatory lung injury causing acute oxygenation failure and
bilateral lung opacities that are not fully explained by heart failure or fluid overload.
Common causes include sepsis, pneumonia, trauma, and aspiration.

\medterm{Area Under the Concentration-Time Curve} The integral of a drug’s plasma concentration over time. AUC estimates total systemic
exposure and is used to compare bioavailability, guide dosing for selected medicines,
and characterize pharmacokinetic interactions.

\medterm{Argininosuccinic Aciduria} Autosomal recessive urea cycle disorder caused by deficiency of argininosuccinate lyase
(ASL gene, 7cen-q11.2). Biochemical hallmark: accumulation of argininosuccinic acid in
blood, urine, and CSF with hyperammonemia, low citrulline, and elevated argininosuccinic
acid.

\medterm{Argyll Robertson Pupil} A small, irregular pupil that constricts during near focusing but responds poorly or not at all to light. It is
classically associated with neurosyphilis but can have other causes; the finding requires
clinical evaluation.

\medterm{Armpit Lump} A lump or swelling in the axilla, commonly caused by an enlarged lymph node, skin
infection, cyst, or benign growth. Persistent, hard, fixed, enlarging, or unexplained
lumps require medical assessment.

\medterm{Arnold-Chiari Malformation} Herniation of cerebellar tonsils through the foramen magnum into the upper cervical
spinal canal. Type II (myelomeningocele-associated) is the most common form in
pediatrics. Associated with hydrocephalus, syringomyelia, and spinal cord dysfunction.
Symptoms include headache, neck pain, and lower cranial nerve palsies.

\medterm{Aromatase Excess Syndrome} Rare autosomal dominant condition caused by mutations in the CYP19A1 gene leading to
overexpression of aromatase enzyme and excessive estrogen production. Presents in males
with gynecomastia, advanced bone age, and accelerated linear growth with early
epiphyseal closure. In females with precocious puberty, macroclitoris, and virilization.

\medterm{Aromatase Inhibitor} A class of medications that inhibit aromatase, the enzyme that converts androgens
(androstenedione, testosterone) to estrogens (estrone, estradiol) in peripheral tissues
(adipose, breast, muscle, bone). Used primarily in hormone receptor-positive breast
cancer in postmenopausal women.

\medterm{Arrector Pili Muscle} A small band of involuntary smooth muscle attached to a hair follicle and the
superficial dermis. Sympathetic stimulation causes it to contract, pulling the hair
upright and producing temporary piloerection, commonly called goosebumps. Contraction
also compresses nearby sebaceous glands and may help release sebum onto the skin.

\synonyms
goosebumps.

\medterm{Arrested Caries} A carious lesion that is no longer progressing, often because the surface has become
hard, smooth, and cleansable. Arrested lesions may be monitored and remineralized;
treatment depends on activity, cavitation, cleansability, and patient risk.

\medterm{Arrhythmia} Any abnormality of cardiac rate, rhythm, or site of impulse origin, arising through
enhanced automaticity, reentry, or triggered activity. Classified by rate as
tachyarrhythmia or bradyarrhythmia and by site as supraventricular or ventricular.
Diagnosis rests on the 12-lead ECG and ambulatory monitoring when intermittent.

\medterm{Arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy} A genetic cardiomyopathy characterized by fibrofatty replacement of the right
ventricular myocardium, predisposing to ventricular arrhythmias and sudden cardiac
death, particularly during exercise. ECG shows T-wave inversions in V1-V3 and epsilon
waves; diagnosis uses echocardiography, cardiac MRI, and genetic testing.

\medterm{Arsenic toxicity} Systemic poisoning from arsenic exposure causing gastrointestinal distress, cardiac
arrhythmias, peripheral neuropathy, and characteristic Mees lines on nails, requiring
chelation therapy with dimercaprol or succimer.

\medterm{Art Therapy} Use of art-making for therapeutic purposes. Emotional expression, coping,
self-awareness.

\medterm{Arterial Blood Gas} Measurement of arterial pH, carbon-dioxide tension, oxygen tension, bicarbonate,
and related acid-base variables. It helps assess ventilation, oxygenation, and metabolic or respiratory acid-base
disorders.

\medterm{Arterial Blood Gas Analyzer} An analyzer that measures arterial pH, partial pressures of oxygen and carbon dioxide,
and usually bicarbonate or calculated acid-base variables. It is used to assess
ventilation, oxygenation, and metabolic or respiratory acid-base disorders.

\medterm{Arterial Catheter} A catheter placed in an artery, commonly the radial artery, for continuous
blood-pressure monitoring and repeated arterial blood sampling. Complications include
hemorrhage, thrombosis, infection, and distal ischemia.

\medterm{Arterial Catheterization} Placement of a catheter in an artery, usually the radial artery, for continuous
blood-pressure measurement and repeated arterial blood sampling. It is used when rapid
hemodynamic or gas-exchange assessment is clinically necessary and carries risks
including bleeding, infection, thrombosis, and ischemia.

\medterm{Arterial Duplex} Ultrasound technique combining B-mode imaging and Doppler flow assessment. Used for
evaluating arterial stenosis, aneurysm, graft patency. Non-invasive, widely available.

\medterm{Arterial insufficiency} Reduced arterial blood flow to a tissue or limb, causing ischemic symptoms such as
claudication, rest pain, cool skin, delayed capillary refill, and diminished pulses.
Most commonly caused by atherosclerosis; assessed by ankle-brachial index and duplex
ultrasound.

\medterm{Arterial Line} A catheter inserted into a peripheral artery (most commonly radial, also femoral,
brachial, dorsalis pedis) for continuous blood pressure monitoring and arterial blood
gas sampling. Allen test: assesses ulnar collateral flow before radial artery
cannulation.

\medterm{Arterial Ulcer} A chronic wound caused by arterial insufficiency (peripheral artery disease). Located on
distal extremities: toes, heels, lateral malleolus, and sites of pressure. Presents with
punched-out, deep, well-demarcated wound with pale or necrotic base, minimal exudate,
and cool, hairless, shiny skin.

\medterm{Arteriole} A small artery that delivers blood to a capillary bed. Arteriolar smooth muscle
regulates vascular resistance and controls tissue perfusion and systemic blood pressure.

\medterm{Arteriosclerosis} Chronic inflammatory disease of medium and large arteries characterized by accumulation
of lipids, inflammatory cells, and fibrous tissue within the arterial wall, forming
atherosclerotic plaques. See Atherosclerosis.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Fistula} A surgically created connection between an artery and a vein, usually for hemodialysis.
The increased blood flow enlarges and strengthens the vein for repeated access. The site and time needed for maturation
depend on the patient's vessels and clinical circumstances.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Graft} Synthetic conduit connecting artery to vein for dialysis. Used when veins inadequate for
fistula. Thrombosis and infection more common than AVF. Can be used earlier than
fistula. Declotting with thrombectomy or angioplasty.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Malformation} Abnormal connection between artery and vein without capillary bed. Can bleed or cause
symptoms.

\medterm{Arthralgia} Pain in one or more joints without objective evidence of joint inflammation such as swelling, warmth, or structural damage. Common causes include viral infections, overuse, autoimmune disease, and early degenerative change; persistent, severe, or multifocal pain warrants clinical evaluation.

\medterm{Artery} A blood vessel that carries oxygenated blood away from the heart to the tissues, except
for the pulmonary arteries which carry deoxygenated blood to the lungs. Arteries have
thick, elastic, muscular walls that withstand high pressure and distribute blood
throughout the body. The aorta is the largest artery.

\medterm{Arthritis} Inflammation of one or more joints, characterized by joint pain, swelling, warmth, and
stiffness.

\medterm{Arthrocentesis} Aspiration of joint fluid for diagnostic or therapeutic purposes. Indications: septic
arthritis (urgent, crystals), inflammatory arthritis (gout, pseudogout), hemorrhagic
effusion (trauma, coagulopathy), and therapeutic drainage for symptomatic relief.

\medterm{Arthrodesis} Surgical fusion of a joint by removing or preparing articular surfaces and stabilizing
the bones until osseous union occurs. It relieves pain and provides stability at the
cost of joint motion.

\medterm{Arthrography} Contrast injected into joint for imaging. Used for shoulder, knee, hip evaluation. MR
arthrography more common now.

\medterm{Arthrogryposis} A group of congenital disorders characterized by multiple joint contractures present at
birth, caused by decreased fetal movement (fetal akinesia) during development.

\medterm{Arthroplasty} Surgical reconstruction or replacement of a joint to relieve pain, restore alignment, or
improve function. It may be partial or total and is commonly performed for advanced
degenerative joint disease, fracture, inflammatory arthritis, or other destructive joint
conditions.

\medterm{Arthroscopy} Minimally invasive joint visualization and intervention via a rigid arthroscope inserted
through small portals, used for meniscal and ligamentous repair, chondroplasty, loose
body removal, and synovectomy.

\medterm{Articular Cartilage Injuries} Chondral/osteochondral lesions: Outerbridge grading (I: softening, II: fibrillation
<50\%, III: fibrillation >50\%, IV: subchondral bone exposed). OCD (osteochondritis
dissecans): subchondral bone necrosis, fragment separation (knee: lateral femoral
condyle, elbow: capitellum, ankle: talar dome).

\medterm{Artifacts} Image distortions not representing true anatomy. Types: motion, beam hardening, metal,
aliasing, partial volume. Recognition important for accurate interpretation.

\medterm{Artificial Pacemaker} An implanted device that delivers electrical impulses to maintain an appropriate heart rate when
the heart's natural conduction system is inadequate.

\medterm{Artificial Rupture of Membranes} The intentional rupture of the amniotic sac with a small hook-like instrument (Amnihook)
during a cervical exam to induce or augment labor. Indications: induction or
augmentation of labor, placement of internal monitors (fetal scalp electrode,
intrauterine pressure catheter), and assessment of amniotic fluid (meconium staining).

\medterm{Artificial Ventricle (Total Artificial Heart)} A pulsatile, pneumatically or electrically driven biventricular replacement that removes
the native ventricles. Indications: end-stage heart failure bridging to transplant or
destination therapy. Requirements: surgical implantation, external power source,
anticoagulation.

\synonyms
Total Artificial Heart

\medterm{Arytenoid Cartilage} The arytenoid cartilages are a pair of small, pyramid-shaped cartilages located on the
posterior aspect of the larynx, articulating with the superior border of the cricoid
cartilage lamina, and they serve as the attachment points for the vocal cords and the
intrinsic laryngeal muscles that control vocal cord movement.

\medterm{ASA Physical Status Classification System} A system developed by the American Society of Anesthesiologists to assess and
communicate a patient's pre-operative physical status and co-morbidities. It ranges from
ASA I (a normal healthy patient) to ASA VI (a declared brain-dead patient whose organs
are being removed for donor purposes).

\medterm{Asbestosis} Diffuse pulmonary fibrosis caused by prolonged inhalation of asbestos fibers. It may cause progressive
shortness of breath and increases the risk of lung cancer and mesothelioma.

\medterm{Ascariasis} Infection with the soil-transmitted roundworm Ascaris lumbricoides.

\medterm{Ascending Aorta} From left ventricle at the aortic valve to the sternal angle (approximately 5 cm),
enclosed in pericardium. Gives rise to right/left coronary arteries at the aortic
sinuses. Most common site of aneurysm and dissection, especially in Marfan syndrome,
bicuspid aortic valve, and hypertension.

\medterm{Ascending aortic aneurysm} Abnormal enlargement of the ascending portion of the aorta. It may be associated with a
bicuspid aortic valve, connective-tissue disease, hypertension, or atherosclerosis.

\medterm{Ascending Colon} Extends from cecum to hepatic flexure below the liver, approximately 15-20 cm,
retroperitoneal (fixed to posterior abdominal wall). Anteriorly: abdominal wall.
Posteriorly: right kidney. Absorbs water/electrolytes and compacts fecal matter. Tumors
may present as a palpable right-sided mass.

\medterm{Ascites} Pathological accumulation of fluid within the peritoneal cavity representing the most
common decompensating event in cirrhosis. The pathogenesis involves splanchnic arterial
vasodilation reducing effective arterial blood volume and activating neurohormonal
mechanisms leading to sodium and water retention.

\medterm{Aseptic Loosening} Loss of fixation of an implant without active infection, commonly caused by mechanical
stress and a biologic response to wear particles. It may cause pain, radiographic
lucency, instability, and need for revision surgery.

\medterm{Aseptic Technique} A set of laboratory practices preventing contamination of sterile sites, cultures, and
specimens by unwanted microorganisms. Key elements include hand hygiene, sterile fields
and instruments, surface disinfection, and minimal exposure of sterile items to air and
touch, forming the basis of reliable clinical and culture work.

\medterm{Asherman Syndrome} Intrauterine adhesions or scar tissue that partially or completely obliterate the
uterine cavity, most often after uterine instrumentation or pregnancy-related curettage.
It may cause hypomenorrhea or amenorrhea, infertility, recurrent miscarriage, or
abnormal placentation.

\medterm{Asperger Syndrome} A former diagnostic term for a neurodevelopmental condition characterized by persistent social-communication
difficulties and restricted or repetitive interests or behaviors, without the intellectual or language impairment
traditionally associated with classic autism. Current diagnostic systems generally classify it within autism
spectrum disorder; support is individualized.

\medterm{Aspergillosis} A spectrum of diseases caused by the fungus Aspergillus (most commonly A. fumigatus).
Allergic forms: allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA, in asthma or cystic
fibrosis -- wheezing, eosinophilia, pulmonary infiltrates), allergic Aspergillus
sinusitis.

\medterm{Asphyxia} A life-threatening lack of oxygen due to drowning, choking, or an obstruction of the
airways. Management: remove obstruction, rescue breathing, CPR if needed. Foreign body
airway obstruction: Heimlich maneuver (abdominal thrusts) for conscious adults; back
blows and chest thrusts for infants.

\medterm{Aspiration} The inhalation of foreign material (oropharyngeal secretions, gastric contents, food,
liquid, or blood) into the larynx and lower respiratory tract. Aspiration pneumonitis
(Mendelson syndrome) is a chemical lung injury caused by acidic gastric fluid.

\medterm{Aspiration Pneumonia} Infectious pneumonia from aspiration of oropharyngeal secretions. Risk factors:
dysphagia, impaired consciousness, enteral feeding. Common pathogens: anaerobes,
gram-negative bacilli. Treatment: antibiotics covering oral flora.

\medterm{Aspiration Pneumonitis} Chemical lung injury caused by inhalation of gastric contents, characterized by
bronchospasm, hypoxemia, pulmonary edema, and atelectasis. Risk factors include full
stomach, emergency surgery, obesity, pregnancy, gastroesophageal reflux, bowel
obstruction, and decreased consciousness.

\medterm{Aspiration Risk} The likelihood that food, liquid, saliva, or gastric contents will enter the larynx or
lower respiratory tract. Risk rises with dysphagia, delirium, stroke, neuromuscular
disease, reflux, poor dentition, and reduced consciousness; assessment and prevention
are multidisciplinary.

\medterm{Acetylsalicylic Acid} The chemical name for aspirin, a salicylate drug with analgesic, antipyretic, anti-inflammatory, and antiplatelet effects.

\medterm{Acrochordon} A benign, soft, flesh-colored skin tag, usually occurring in skin folds such as the neck, axilla, or groin.

\medterm{Advanced Cardiac Life Support} A structured set of interventions for cardiac arrest and other life-threatening cardiovascular emergencies, including high-quality CPR, rhythm recognition, defibrillation, airway management, and medications.

\medterm{Allen Test} A bedside test of collateral blood flow to the hand through the radial and ulnar arteries, commonly performed before radial-artery puncture or cannulation.

\medterm{Anatomic Snuffbox} A triangular depression on the lateral wrist that becomes visible when the thumb is extended. Tenderness there may suggest a scaphoid fracture.

\medterm{Ankle-Foot Orthosis} A brace that supports the ankle and foot and helps control alignment, stability, or foot drop.

\medterm{Aspirin (Salicylate) Toxicity} Mixed respiratory alkalosis and metabolic acidosis. Early: tinnitus, hyperventilation,
respiratory alkalosis. Late: metabolic acidosis, fever, seizures, coma. Treatment:
activated charcoal, urine alkalinization (sodium bicarbonate), hemodialysis if severe
(renal failure, pulmonary edema, severe acidosis, very high levels).

\synonyms
Salicylate

\medterm{Aspirin-Exacerbated Respiratory Disease} A triad of asthma, nasal polyps, and aspirin sensitivity due to COX-1 inhibition. It
involves overproduction of cysteinyl leukotrienes. Leukotriene receptor antagonists are
specifically effective.

\medterm{Assist-Control Ventilation} Ventilator delivers a set tidal volume at a set rate. Every breath receives the full set
volume. Guarantees minimum rate and minute ventilation. Risk of respiratory alkalosis
and auto-PEEP.

\medterm{Assisted Breech Delivery} Vaginal breech delivery with operator assistance after spontaneous delivery to the
umbilicus.

\medterm{Assisted Living} Residential setting with assistance for ADLs. Less intensive than nursing home.

\medterm{Assisted Reproductive Technology} ART: fertility treatments involving manipulation of oocytes/sperm/embryos: in vitro
fertilization (IVF), intracytoplasmic sperm injection (ICSI), intrauterine insemination
(IUI, some definitions), embryo cryopreservation, gamete donation, and surrogacy.

\medterm{Assistive Device} Equipment that helps a person perform activities, move safely, communicate, or maintain independence.

\medterm{Assistive Device Training} Instruction in selecting, fitting, using, and maintaining devices such as walkers, canes, or crutches, including safe walking and stair techniques.

\medterm{Association Study} Comparison of allele frequencies between cases and controls. Genome-wide association
studies (GWAS) identify common variants with small effects on complex diseases.

\medterm{Astereognosis} Inability to identify an object by touch alone despite intact primary somatosensory
modalities (touch, vibration, proprioception), indicating dysfunction of the
contralateral parietal association cortex (typically the postcentral gyrus region or the
dominant inferior parietal lobule).

\medterm{Asterixis} Brief, irregular, non-rhythmic lapse of sustained posture causing a flapping movement,
best elicited by asking the patient to hold the arms extended with wrists dorsiflexed
("hold arms up") for 30 seconds.

\medterm{Asthenopia} Eye strain or visual fatigue, often experienced as aching, burning, blurred vision, headache, or difficulty
focusing after prolonged visual tasks. Refractive errors, dry eye, poor lighting, and extended
screen use may contribute.

\medterm{Asthma} A chronic inflammatory disorder of the airways characterized by recurrent episodes of
wheezing, breathlessness, chest tightness, and cough. Airflow limitation is often
reversible. It involves eosinophilic inflammation, mucus hypersecretion, and smooth
muscle hypertrophy.

\medterm{Asthma exacerbation} An acute or subacute worsening of asthma symptoms and airflow limitation, typically
causing increased wheeze, cough, chest tightness, or breathlessness.

\medterm{Asthma-COPD Overlap} Features of both asthma and COPD in the same patient. Characterized by persistent
airflow limitation with features of both diseases: airway inflammation (eosinophilic and
neutrophilic), bronchial hyperresponsiveness, and partially reversible obstruction.
Treatment: inhaled corticosteroids plus long-acting bronchodilators.

\medterm{Astigmatism} A refractive error caused by an irregular curvature of the cornea or lens, resulting in
light rays being focused at multiple points rather than a single point on the retina,
leading to blurred or distorted vision at all distances.

\medterm{Astrocytoma} A glioma arising from star-shaped cells called astrocytes. Graded I-IV by the WHO; grade
IV (glioblastoma) is the most aggressive and common primary malignant brain tumor in
adults.

\medterm{Asymptomatic Bacteriuria in Pregnancy} The presence of significant bacterial growth in a pregnant person's urine
without urinary symptoms. It is identified by urine culture and is treated because it increases the risk of symptomatic
urinary infection and kidney infection during pregnancy.

\medterm{Asystole} Complete cessation of cardiac electrical activity, presenting as a flat line on the ECG
with no QRS complexes, P waves, or other organized electrical activity.

\medterm{Ataxia} Lack of voluntary coordination of muscle movements, resulting from dysfunction of the
cerebellum, posterior columns, or peripheral nerves. Types: cerebellar (gait and limb
ataxia, dysarthria, nystagmus), sensory (impaired proprioception causing sensory ataxia,
positive Romberg sign), and vestibular (vertigo, nystagmus, nausea).

\medterm{Ataxia-Telangiectasia} An autosomal-recessive disorder usually caused by biallelic ATM variants, characterized by progressive ataxia, telangiectasias, immune deficiency, and increased cancer risk.

\medterm{Ataxic Breathing} An irregular, unpredictable breathing pattern with random variations in rate and depth,
seen in medullary lesions. It is the most dangerous respiratory pattern and often
precedes respiratory arrest. It requires mechanical ventilation.

\medterm{Atelectasis} Complete or partial collapse of lung tissue, resulting in loss of aeration. It may be
caused by airway obstruction (resorption atelectasis), pleural disease (compressive
atelectasis), surfactant dysfunction (dissolution atelectasis), or scarring
(cicatrization atelectasis). It impairs gas exchange and may predispose to infection.

\medterm{Atherectomy} Mechanical removal of atherosclerotic plaque. Types: directional, laser, rotational,
orbital. Used for peripheral arterial disease.

\medterm{Atherectomy (Vascular)} Removal or modification of atherosclerotic plaque from inside a blood vessel, usually with a
catheter-based device.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Atheroembolic Renal Disease} Cholesterol crystal embolization to kidneys after vascular procedures. AKI with livedo
reticularis and peripheral eosinophilia. Renal biopsy shows cholesterol clefts. Poor
prognosis with progressive renal failure.

\medterm{Atherosclerosis} Chronic inflammatory disease of medium and large arteries characterized by accumulation
of lipids, inflammatory cells, and fibrous tissue within the arterial wall, forming
atherosclerotic plaques. Initiating event is endothelial dysfunction from risk factors
(hypertension, smoking, diabetes, dyslipidemia).

\medterm{Atherosclerotic Plaque} An atherosclerotic plaque is a lesion within the arterial intima composed of a necrotic
lipid core covered by a fibrous cap, representing the pathological substrate of
atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease.

\medterm{Athetosis} Slow, involuntary, writhing, sinuous movements predominantly of the distal extremities,
sometimes also affecting the face, neck, and trunk, arising from dysfunction of the
basal ganglia (putamen and globus pallidus).

\medterm{Athlete Foot} Tinea pedis: dermatophyte infection (Trichophyton rubrum, T. mentagrophytes) of the
feet, common in those who wear occlusive footwear or use communal showers. Forms:
interdigital (scaly, fissured, macerated between toes—most common), moccasin (diffuse
plantar scaling), vesicular.

\medterm{Athlete's Foot} A superficial fungal infection of the feet, usually affecting the spaces between the
toes and causing itching, scaling, maceration, or fissures. It is also called tinea
pedis.

\synonyms
tinea.

\medterm{Atlanto-occipital dislocation} Traumatic separation of the atlas from the occipital condyles, a highly unstable injury
with high mortality often associated with spinal cord injury and requiring emergent
immobilization.

\medterm{Atlas} First cervical vertebra articulating with occipital condyles at the atlanto-occipital
joint for nodding movement. Lacks a body and spinous process; has anterior/posterior
arches and lateral masses. Transverse foramina transmit vertebral arteries. The anterior
arch has a facet for the axis dens.

\medterm{Atopic Dermatitis} A chronic, relapsing inflammatory skin disease characterized by pruritus, xerosis, and
eczematous lesions. It reflects epidermal-barrier dysfunction and immune dysregulation,
with flares triggered by irritants, allergens, infection, stress, or climate.

\medterm{Atopic eruption of pregnancy} Pruritic eczematous skin eruptions occurring in pregnancy, presenting as eczematous
patches or pruritic urticarial papules and plaques, typically resolving after delivery.

\medterm{Atopy} The genetic predisposition to develop immediate (IgE-mediated, type I) hypersensitivity
reactions to common environmental allergens (pollen, dust mites, foods, pet dander).

\medterm{Atovaquone–proguanil} An antimalarial combination used for prevention and treatment of Plasmodium falciparum
malaria and selected travel-related malaria regimens.

\medterm{ATP (Adenosine Triphosphate)} The primary energy currency of the cell, composed of adenine, ribose, and three
phosphate groups. Hydrolysis of the terminal phosphoanhydride bond releases $\Delta G^\circ = -30.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$
($-7.3\,\mathrm{kcal/mol}$), driving endergonic cellular processes such as muscle
contraction, active transport, and biosynthesis.

\synonyms
Adenosine Triphosphate

\medterm{ATP Synthase} Complex V of electron transport chain. Uses proton gradient to synthesize ATP.
Rotational catalysis mechanism.

\medterm{Atransferrinemia} Very rare autosomal recessive disorder of iron transport caused by mutations in the TF
gene (3q22.1) leading to absence or severe deficiency of transferrin.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation} The most common sustained cardiac arrhythmia, characterized by rapid, irregular atrial
activity (350-600 bpm) with irregular ventricular response. ECG shows absent P waves,
irregularly irregular RR intervals, and narrow QRS complexes (unless aberrant
conduction).

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation Ablation} A catheter-based procedure that creates lesions, usually around the pulmonary veins,
to reduce the electrical triggers of atrial fibrillation. It may be considered for
symptomatic patients when medicines are ineffective, not tolerated, or not preferred.
Results and the need for repeat treatment vary with the type of atrial fibrillation and
the person’s clinical features.

\medterm{Atrial Flutter} A rapid, regular atrial rhythm (typically 250-350 bpm) caused by a macro-reentrant
circuit, most commonly in the right atrium. ECG shows sawtooth flutter waves best seen
in leads II, III, and aVF, with regular or irregular ventricular response depending on
AV conduction ratio (commonly 2:1 or 4:1).

\medterm{Atrial Natriuretic Peptide} A peptide hormone secreted by atrial myocytes in response to atrial stretch from volume
expansion. ANP promotes natriuresis and diuresis by increasing glomerular filtration
rate, inhibiting sodium reabsorption in the collecting ducts, and reducing renin and
aldosterone secretion.

\medterm{Atrial Septal Defect} Congenital opening in the interatrial secundum allowing left-to-right shunting of blood.
Small defects may close spontaneously. Larger defects cause right heart volume overload,
right atrial and ventricular dilation, pulmonary hypertension, and eventually right
heart failure. Paradoxical embolism may occur with right-to-left shunting.

\medterm{Atrial Tachycardia} Supraventricular tachycardia arising from an atrial focus outside the sinus node, often
focal with rates 100-250 beats per minute with discrete P waves of different morphology
from sinus P waves.

\medterm{Atrioventricular Node} Located in the posterior-inferior region of the interatrial septum near the coronary
sinus. It receives impulses from the SA node via internodal pathways and delays them by
approximately 120-200 milliseconds before transmitting them to the ventricles. This
delay allows atrial contraction to complete before ventricular contraction.

\medterm{Atrium} One of the two upper chambers of the heart that receive blood returning to the heart.
The right atrium receives deoxygenated blood from the superior and inferior venae cavae
and the coronary sinus; the left atrium receives oxygenated blood from the four
pulmonary veins.

\medterm{Atropine in Organophosphate Poisoning} A muscarinic antagonist used to reverse bronchorrhea, bronchospasm, bradycardia, and
other muscarinic effects of organophosphate toxicity. Treatment is titrated to drying of
respiratory secretions and improvement in ventilation, not to pupil size alone.

\medterm{Attachment (Dental)} Retentive device connecting denture to implant or tooth. Precision attachments preferred
over bar attachments.

\synonyms
Dental

\medterm{Attack Rate} The proportion of an at-risk population that develops a specified illness during a
defined outbreak or exposure period. It is a cumulative incidence measure commonly used
for foodborne and other localized outbreaks.

\medterm{Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder} A neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by persistent patterns of inattention,
hyperactivity, and impulsivity that interfere with functioning or development. Three
presentations: predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, and
combined.

\medterm{Attenuation Correction} Compensation for photon absorption in tissue. CT-based (PET/CT), transmission source
(SPECT), or MR-based (PET/MRI).

\medterm{Attributable Risk} Difference in risk between exposed and unexposed. AR = incidence exposed - incidence
unexposed.

\medterm{Atypia} Cytologic or architectural abnormality that deviates from the expected appearance of a
cell or tissue. Atypia may be reactive, degenerative, premalignant, or malignant and
must be interpreted with architecture, clinical context, and ancillary studies.

\medterm{Atypical Antipsychotics} A class of modern psychiatric medications used to treat schizophrenia and bipolar
disorder. They work by balancing dopamine and serotonin levels in the brain, which helps
reduce symptoms like hallucinations and delusions with fewer movement-related side
effects than older antipsychotics.

\medterm{Atypical Facial Pain} Chronic facial pain without identifiable cause. Psychological factors may contribute.

\medterm{Atypical Features} A specifier for depressive episodes characterized by mood reactivity, significant weight
gain or increased appetite, hypersomnia, leaden paralysis, and a long-standing pattern
of interpersonal rejection sensitivity. Atypical depression often presents in younger
patients and may respond preferentially to MAOIs or SSRIs rather than TCAs.

\medterm{Atypical Pneumonia} Pneumonia with a gradual onset, relatively mild chest findings, and prominent systemic
or extrapulmonary symptoms. Common causes include Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydophila
pneumoniae, and respiratory viruses.

\medterm{Audiogram} A chart that shows a person’s ability to hear at different pitches or frequencies.

\medterm{Audiologist} A health professional who assesses hearing and fits hearing aids.

\medterm{Audiometry} Measurement of hearing sensitivity at different sound frequencies and intensities, usually using headphones or insert earphones.

\medterm{Aural Fullness} A sensation of pressure, blockage, or plugged feeling in the ear. Causes include eustachian-tube dysfunction,
middle-ear fluid, cerumen impaction, inner-ear disorders, and temporomandibular-joint disease.

\synonyms
Ear pressure; plugged-ear sensation; blocked-ear sensation.

\medterm{Auer Rods} Rod-shaped inclusions formed from fused azurophilic granules in myeloid precursor cells. They strongly
support a myeloid neoplasm, especially acute myeloid leukemia, but diagnosis requires
immunophenotyping, genetic studies, and clinical correlation.

\medterm{Auger Electrons} Low-energy electrons emitted during electron capture decay. Short range (nanometers),
high linear energy transfer. Potential for targeted radiotherapy if localized near DNA.

\medterm{Augmentation of Labor} The stimulation of spontaneous contractions that are judged to be inadequate to achieve
progressive cervical dilatation. Most commonly uses oxytocin (Pitocin) infusion. Also
includes artificial rupture of membranes (AROM, amniotomy).

\medterm{Auricle} The visible external portion of the ear that projects from the side of the head,
designed to collect and channel sound waves into the ear canal.

\medterm{Auricular Hematoma} A collection of blood between the cartilage of the external ear and its perichondrium, usually after blunt trauma.
Without prompt drainage and compression, it may cause cartilage necrosis and permanent cauliflower-ear deformity.

\synonyms
Pinna hematoma; subperichondrial auricular hematoma.

\medterm{Auscultation} The act of listening to sounds produced within the body, typically using a stethoscope.
Essential for assessing heart sounds, lung sounds, bowel sounds, and vascular bruits.
Normal findings: S1 and S2 heart sounds, vesicular breath sounds, normal bowel sounds.
Abnormal: murmurs, crackles, wheezes, rubs, bruits.

\medterm{Auscultatory Gap} A transient silence between the first and second sounds of blood pressure measurement,
sometimes heard in hypertensive patients. Can lead to underestimation of systolic
pressure if not recognized.

\medterm{Austin Flint Murmur} A mid-diastolic, low-pitched rumbling murmur heard at the apex, caused by the
regurgitant jet from severe aortic regurgitation striking the anterior mitral leaflet.

\medterm{Autism Spectrum Disorder} Neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by persistent deficits in social communication
and social interaction across multiple contexts, restricted and repetitive patterns of
behavior, interests, or activities. Symptoms present in early developmental period and
cause clinically significant impairment.

\medterm{Autoantibody} An immunoglobulin that recognizes and binds an endogenous antigen. Autoantibodies may be
pathogenic, as in autoimmune hemolytic anemia, or may serve as laboratory markers of
autoimmune disease.

\medterm{Autoclave} Sterilization device using high-pressure steam. Parameters: 121°C, 15 psi, 15-30
minutes. Essential for infection control.

\medterm{Autogenic Drainage} A breathing technique using controlled breathing at different lung volumes to mobilize
secretions from peripheral to central airways. It involves breathing at low lung volumes
(collect), mid lung volumes (loosen), and high lung volumes (evacuate). It requires
patient training and cooperation.

\medterm{Autograft} Tissue transplanted from one site to another within the same individual. Autografts are
genetically identical to the recipient and generally avoid alloimmune rejection,
although donor-site morbidity may occur.

\medterm{Autoimmune Disease} Autoimmune diseases result from loss of immunological self-tolerance, leading to
immune-mediated attack on the body's own tissues.

\medterm{Autoimmune Disorder} A disease caused by an abnormal immune response against the body's own cells, tissues,
or organs. Autoimmune disorders may be organ-specific or systemic and can produce
inflammation, tissue injury, functional impairment, or circulating autoantibodies.

\medterm{Autoimmune Enteropathy} Rare immune-mediated disease causing persistent diarrhoea and small-intestinal mucosal
injury, usually with villous atrophy and crypt hyperplasia. It may occur in children or
adults and can be associated with other autoimmune disorders or immune dysregulation.

\medterm{Autoimmune Gastritis} Chronic atrophic gastritis caused by antibodies against parietal cells leading to
achlorhydria and vitamin B12 deficiency. Associated with pernicious anemia and increased
risk of gastric carcinoid tumors and type 1 gastric neuroendocrine tumors. Anti-parietal
cell antibodies are present in approximately ninety percent of patients.

\medterm{Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia} AIHA: hemolytic anemia due to antibodies directed against red blood cells. Warm AIHA
(IgG, 37C—idiopathic, SLE, CLL, drugs: methyldopa, penicillin): splenic sequestration,
spherocytes, positive direct antiglobulin (DAT).

\medterm{Autoimmune Hepatitis} Chronic hepatitis caused by immune-mediated destruction of hepatocytes characterized by
interface hepatitis with lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate, elevated transaminases,
hypergammaglobulinemia, and autoantibodies.

\medterm{Autoimmune keratitis} Corneal inflammation associated with an immune-mediated disorder, sometimes occurring with
rheumatoid arthritis, lupus, or granulomatosis with polyangiitis. It may cause pain,
redness, corneal opacity, and reduced vision and requires urgent ophthalmic assessment.
Treatment is directed at the cause and may include anti-inflammatory or immunosuppressive
therapy.

\medterm{Autoimmune Pancreatitis} Chronic inflammatory condition characterized by dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate with
IgG4-positive plasma cells representing the pancreatic manifestation of IgG4-related
disease. Type 1 is more common affecting older men with elevated serum IgG4 and
extrapancreatic involvement.

\medterm{Autoimmune Polyendocrine Syndromes} APS-1 (APECED, AIRE mutation): autosomal recessive, chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis,
hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency (Addison's), plus type 1 diabetes, autoimmune
thyroiditis, vitiligo, alopecia, pernicious anemia, hepatitis, asplenia.

\medterm{Autoimmunity: Mechanisms} Loss of tolerance: central (thymic negative selection defects, AIRE mutation -> APS-1)
and peripheral (Treg dysfunction, anergy failure, ignorance loss). Molecular mimicry:
cross-reactivity between pathogen and self (rheumatic fever: Strep M protein + cardiac
myosin; Guillain-Barre: Campylobacter + GM1 ganglioside; reactive arthritis).

\medterm{Autologous Blood Donation} Autologous blood donation involves preoperative collection and storage of the patient's
own blood for intraoperative or postoperative transfusion. It eliminates risks of
alloimmune reactions, transfusion-transmitted infections, and immune modulation.

\medterm{Autonomic Dysreflexia} Acute, potentially life-threatening sympathetic discharge in a person with spinal-cord
injury at or above approximately T6, triggered by a noxious stimulus below the lesion.
It causes sudden hypertension, headache, flushing or sweating above the lesion, and
reflex bradycardia or other heart-rate changes.

\medterm{Autonomic Nervous System} The part of the peripheral nervous system that regulates involuntary functions,
including heart rate, vascular tone, digestion, sweating, pupil size, and bladder
activity.

\medterm{Autonomic Neuropathy} Damage to nerves that control involuntary functions such as heart rate, blood pressure, digestion, sweating, bladder
function, and sexual function. Diabetes is a common cause, but other diseases, toxins, and medicines may contribute.
Symptoms depend on the affected organs and require evaluation of the underlying cause.

\medterm{Autophagy} Cellular self-degradation process delivering cytoplasmic components to lysosomes for
recycling. Macroautophagy: phagophore forms, expands to autophagosome (LC3-II on
membrane), fuses with lysosome (autolysosome) for degradation. Selective autophagy:
mitophagy (damaged mitochondria), xenophagy (pathogens), and aggrephagy (protein
aggregates).

\medterm{Autopsy} A postmortem examination of a body to determine cause and manner of death, performed by
a forensic pathologist (medical examiner/coroner) or, for hospital autopsies, a
pathologist.

\medterm{Autopsy (Medicolegal)} Systematic external examination and surgical dissection of a deceased body performed
under legal authority by a forensic pathologist to determine the Cause, Manner, and
Mechanism of Death, document injuries, collect evidence, and assist judicial
investigations.

\synonyms
Medicolegal

\medterm{Autopsy (Post-Mortem Examination)} Specialized surgical procedure performed on a deceased body to determine the cause of
death, mechanism of death, manner of death (natural, accidental, suicide, homicide, or
undetermined), state of health prior to death, and to document injuries or disease
processes.

\synonyms
Post-Mortem Examination

\medterm{Autosomal Dominant PKD} Most common hereditary kidney disease affecting 1 in 400 to 1000. PKD1 chromosome 16
more severe with earlier ESRD than PKD2 chromosome 4. Progressive cyst growth causes
hypertension, pain, hematuria, infections, and eventually ESRD. Screen family members
with ultrasound. Tolvaptan may slow cyst growth.

\medterm{Autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease} An inherited disorder causing progressive growth of numerous renal cysts that enlarge
the kidneys and destroy parenchyma, leading to hypertension, flank pain, and eventual
kidney failure. Associated with hepatic cysts and intracranial aneurysms.
