Dental drainage, root-canal treatment, or extraction may be needed; fever, facial swelling, or difficulty swallowing requires urgent care.

\medterm{Abscesses Skin (Boils)} Alternate index label for Boils.

\medterm{Abuse Child (Child Abuse Facts)} Alternate index label for Child Abuse Facts.

\medterm{Abuse Steroid (Anabolic Steroid Abuse)} Alternate index label for Anabolic Steroid Abuse.

\medterm{ACA (Affordable Care Act (ACA or ObamaCare))} Alternate index label for Affordable Care Act (ACA or ObamaCare).

\medterm{Aches, Pain, Fever} Aches, pain, and fever are common symptoms of infection, inflammation, injury, medication effects, or other illness. The pattern, duration, associated symptoms, and temperature help determine whether evaluation or urgent treatment is needed.

\medterm{Achondroplasia vs Pseudoachondroplasia Differences} Achondroplasia is a common skeletal dysplasia caused by altered FGFR3 signaling, whereas pseudoachondroplasia is usually caused by COMP or related cartilage-matrix variants. Both cause disproportionate short stature, but their spine, joint, radiographic, and inheritance features differ.

\medterm{Acid reflux (Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease (GERD))} Alternate index label for Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease (GERD).

\medterm{Acid Reflux (GERD in Infants and Children)} Alternate index label for GERD in Infants and Children.

\medterm{Acid Reflux vs. Heartburn (Heartburn vs. Acid Reflux Differences Similarities)} Alternate index label for Heartburn vs. Acid Reflux Differences Similarities.

\medterm{acne rosacea (Rosacea)} Alternate index label for Rosacea.

\medterm{Acquired Generalized and Inherited Lipodystrophy} Lipodystrophy is abnormal loss or absence of adipose tissue. Acquired generalized forms develop after an immune or other trigger, while inherited forms result from gene variants; both can cause severe insulin resistance, diabetes, high triglycerides, and fatty liver.

\medterm{Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (AIDS)} Alternate index label for AIDS.

\medterm{acrochordon (Skin Tag)} Alternate index label for Skin Tag.

\medterm{Actinic Purpura Vs Senile Purpura} Actinic or senile purpura consists of painless purple bruises on sun-damaged, thin skin, especially on the hands and forearms. It reflects fragile dermal vessels and connective tissue rather than a dangerous bleeding disorder, although widespread or unexplained bruising needs assessment.

\medterm{Acute and Chronic Bursitis} Bursitis is inflammation of a fluid-filled bursa that cushions a joint. Acute bursitis may follow trauma, infection, or sudden overuse; chronic bursitis reflects repeated pressure or inflammatory disease and causes localized pain, swelling, and tenderness with movement.

\medterm{Acute Bronchitis (Bronchitis (Acute))} Alternate index label for Bronchitis (Acute).

\medterm{Addiction Sexual (Sexual Addiction)} Alternate index label for Sexual Addiction.

\medterm{Addisons anemia (Pernicious Anemia)} Alternate index label for Pernicious Anemia.

\medterm{Addison’s Disease How Treated and Can It Be Cured} Addison disease is primary adrenal insufficiency, in which the adrenal glands do not produce enough cortisol and often aldosterone. Lifelong hormone replacement usually controls it; stress dosing and emergency injectable steroid plans help prevent adrenal crisis.

\medterm{Adenoids and Tonsils} The adenoids and tonsils are lymphoid tissues in the upper airway that participate in immune surveillance. Enlargement or recurrent infection can cause mouth breathing, snoring, sleep apnea, swallowing problems, or recurrent throat disease and may require medical or surgical evaluation.

\medterm{ADHD in Teens (Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder in Teens)} Alternate index label for Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder in Teens.

\medterm{adhesive capsulitis of shoulder (Frozen Shoulder)} Alternate index label for Frozen Shoulder.

\medterm{Adult ADHD} Adult attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder is a persistent pattern of inattention, impulsivity, and hyperactivity that begins in childhood and impairs work, relationships, or daily organization. Diagnosis uses developmental history and symptom assessment; treatment may combine skills-based therapy and medication.

\medterm{Adult-Onset Asthma} Adult-onset asthma is variable airway inflammation and narrowing that begins in adulthood, producing episodic wheezing, cough, chest tightness, or shortness of breath. Diagnosis uses symptoms and lung-function testing, and treatment reduces triggers and airway inflammation.

\medterm{Adults Autism (Autism and Communication)} Alternate index label for Autism and Communication.

\medterm{Adults Jaundice (Jaundice in Adults)} Alternate index label for Jaundice in Adults.

\medterm{Advance Directives (Advance Medical Directives)} Alternate index label for Advance Medical Directives.

\medterm{Advance Medical Directives} Advance medical directives record a person's preferences for future medical care and identify a health-care proxy when the person cannot decide. They may address resuscitation, life support, artificial nutrition, and other treatments, and should be reviewed as circumstances change.

\medterm{Advantages and Disadvantages of a Hospital Birth} A hospital birth provides immediate access to obstetric, anesthesia, newborn, and surgical services, which can be important when complications arise. Tradeoffs may include more routine interventions, less control over setting, and separation or transfer policies that vary by facility.

\medterm{Advantages and Disadvantages of Hysterectomy} Hysterectomy removes the uterus and permanently ends pregnancy and menstruation. It can treat cancer, fibroids, severe bleeding, prolapse, or pain, but involves operative risks, recovery time, and possible effects on ovarian function or pelvic organs depending on the procedure.

\medterm{AF (Atrial Fibrillation)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation.

\medterm{Affordable Care Act (ACA or ObamaCare)} Alternate index label for ACA or ObamaCare.

\medterm{Afib (Atrial Fibrillation (AFib) Warning Symptoms)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation (AFib) Warning Symptoms.

\medterm{Afib (Atrial Fibrillation)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation.

\medterm{AFib vs VFib Symptoms (Atrial Fibrillation vs. Ventricular Fibrillation)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation vs. Ventricular Fibrillation.

\medterm{After a Positive Ovulation Test Are You Fertile?} A positive ovulation test detects a rise in luteinizing hormone that usually precedes ovulation by about one to two days. Fertility is highest around this interval, but timing varies and the test does not confirm that an egg was released.

\medterm{After Endolymphatic Sac Decompression Surgery} After endolymphatic sac decompression, follow-up focuses on wound healing, vertigo, hearing, balance, and recurrence of inner-ear symptoms. Activity and ear-care instructions depend on the operation, and persistent severe vertigo, drainage, fever, or facial weakness needs prompt review.

\medterm{age related macular degeneration (Macular Degeneration)} Alternate index label for Macular Degeneration.

\medterm{AIDS vs. HIV (HIV vs AIDS)} Alternate index label for HIV vs AIDS.

\medterm{Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism} Alcohol use disorder is a medical condition involving impaired control over drinking despite harm, craving, tolerance, or withdrawal. Treatment can include medically supervised withdrawal, counseling, peer support, and medications that reduce drinking or relapse risk.

\medterm{Alcohol and Teens} Alcohol exposure during adolescence can impair judgment, increase injury and unsafe-sex risk, and affect developing brain systems involved in learning and impulse control. Early screening, supportive communication, treatment of coexisting mental-health problems, and limiting access reduce harm.

\medterm{alcohol dependence (Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism)} Alternate index label for Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism.

\medterm{Alcohol Pregnancy (Fetal Alcohol Syndrome (FAS))} Alternate index label for Fetal Alcohol Syndrome (FAS).

\medterm{Allergic Cascade} The allergic cascade is the sequence in which an allergen activates immune cells, including IgE-bearing mast cells, causing release of histamine and other mediators. This produces symptoms such as itching, swelling, mucus, wheezing, hives, or anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergies (Allergy)} Alternate index label for Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy} An allergy is an inappropriate immune response to a normally harmless substance such as pollen, food, medicine, insect venom, or animal proteins. Symptoms range from localized rhinitis or hives to life-threatening anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergy Diaper (Intertrigo)} Alternate index label for Intertrigo.

\medterm{Allergy Eczema (Atopic Dermatitis)} Alternate index label for Atopic Dermatitis.

\medterm{Allergy Eye (Eye Allergy)} Alternate index label for Eye Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy Food (Food Allergy)} Alternate index label for Food Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy Home Remedies (Allergy Treatment Begins At Home)} Alternate index label for Allergy Treatment Begins At Home.

\medterm{Allergy Insect (Insect Sting Allergies)} Alternate index label for Insect Sting Allergies.

\medterm{Allergy Latex (Latex Allergy)} Alternate index label for Latex Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy Plant Contact (Poison Ivy, Oak, and Sumac)} Alternate index label for Poison Ivy, Oak, and Sumac.

\medterm{Allergy Rash (Rash)} Alternate index label for Rash.

\medterm{Allergy Treatment Begins At Home} Home allergy care may include reducing exposure to the trigger, improving indoor air filtration, washing after outdoor exposure, saline nasal rinsing with safe water, and using recommended medicines correctly. Breathing difficulty, throat swelling, faintness, or widespread symptoms require emergency care.

\medterm{Alopecia (Hair Loss)} Alternate index label for Hair Loss.

\medterm{ALS vs. MS (ALS vs. MS Differences and Similarities)} Alternate index label for ALS vs. MS Differences and Similarities.

\medterm{ALS vs. MS Differences and Similarities} Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis primarily destroys motor neurons and causes progressive weakness, while multiple sclerosis causes immune-mediated lesions in the brain and spinal cord with varied sensory, visual, motor, or cognitive symptoms. Examination, imaging, laboratory testing, and time course distinguish them.

\medterm{Alternative Therapy for Multiple Sclerosis} Complementary approaches for multiple sclerosis may support symptoms such as fatigue, pain, stress, or mobility but do not replace disease-modifying treatment. Exercise, rehabilitation, mindfulness, and selected supplements should be discussed with clinicians because interactions and unproven claims are common.

\medterm{Alternative Treatments for Hot Flashes} Nonhormonal approaches to hot flashes include cooling strategies, layered clothing, sleep and stress measures, cognitive behavioral therapy, and selected prescription medicines. Herbal products have variable evidence and can interact with medicines; treatment choice depends on symptoms and medical history.

\medterm{alveolar osteitis (Dry Socket Overview)} Alternate index label for Dry Socket Overview.

\medterm{Alzheimer's Disease Causes, Stages, and Symptoms} Alzheimer disease is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder causing decline in memory and other thinking abilities, followed by increasing difficulty with communication, judgment, movement, and self-care. Progression varies, and evaluation looks for reversible contributors and other dementias.

\medterm{Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms and Stages} Alzheimer disease commonly progresses from subtle memory and planning problems to loss of independence, language and recognition difficulties, and eventually extensive assistance needs. Symptoms and speed of progression differ, so staging supports care planning rather than predicting an exact timeline.

\medterm{Alzheimers Disease Stages and Symptoms (Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms and Stages)} Alternate index label for Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms and Stages.

\medterm{Am I Depressed} Depression is more than temporary sadness: it involves persistent low mood or loss of interest with changes in sleep, appetite, energy, concentration, movement, self-worth, or hope. Thoughts of self-harm require immediate emergency or crisis support.

\medterm{Amblyopia Lazy Eye} Amblyopia is reduced vision in an eye that did not develop normal visual processing during childhood, often because of unequal focusing power, misalignment, or obstruction. Early treatment may include glasses, patching or blurring the stronger eye, and correction of the underlying problem.

\medterm{Amebiasis Entamoeba histolytica Infection} Amebiasis is infection with Entamoeba histolytica, acquired through fecally contaminated food, water, or hands. It may cause diarrhea or dysentery and can spread to the liver; stool testing and appropriate tissue-active and luminal treatment are used.

\medterm{amoebic dysentery (Amebiasis Entamoeba histolytica Infection)} Alternate index label for Amebiasis Entamoeba histolytica Infection.

\medterm{amyloid disease (Amyloidosis)} Alternate index label for Amyloidosis.

\medterm{Anabolic Steroid Abuse} Anabolic-androgenic steroid misuse can cause infertility, testicular shrinkage, acne, mood changes, high blood pressure, adverse cholesterol changes, liver injury, and cardiovascular events. Stopping may produce withdrawal symptoms and is safest with medical support.

\medterm{anal ulcer (Anal Fissure)} Alternate index label for Anal Fissure.

\medterm{Anemia How Is It Treated and Can It Be Cured} Anemia is reduced oxygen-carrying capacity from low red-cell number or hemoglobin. Treatment depends on cause, such as iron or vitamin deficiency, blood loss, kidney disease, inflammation, or inherited disease; correcting the cause may resolve it, while some conditions require long-term management.

\medterm{Aneurysm vs Stroke (Stroke vs Aneurysm Differences and Similarities)} Alternate index label for Stroke vs Aneurysm Differences and Similarities.

\medterm{Aneurysm vs Stroke Which Is Worse} An aneurysm is a weakened, bulging blood-vessel wall, whereas a stroke is brain injury from interrupted blood flow or bleeding. A ruptured brain aneurysm can cause a hemorrhagic stroke, and the seriousness of either condition depends on location, size, bleeding, and neurologic injury.

\medterm{angiitis (Vasculitis)} Alternate index label for Vasculitis.

\medterm{Angina Symptoms} Angina is chest discomfort caused by inadequate blood flow to heart muscle, commonly from coronary artery disease. Pressure or squeezing may spread to the arm, jaw, back, or shoulder and can occur with exertion; new, severe, or resting symptoms require emergency assessment.

\medterm{Angioedema (Hives)} Alternate index label for Hives.

\medterm{Angioedema Hereditary (Hereditary Angioedema HAE)} Alternate index label for Hereditary Angioedema HAE.

\medterm{Ankle Dislocated (Dislocated Ankle (Ankle Dislocation))} Alternate index label for Dislocated Ankle (Ankle Dislocation).

\medterm{Ankle Pain and Tendinitis} Ankle pain may result from sprain, fracture, arthritis, tendon inflammation, nerve disease, infection, or referred pain. Tendinitis causes pain along an affected tendon and often worsens with loading; swelling, deformity, inability to bear weight, fever, or numbness warrants evaluation.

\medterm{Ankle Swollen (Swollen Ankles and Swollen Feet)} Alternate index label for Swollen Ankles and Swollen Feet.

\medterm{antidiabetics-oral} Oral antidiabetic medicines lower blood glucose through different mechanisms, including increasing insulin release, improving insulin sensitivity, reducing hepatic glucose production, or increasing urinary glucose loss. Selection considers kidney function, hypoglycemia risk, weight, cardiovascular disease, and cost.

\medterm{Antiemetics} Antiemetics are medicines used to prevent or treat nausea and vomiting. Drug classes act on serotonin, dopamine, histamine, acetylcholine, or neurokinin pathways; choice depends on the cause, age, pregnancy, other medicines, heart rhythm risk, and hydration status.

\medterm{Anxiety Separation (Separation Anxiety)} Separation anxiety is excessive fear or distress when separated from an attachment figure, beyond what is expected for developmental stage. It can cause avoidance, physical complaints, sleep difficulty, and school impairment and is treated with behavioral therapy and, when needed, medication.

\medterm{Apnea Sleep (Sleep Apnea)} Sleep apnea consists of repeated pauses or reductions in breathing during sleep. Obstructive apnea results from upper-airway collapse, while central apnea reflects reduced respiratory drive; untreated disease can cause daytime sleepiness, impaired concentration, hypertension, and cardiovascular risk.

\medterm{APS (Antiphospholipid Syndrome)} Alternate index label for Antiphospholipid Syndrome.

\medterm{Are Antiperspirants Harmful} Antiperspirants reduce sweating, usually with aluminum salts, and are generally safe when used on intact skin as directed. Irritation or contact dermatitis can occur; persistent excessive sweating, a new generalized change, or symptoms of illness deserves medical evaluation.

\medterm{Are Bananas Bad for Kidneys?} Bananas are not universally harmful to the kidneys, but they contain potassium. People with advanced kidney disease or medicines that raise potassium may need individualized portions and laboratory monitoring rather than making dietary changes without clinical advice.

\medterm{Are Bananas Bad if You Are Trying to Lose Weight?} Bananas can fit a weight-loss eating pattern because they provide carbohydrate, fiber, and nutrients, but portion size and total energy intake determine weight change. Pairing fruit with a balanced meal or protein may improve satiety.

\medterm{Are Bananas Fattening for You?} Bananas are not inherently fattening; body weight changes when average energy intake exceeds expenditure. They contain natural carbohydrate and calories, so serving size matters, especially in calorie-restricted diets or diabetes meal planning.

\medterm{Are Bananas Good for Diabetes} People with diabetes can often include bananas in portions that fit their carbohydrate plan. Ripeness, serving size, total meal composition, glucose-lowering treatment, and individual glucose response influence their effect on blood sugar.

\medterm{Are Bean Sprouts Good for You} Bean sprouts provide fiber and micronutrients but can harbor Salmonella, E. coli, or other pathogens because they grow in warm, moist conditions. Cooking sprouts thoroughly lowers infection risk, especially for pregnant people, children, older adults, and immunocompromised people.

\medterm{Are Bedbugs Contagious} Bedbugs do not spread from person to person like an infection, but they can travel in clothing, luggage, bedding, and furniture. Bites cause itchy skin reactions in some people; control requires treating the environment rather than treating the person for contagion.

\medterm{Are Belly Bands Safe During Pregnancy} A pregnancy belly band may temporarily support the abdomen or reduce pelvic and back discomfort when properly fitted. It should not be tight enough to impair breathing, circulation, digestion, or fetal movement, and persistent pain or swelling needs obstetric evaluation.

\medterm{Are Blood Clots Normal in Early Pregnancy} Small clots or spotting in early pregnancy are not automatically normal and can occur with benign cervical changes, miscarriage, or ectopic pregnancy. Heavy bleeding, one-sided pain, shoulder pain, dizziness, or fainting requires urgent assessment.

\medterm{Are Breast Implants Worth It?} Whether breast implants are worthwhile depends on goals, expected appearance, health, cost, and willingness to accept revision surgery and implant-related risks. Complications include capsular contracture, rupture, infection, changes in sensation, and rare implant-associated malignancy.

\medterm{Are C-Sections Really That Bad} Cesarean delivery can be lifesaving and appropriate, but it is major abdominal surgery with risks such as infection, bleeding, blood clots, organ injury, and longer recovery. It can also affect risks in later pregnancies; decisions depend on maternal and fetal circumstances.

\medterm{Are Canker Sores Contagious} Canker sores are shallow ulcers inside the mouth and are not contagious. They may be associated with minor trauma, stress, nutritional deficiency, immune disease, or other triggers; unusually large, frequent, persistent, or systemic-associated ulcers need evaluation.

\medterm{Are Carrots Healthy for Babies?} Carrots can provide fiber and beta-carotene for babies when introduced in an age-appropriate, soft form. Choking risk depends on texture and shape, so whole raw pieces are unsafe for young children; introduce foods according to developmental readiness.

\medterm{Are Collagen Supplements Good for You?} Collagen supplements provide amino-acid peptides that may modestly affect skin elasticity or joint symptoms in some studies, but evidence and product quality vary. They do not replace adequate dietary protein, and allergies, contaminants, cost, and medicine interactions should be considered.

\medterm{Are Contact Lenses Better Than Glasses?} Contact lenses provide a wider field of view and may be preferred for sports or appearance, while glasses are easier to maintain and avoid direct corneal infection risk. The better option depends on prescription, eye health, comfort, hygiene, and ability to follow care instructions.

\medterm{Are Contact Lenses Safe?} Contact lenses are generally safe with proper fitting, hand hygiene, cleaning, replacement, and wear time. Sleeping or swimming in lenses and using contaminated solutions raises the risk of microbial keratitis, which can threaten vision and requires urgent care for pain, redness, or light sensitivity.

\medterm{Are CPAP Machines Worth It} Continuous positive airway pressure can reduce obstructive sleep-apnea events, improve oxygenation and daytime alertness, and lower some health risks when used consistently. Benefit depends on correct diagnosis, mask fit, comfort, adherence, and follow-up adjustment.

\medterm{Are Dental Implants Worth It} Dental implants can restore chewing and support a crown, bridge, or denture after tooth loss. Success depends on bone, gums, smoking, diabetes control, oral hygiene, surgical risk, time, and cost; complications include infection, bone loss, nerve injury, and implant failure.

\medterm{Are Dimples Rare?} Facial dimples are common inherited variations caused by differences in the muscles or connective tissue beneath the skin. They are usually harmless and do not indicate a medical disorder.

\medterm{Are Earwigs Harmful to Humans} Earwigs are not known to crawl into human ears as a normal behavior and are generally harmless. They can pinch defensively, but they do not have venom; a bite or pinch should be cleaned and observed for infection.

\medterm{Are Eggs Bad for Pancreatitis} Eggs are not automatically prohibited after pancreatitis, but tolerance depends on disease severity and dietary fat. During recovery, clinicians may recommend a gradual return to lower-fat meals; persistent pain, vomiting, or inability to eat needs medical review.

\medterm{Are Eggs Good for an Upset Stomach} Plain, well-cooked eggs may be tolerated during recovery from some stomach illnesses and provide protein, but they can worsen symptoms for people with particular food intolerance, reflux, gallbladder disease, or infection. Hydration and the underlying cause remain more important than one food.

\medterm{Are Gastric Bypasses Reversible?} Gastric bypass anatomy can sometimes be revised, but reversal is complex and not routine. It may be considered for serious complications or specific nutritional problems and carries operative risks; long-term dietary and supplement needs continue to require monitoring.

\medterm{Are Ground-Glass Opacities Common} Ground-glass opacity is a hazy area on chest CT in which vessels remain partly visible. It is a radiologic finding rather than a diagnosis and can reflect infection, inflammation, edema, hemorrhage, fibrosis, or early malignancy; interpretation depends on pattern and clinical context.

\medterm{Are Heated Blankets Bad for You} Heated blankets are generally safe when used according to instructions, but burns, overheating, fire, and electrical injury can occur. People with reduced sensation, limited mobility, or inability to respond to heat should use extra caution or avoid them.

\medterm{Are Hiccups Good or Bad} Hiccups are involuntary diaphragm contractions that are usually brief and harmless. Persistent or recurrent hiccups can result from irritation, medication, metabolic disease, or nervous-system disorders and may interfere with eating, sleeping, or breathing.

\medterm{Are Hives (Urticaria) Contagious} Alternate index label for Urticaria) Contagiou.

\medterm{Are Hives and Rash the Same Thing} Hives are a specific type of rash consisting of raised, itchy welts that usually move or fade within 24 hours. Other rashes may involve scales, blisters, bruising, or fixed lesions, so appearance and duration help identify the cause.

\medterm{Are Katydids Dangerous?} Katydids are generally harmless insects and do not normally attack people. Contact may cause a minor pinch or local irritation, but severe reactions are unusual; rapidly spreading swelling, breathing trouble, or systemic symptoms need emergency care.

\medterm{Are Loofahs Bad for You?} Loofahs can harbor bacteria and fungi when kept damp and may irritate or abrade skin. Rinse and dry them thoroughly, replace them regularly, and avoid sharing or using them on broken, infected, or freshly shaved skin.

\medterm{Are Menstrual Cups Bad for You} Menstrual cups are generally safe when correctly fitted, cleaned, and emptied on schedule. Possible problems include irritation, pain, leakage, difficult removal, and rare toxic shock syndrome; use during an intrauterine-device placement should be discussed with a clinician.

\medterm{Are Pregnancy Pillows Good for Back Pain?} Pregnancy pillows can support the abdomen, hips, or knees and may improve sleep-related back or pelvic discomfort. They do not treat serious causes of pain; weakness, numbness, fever, bleeding, contractions, or severe persistent pain needs obstetric advice.

\medterm{Are Rubella and German Measles the Same Thing} Rubella and German measles are the same viral infection. It is usually mild in children and adults but can cause miscarriage, fetal death, or congenital rubella syndrome when infection occurs during pregnancy; vaccination prevents it.

\medterm{Are Shirataki Noodles Bad for You?} Shirataki noodles contain glucomannan fiber and are generally safe in appropriate portions with adequate fluid. Large amounts may cause bloating, diarrhea, choking, or obstruction, and they can affect absorption of medicines if taken too close together.

\medterm{Are Skin Rashes Contagious} Some rashes spread through infection, while others result from allergy, irritation, inflammation, medication, or internal disease and are not contagious. Fever, rapid spread, mucosal involvement, severe pain, blistering, or breathing symptoms warrants prompt evaluation.

\medterm{Are Smelly Farts Healthy? Types of Farts} Intestinal gas and odor are normal results of swallowed air and bacterial fermentation of food. A sudden persistent change with weight loss, bleeding, severe pain, vomiting, fever, or altered bowel habits can indicate gastrointestinal disease.

\medterm{Are Strawberries Good for Your Stomach?} Strawberries provide fiber and vitamin C and are tolerated by many people. Their acidity, seeds, or fermentable carbohydrates can aggravate reflux, allergy, or bowel symptoms in some individuals, so response and portion size matter.

\medterm{Are Warts Caused by Being Dirty} Warts are caused by infection with human papillomavirus, not by being dirty. The virus enters small breaks in skin and spreads through contact or shared surfaces; hygiene can reduce spread but does not imply blame.

\medterm{Are Warts Contagious?} Warts can spread through direct contact, shared items, or self-inoculation from one body site to another. Avoid picking them, keep them covered when appropriate, and do not share towels, nail tools, or footwear.

\medterm{Are Widow’s Peaks Attractive} A widow's peak is a V-shaped hairline variant with no established medical significance. Its appearance is a normal trait and does not reliably indicate personality, health, or genetic disease.

\medterm{Are You Too Sick to Work} Whether illness warrants staying home depends on symptoms, contagiousness, ability to function safely, job demands, and workplace policy. Fever, vomiting, significant diarrhea, uncontrolled respiratory symptoms, or a positive contagious-infection test commonly support staying home and seeking advice.

\medterm{Arrest Cardiac (Sudden Cardiac Death)} Alternate index label for Sudden Cardiac Death.

\medterm{Arsenic Poisoning} Arsenic poisoning results from acute or chronic exposure to inorganic or organic arsenic. It can cause gastrointestinal illness, cardiovascular instability, neuropathy, characteristic skin changes, anemia, and increased cancer risk; suspected exposure requires poison-control or emergency guidance.

\medterm{Arthritis Ankylosing Spondylitis (Ankylosing Spondylitis)} Alternate index label for Ankylosing Spondylitis.

\medterm{Arthritis Degenerative (Osteoarthritis)} Alternate index label for Osteoarthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Gout (Gout (Gouty Arthritis))} Alternate index label for Gout (Gouty Arthritis).

\medterm{Arthritis Infectious (Septic Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Septic Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Juvenile (Juvenile Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Juvenile Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Lyme (Lyme Disease)} Alternate index label for Lyme Disease.

\medterm{Arthritis MCTD (Mixed Connective Tissue Disease)} Alternate index label for Mixed Connective Tissue Disease.

\medterm{Arthritis Plant Thorn (Plant Thorn Synovitis)} Alternate index label for Plant Thorn Synovitis.

\medterm{Arthritis Pseudogout (Pseudogout)} Alternate index label for Pseudogout.

\medterm{Arthritis Psoriatic (Psoriatic Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Psoriatic Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Quackery (Quackery Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Quackery Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Reactive (Reactive Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Reactive Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Reiters (Reactive Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Reactive Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Sarcoid (Sarcoidosis)} Alternate index label for Sarcoidosis.

\medterm{Arthritis Scleroderma (Scleroderma)} Alternate index label for Scleroderma.

\medterm{Arthritis Sjogrens Syndrome (Sjogren's Syndrome)} Alternate index label for Sjogren's Syndrome.

\medterm{Arthritis SLE (Systemic Lupus)} Alternate index label for Systemic Lupus.

\medterm{Arthritis Still (Still's Disease)} Alternate index label for Still's Disease.

\medterm{Asbestos-Related Disorders} Asbestos exposure can cause pleural plaques, diffuse pleural thickening, asbestosis, lung cancer, and mesothelioma after a long latency. Risk increases with cumulative exposure and smoking, and evaluation may include occupational history, imaging, and lung-function testing.

\medterm{Aspergillus Infection (Aspergillosis)} Alternate index label for Aspergillosis.

\medterm{Asthma Adult-Onset (Adult-Onset Asthma)} Alternate index label for Adult-Onset Asthma.

\medterm{Asthma Complexities} Asthma varies in trigger, severity, airway inflammation, and response to treatment. Difficult-to-control symptoms may reflect poor inhaler technique, ongoing exposure, incorrect diagnosis, comorbid disease, or severe asthma requiring specialist assessment.

\medterm{Asthma Medications} Asthma medicines include controller treatments that reduce airway inflammation and reliever treatments that quickly relax airway muscle. The regimen is guided by symptom frequency, lung function, exacerbation risk, age, pregnancy, technique, and response.

\medterm{Asthma OTC medication (Asthma: Over The Counter Treatment)} Alternate index label for Asthma: Over The Counter Treatment.

\medterm{Asthma Overview} Asthma is a chronic disorder of variable airway inflammation and narrowing that causes wheezing, cough, chest tightness, or breathlessness. Symptoms fluctuate with triggers, and management combines an individualized action plan, inhaled medicines, trigger reduction, and monitoring.

\medterm{Asthma vs. COPD (COPD vs. Asthma Differences and Similarities)} Alternate index label for COPD vs. Asthma Differences and Similarities.

\medterm{Asthma: Over The Counter Treatment} Over-the-counter products do not replace prescription asthma evaluation or a rescue plan. Some may temporarily relieve symptoms but can delay treatment of dangerous airway obstruction; worsening breathlessness, difficulty speaking, cyanosis, or poor response to a reliever is an emergency.

\medterm{Astigmatism Overview} Astigmatism is an irregular curvature of the cornea or lens that prevents light from focusing at one retinal point. It can cause blurred or distorted vision, eyestrain, and headaches and is corrected with glasses, contact lenses, or selected procedures.

\medterm{At What Age Do Late Talkers Talk} Late talking varies widely, but a child with delayed words, gestures, understanding, or social communication should be assessed rather than watched indefinitely. Hearing testing and developmental evaluation can identify language disorder, autism, hearing loss, or other causes and support early therapy.

\medterm{At What Age Does Gender Identity Develop} Gender identity is a person's internal sense of gender and develops through a combination of biological, developmental, and social factors. Children may express a gender identity early, and supportive, nonjudgmental care is appropriate regardless of whether identity changes over time.

\medterm{At What Age Is a Parent Not Legally Responsible} Legal responsibility for a child varies by jurisdiction, age, custody arrangement, and circumstances such as disability or emancipation. Medical consent and financial responsibility may follow different rules, so local legal or social-service advice is needed for a specific case.

\medterm{At What Age is Co-Sleeping Safe with Your Baby} The safest sleep arrangement for an infant is a separate, firm, flat sleep surface in the same room as a caregiver, without pillows, loose bedding, or soft objects. Bed-sharing carries increased suffocation and sleep-related death risk, especially for young infants and when adults smoke, drink, or use sedating drugs.

\medterm{At What Age Should You Stop Birth Control?} Birth control is usually continued until pregnancy is no longer possible, which is commonly around menopause but varies by method and health history. Stopping requires a plan for pregnancy prevention or conception and review of bleeding, migraine, clot, cancer, and medication risks.

\medterm{At What Stage Is CLL Treated?} Chronic lymphocytic leukemia is often observed when it is asymptomatic and stable. Treatment is generally started for progressive anemia or thrombocytopenia, enlarging disease, significant symptoms, rapid progression, or complications, using blood counts, examination, genetics, and symptoms to guide timing.

\medterm{At What Stage of Cancer is Chemotherapy Used} Chemotherapy may be used before surgery or radiation, after local treatment, as the main treatment for disseminated disease, or for symptom control. The stage and regimen depend on cancer type, molecular features, treatment goals, organ function, and patient preferences.

\medterm{Athletes Foot (Athlete's Foot)} Alternate index label for Athlete's Foot.

\medterm{Atopic Dermatitis vs Contact Dermatitis} Atopic dermatitis is a chronic eczema associated with skin-barrier dysfunction and an atopic tendency, while contact dermatitis follows exposure to an irritant or allergen. Distribution, exposure history, recurrence, and patch testing can help distinguish them; both require skin protection and targeted treatment.

\medterm{Atopic Dermatitis vs Psoriasis} Atopic dermatitis commonly causes intensely itchy, poorly defined eczema in flexural areas, while psoriasis produces well-demarcated thicker plaques with silvery scale and may involve nails or extensor surfaces. Overlap occurs, so examination and history guide diagnosis.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation (AFib) Warning Symptoms} Alternate index label for AFib) Warning Symptom.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation AFib Treatment Drugs} Medicines for atrial fibrillation may slow the ventricular rate, restore or maintain rhythm, or reduce stroke risk with anticoagulation. Choice depends on symptoms, heart structure, kidney function, bleeding risk, other diseases, and whether rhythm control is appropriate.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation vs. Atrial Flutter (Atrial Flutter vs. Atrial Fibrillation)} Alternate index label for Atrial Flutter vs. Atrial Fibrillation.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation vs. Ventricular Fibrillation} Atrial fibrillation is a rapid, irregular atrial rhythm that may allow the ventricles to pump, whereas ventricular fibrillation is chaotic ventricular activity that stops effective circulation. Ventricular fibrillation causes cardiac arrest and requires immediate defibrillation and resuscitation.

\medterm{Atrial Flutter vs. Atrial Fibrillation} Atrial flutter usually has a rapid organized atrial circuit with characteristic sawtooth activity, while atrial fibrillation has chaotic atrial activation and an irregular ventricular response. Both can cause palpitations and stroke risk and may require rate control, rhythm treatment, and anticoagulation.

\medterm{Atrophy Vaginal (Vaginal Dryness and Vaginal Atrophy)} Alternate index label for Vaginal Dryness and Vaginal Atrophy.

\medterm{Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder (Adult ADHD)} Alternate index label for Adult ADHD.

\medterm{Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder in Teens} ADHD in teenagers causes persistent inattention, impulsivity, or hyperactivity that interferes with school, relationships, driving, or organization. Assessment includes childhood history and screening for learning disorders, sleep problems, substance use, anxiety, depression, and other conditions.

\medterm{Autism and Communication} Autism can affect social communication, language development, nonverbal interaction, and the use of communication for shared attention or relationships. Individualized speech-language therapy, visual supports, augmentative communication, and respectful environmental accommodations can improve participation.

\medterm{Autonomic Neuropathy Diabetic (Diabetic Neuropathy)} Alternate index label for Diabetic Neuropathy.

\medterm{Avascular necrosis (Aseptic Necrosis)} Alternate index label for Aseptic Necrosis.

\medterm{Average Life Expectancy After a Liver Transplant?} Life expectancy after liver transplantation varies with the original disease, recipient health, graft function, rejection, infection, cancer recurrence, and adherence to follow-up. Lifelong immunosuppression, surveillance, preventive care, and management of cardiovascular and metabolic risks are essential.

\medterm{Avian Flu (Avian Influenza (Bird Flu))} Avian influenza is infection with influenza A viruses adapted primarily to birds. Human infection is uncommon but can be severe after contact with infected birds or mammals; exposure history, respiratory symptoms, and public-health testing guide evaluation and treatment.

\medterm{Avian Influenza (Bird Flu)} Avian influenza is infection with influenza A viruses adapted primarily to birds. Human infection is uncommon but can be severe after contact with infected birds or mammals; exposure history, respiratory symptoms, and public-health testing guide evaluation and treatment.

\medterm{A (adenine)} In genetics, A denotes adenine, a nucleobase that pairs with thymine in DNA and with uracil in RNA.

\medterm{A-T} A-T is the adenine--thymine base pair in DNA, formed by specific hydrogen bonding between the two complementary bases.

\medterm{a.c.} A prescription abbreviation meaning “before meals,” from the Latin ante cibum.

\medterm{AAA} Abdominal aortic aneurysm: an abnormal enlargement or ballooning of the abdominal aorta that can rupture and cause life-threatening internal bleeding.

\medterm{AAAS} The American Association for the Advancement of Science, a scientific organization that publishes the journal Science and supports research and science education.

\medterm{AAD} The American Academy of Dermatology, a professional organization representing dermatologists and advancing dermatologic care, education, and research.

\medterm{AAO} An abbreviation that may mean alert and oriented or refer to an ophthalmology, otolaryngology, or orthodontics professional organization; the clinical context determines the intended meaning.

\medterm{AAP} The American Academy of Pediatrics, a professional organization focused on the health and well-being of infants, children, adolescents, and young adults.

\medterm{Aarskog-Scott syndrome} A rare inherited developmental disorder, often related to FGD1 variants, characterized by distinctive facial features, short stature, skeletal differences, and genital anomalies.

\medterm{Aase-Smith syndrome I} A rare congenital-malformation syndrome associated with hydrocephalus, cleft palate, severe joint contractures, distinctive hand and facial findings, and sometimes heart defects.

\medterm{Aase-Smith syndrome II} A rare inherited disorder characterized by hypoplastic anemia and triphalangeal thumbs, in which the thumbs contain three phalanges rather than the usual two.

\medterm{Ab-} A medical prefix meaning away from, off, or separating from, as in abduction (movement away from the midline) and ablation (removal or destruction of tissue).

\medterm{Abate} To become less intense or to reduce in severity, frequency, or amount; symptoms, inflammation, pain, or fever may abate.

\medterm{Abatement} A reduction in the severity, intensity, frequency, or amount of a symptom, disease process, exposure, or other condition.

\medterm{Abdomen, acute} Acute abdomen is sudden or severe abdominal pain with possible tenderness, guarding, vomiting, distention, or altered bowel function that may indicate a surgical or other urgent condition.

\medterm{Abdominal} Relating to the abdomen, the body region between the chest and pelvis that contains organs of the digestive, urinary, and reproductive systems.

\medterm{Abdominal guarding} Involuntary or voluntary tensing of the abdominal muscles in response to palpation, often associated with irritation or inflammation of the peritoneum.

\medterm{Abdominal hysterectomy} Surgical removal of the uterus through an incision in the abdominal wall; the cervix, ovaries, and fallopian tubes may or may not also be removed.

\medterm{Abducent nerve} The sixth cranial nerve, also called the abducens nerve, which supplies the lateral rectus muscle and abducts the eye.

\medterm{Abductor muscle} A muscle that moves a body part away from the midline or, for a digit, away from the axis of the hand or foot.

\medterm{Aberration} A deviation from normal structure, function, or appearance; in optics, it is an imperfection that prevents light from forming a sharply focused image.

\medterm{Abiotic} Nonliving; in biology and medicine, the term describes physical or chemical environmental factors rather than living organisms.

\medterm{Abiotrophy} Progressive degeneration or loss of function in a tissue or organ, often due to an inherited or developmental defect rather than an acquired injury.

\medterm{Ablate} To remove or destroy tissue, usually by surgery, heat, cold, chemicals, or another energy source.

\medterm{Abortive} Incomplete, unsuccessful, or stopped before full development; an abortive infection produces limited disease without the usual complete clinical course.

\medterm{Abortive polio} A mild, nonparalytic poliovirus infection causing nonspecific fever, sore throat, headache, nausea, or malaise without the neurologic findings of paralytic polio.

\medterm{ABR test} Auditory brainstem response testing records electrical activity from the auditory nerve and brainstem through scalp electrodes to assess hearing and auditory-pathway function, especially in infants or people unable to provide reliable behavioral responses.

\medterm{Abrade} To wear away tissue by friction or erosion; an abrasion is the resulting scrape or superficial loss of skin.

\medterm{Abs} An informal term for the abdominal muscles, particularly the anterior abdominal-wall muscles.

\medterm{Abscission} Removal of tissue by cutting it away, as in a surgical procedure.


\medterm{Absence of the breast} Congenital absence of breast development, called amastia; it may affect one or both sides and can occur with other developmental anomalies.

\medterm{Absence of the nipple} Athelia: a rare congenital absence of one or both nipples, which may occur with hereditary disorders, chromosomal abnormalities, or developmental syndromes.

\medterm{Absent eye} Anophthalmia, a congenital absence or severe underdevelopment of the eye globe and ocular tissue; it may affect one or both eyes and may occur with other birth defects.

\medterm{Absinthism} A historical disorder attributed to habitual absinthe abuse, associated with alcohol dependence and reported hallucinations, insomnia, tremor, and seizures; the specific contribution of absinthe constituents was uncertain.

\medterm{Absorb} To take in a substance, such as through the skin or intestine, or to reduce the intensity of radiation or light by taking up its energy.

\medterm{Abstinence} Deliberate avoidance of a substance or behavior, especially refraining from alcohol, drugs, tobacco, or sexual activity.

\medterm{AC joint} The acromioclavicular joint, a gliding joint between the acromion of the scapula and the clavicle at the top of the shoulder.

\medterm{Acanthamoeba} A free-living amoeba found in soil and water that can cause keratitis, skin infection, or rarely granulomatous amoebic encephalitis, especially in people with impaired immunity.

\medterm{Acaricide} A pesticide or other agent used to kill mites and related arachnids such as ticks.

\medterm{ACC} Adenoid cystic carcinoma, a malignant tumor that most often arises in glandular tissues such as the salivary glands and may grow slowly but invade nerves or recur.

\medterm{Access} In health care, the opportunity to obtain care; in dialysis, vascular access is the site where a needle or catheter enters the bloodstream.

\medterm{Accessory} Additional or supplementary; in anatomy, an accessory structure is an extra part or a structure that assists the usual organ or system.

\medterm{Accessory digestive organ} An organ that supports digestion but is not part of the alimentary canal, such as the liver, pancreas, gallbladder, or salivary glands.

\medterm{Accessory dwelling unit} A secondary, independent residential unit on the same property as a primary home; it is a housing and community-health term rather than a disease or body structure.

\medterm{Accessory placenta} An extra placental lobe separate from the main placenta but connected to it by fetal blood vessels; it can increase the risk of retained placental tissue or vessel injury during delivery.

\medterm{Acellular} Lacking cells; an acellular material may consist of extracellular matrix, mineral, fluid, or other components without intact living cells.

\medterm{Acentric chromosome} A chromosome fragment that lacks a centromere and therefore usually cannot attach normally to the spindle during cell division.

\medterm{Acetaminophen} A non-opioid analgesic and antipyretic, also called paracetamol, used to relieve mild-to-moderate pain and reduce fever. It has little clinically useful anti-inflammatory activity. Excessive doses can cause severe, potentially fatal hepatic injury, especially with alcohol misuse, fasting, liver disease, or concurrent use of other acetaminophen-containing products; dosing must therefore follow the product label or a clinician's advice.

\medterm{Acetate} The salt or ester of acetic acid; acetate is also a two-carbon metabolic substrate that can be converted to acetyl-CoA.

\medterm{Acetylcysteine} A cysteine derivative used as a mucolytic and as the antidote for acetaminophen poisoning because it replenishes glutathione and helps detoxify its harmful metabolite.

\medterm{ACh (acetylcholine)} Acetylcholine, a neurotransmitter released at the neuromuscular junction and in autonomic and central nervous systems; it acts through nicotinic and muscarinic receptors.

\medterm{AChE (acetylcholinesterase)} An enzyme that rapidly breaks down acetylcholine into acetate and choline, terminating cholinergic signaling at synapses and neuromuscular junctions.

\medterm{Achillobursitis} Inflammation of a bursa near the Achilles tendon, causing pain, tenderness, swelling, or friction-related discomfort at the back of the heel.

\medterm{Achoo syndrome} The photic sneeze reflex: repeated sneezing triggered by sudden exposure to bright light, especially after entering sunlight from darkness.

\medterm{Acid indigestion} Dyspepsia or upper-abdominal discomfort that may include burning, fullness, belching, or nausea; it can accompany reflux, gastritis, ulcer disease, or other conditions.

\medterm{Acidophilus} Lactobacillus acidophilus, a lactic-acid-producing bacterium found in the intestinal and genital microbiota and used in some fermented foods and probiotic products.

\medterm{Acinus, pulmonary} The portion of a lung supplied by a terminal bronchiole and containing respiratory bronchioles, alveolar ducts, and alveoli where gas exchange occurs.

\medterm{Acou-} A medical combining form referring to hearing or sound, as in acoustic nerve, audiology, and acoumeter.

\medterm{Acoustic} Relating to sound, hearing, or the physical properties and transmission of sound waves.

\medterm{Acquired} Developed after birth rather than being present at birth or inherited; an acquired condition may result from infection, exposure, injury, aging, or another later event.

\medterm{Acquired immunity} Immune protection that develops after infection, vaccination, or transfer of antibodies, involving adaptive immune responses directed toward specific antigens.

\medterm{Acrocentric chromosome} A chromosome whose centromere lies close to one end, producing a very short p arm and a longer q arm; human acrocentric chromosomes include 13, 14, 15, 21, and 22.

\medterm{Acrodynia} A painful condition with red, swollen, sweaty hands and feet, classically associated with mercury poisoning and historically called pink disease in children.

\medterm{Acrophobia} An excessive, persistent fear of heights that causes marked anxiety, avoidance, or impairment beyond the ordinary caution associated with high places.

\medterm{Actinic} Relating to ultraviolet radiation from sunlight or UV lamps; chronic actinic exposure can cause sunburn, photoaging, and actinic keratoses.

\medterm{Acu-} A combining form meaning sharp, pointed, or related to acuity; in medical terms it may refer to sharp sensation or vision, as in acuity, acute, or acupuncture, depending on the word formed.

\medterm{Acute idiopathic polyneuritis} Guillain--Barré syndrome, an acute immune-mediated disorder of peripheral nerves that usually causes progressive symmetric weakness and reduced reflexes and may impair breathing.

\medterm{Acute illness} A disease or health problem that begins suddenly or over a short period and generally lasts a limited time, although its severity may range from mild to life-threatening.

\medterm{Acute mountain sickness (AMS)} A high-altitude illness caused by insufficient acclimatization, typically producing headache with nausea, dizziness, fatigue, or sleep disturbance after ascent.

\medterm{Acute myocardial infarction} Sudden death of heart muscle caused by prolonged inadequate coronary blood flow, usually from an acutely obstructed coronary artery; it is a medical emergency.

\medterm{Acute thrombocytopenic purpura} An acute disorder with a markedly reduced platelet count and bleeding or purpura, historically used for immune thrombocytopenic purpura, often following an infection in children.

\medterm{Acyclovir} An antiviral drug that inhibits herpesvirus DNA polymerase and is used for infections caused by herpes simplex virus and varicella-zoster virus.

\medterm{Ad lib} A prescription or clinical instruction meaning as desired or as tolerated, from the Latin ad libitum.

\medterm{Ad-} A medical prefix meaning toward, to, or near, as in adduction (movement toward the midline) and adrenal (near the kidney).

\medterm{ADA deficiency} An inherited lack of adenosine deaminase activity that causes toxic purine metabolites to accumulate and can produce severe combined immunodeficiency affecting both T- and B-cell function.

\medterm{Adamantine} Hard or resembling diamond; in dentistry, adamantine refers to enamel or enamel-like tissue.

\medterm{ADCC (antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotox.)} Antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity, in which immune effector cells such as natural killer cells recognize antibodies attached to a target cell and destroy it.

\medterm{ADD (attention deficit disorder)} An older term for attention-deficit disorder, now generally classified within attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, especially presentations dominated by inattention.

\medterm{Addison anemia} An older name for pernicious anemia, a vitamin B12-deficiency megaloblastic anemia caused by inadequate intrinsic factor and impaired B12 absorption.

\medterm{Addisonian anemia} Another historical term for pernicious anemia, in which autoimmune loss of gastric parietal-cell function or intrinsic factor causes vitamin B12 deficiency.

\medterm{Additive genetic effects} The contributions of individual alleles that combine approximately additively to influence a measurable trait, without requiring interaction between those alleles.

\medterm{Adductor muscle} A muscle that moves a body part toward the body’s midline or, for a digit, toward the axis of the hand or foot.

\medterm{Aden-} A combining form meaning gland or glandular tissue, as in adenitis and adenoma.

\medterm{Adeno-} A combining form referring to a gland or glandular tissue, as in adenovirus, adenoid, and adenocarcinoma.

\medterm{Adenoiditis} Inflammation or infection of the adenoids, the pharyngeal tonsil behind the nose, which may cause nasal obstruction, mouth breathing, snoring, or ear problems.

\medterm{Adenopathy} Disease or enlargement of a gland, most often used for lymph-node enlargement or abnormal lymph-node involvement.

\medterm{Adenosine} A purine nucleoside made of adenine linked to ribose; it participates in cellular energy transfer and signaling and can transiently slow atrioventricular-node conduction when given medically.

\medterm{Adenosine deaminase (ADA) deficiency} An inherited purine-metabolism disorder in which toxic deoxyadenosine metabolites accumulate and damage lymphocytes, commonly causing severe combined immunodeficiency.

\medterm{ADHD (Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder)} A neurodevelopmental disorder involving persistent inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity that begins in childhood and impairs functioning in more than one setting.

\medterm{ADI} Acceptable daily intake: the estimated amount of a substance that can be consumed every day over a lifetime without appreciable health risk, based on available evidence and safety factors.

\medterm{Adipose} Relating to body fat or composed of adipose tissue, which stores energy and also functions as an endocrine and immune-active organ.

\medterm{Adiposity} The amount or distribution of body fat; excess adiposity is associated with increased risk of metabolic, cardiovascular, and mechanical disease.

\medterm{Adjacent} Next to, close to, or sharing a boundary with another structure, tissue, organ, or lesion.

\medterm{Adjuvant} An added treatment or substance that enhances the effect of a primary treatment; in vaccines, an adjuvant strengthens the immune response to an antigen.

\medterm{ADLs (activities of daily living)} Basic self-care activities such as bathing, dressing, toileting, transferring, eating, and maintaining continence; ADL ability is used to assess functional independence.

\medterm{Admission} The process of accepting a patient into a hospital or other facility for evaluation, treatment, monitoring, or care.

\medterm{Adrenal failure} Inadequate production of adrenal hormones, especially cortisol and sometimes aldosterone; severe acute deficiency can cause adrenal crisis with hypotension and shock.

\medterm{Adrenaline} Epinephrine, a hormone and neurotransmitter released during stress that increases heart rate and contractility, constricts some blood vessels, and relaxes bronchial muscle.

\medterm{ADU} Accessory dwelling unit, a secondary independent residential unit located on the same property as a primary home.

\medterm{Adult primary liver cancer} Cancer that begins in the liver of an adult, most commonly hepatocellular carcinoma or intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma, rather than cancer that has spread to the liver from another organ.

\medterm{Advance directives} Written instructions about a person’s future medical-care preferences and/or designation of a health-care proxy if the person later cannot make or communicate decisions.

\medterm{Adventitious} Abnormal, unexpected, or arising from an unusual location or process; adventitious lung sounds include crackles, wheezes, and other added breath sounds.

\medterm{Adverse effect} An unintended and harmful or bothersome effect of a medicine or other treatment given at normal doses or for an intended purpose.

\medterm{Aer-, aero-} A combining form meaning air or gas, as in aerobic, aerosol, and aerophagia.

\medterm{Aerophobia} An excessive fear of flying or air travel that causes significant anxiety, avoidance, or impairment.

\medterm{Aerotitis} Ear pain or middle-ear injury caused by failure to equalize pressure during rapid changes in altitude, such as during aircraft ascent or descent.

\medterm{Aesculapius} The Roman name for Asclepius, the Greek god of healing; the name is associated with medicine and the rod-and-serpent medical symbol.

\medterm{Aetiology} The cause or causes of a disease, injury, symptom, or other medical condition; also spelled etiology.

\medterm{Affect} The observable expression of a person’s emotional state, including facial expression, voice, posture, and emotional responsiveness.

\medterm{Afferent} Carrying signals toward a central organ or structure; afferent nerves transmit sensory information from the body toward the brain and spinal cord.

\medterm{Affinity} The strength of attraction or binding between two molecules, such as an antibody and antigen, receptor and ligand, or enzyme and substrate.

\medterm{AFIP} The Armed Forces Institute of Pathology, a former United States institution known for consultation, education, and research in diagnostic pathology.

\medterm{AFO (ankle-foot orthosis)} A brace that supports the ankle and foot, helps control alignment and motion, and may improve walking in people with weakness, spasticity, instability, or foot drop.

\medterm{Afterbirth} The placenta and fetal membranes expelled from the uterus after delivery of the baby.

\medterm{Age by decade} Classification of a person’s age into ten-year groups, such as the twenties, thirties, or seventies, for demographic description or analysis.

\medterm{Agent, tocolytic} A medicine that reduces uterine contractions, used selectively to delay preterm birth long enough for corticosteroids, transfer, or other preparations to take effect.

\medterm{Aggressive} Characterized by forceful or hostile behavior, or by rapid growth and invasive or destructive clinical behavior, depending on context.

\medterm{Agita} An informal term for heartburn, indigestion, or an uneasy feeling of stomach discomfort; its meaning can vary by region and context.

\medterm{Ague} A fever, often associated with malaria, marked by recurrent episodes of chills, fever, and sweating; the term can also mean a fit of shivering.

\medterm{Agyrophobia} An excessive, persistent fear of crossing streets, highways, or other thoroughfares, causing anxiety or avoidance despite limited objective danger.

\medterm{AHD} An abbreviation that may mean alveolar hydatid disease, American Hospital Directory, or atherosclerotic heart disease; the clinical context determines the meaning.

\medterm{AID (artificial insemination by donor)} Intrauterine insemination using sperm from a donor other than the intended mother’s partner to attempt fertilization and pregnancy.

\medterm{AIH (artificial insemination by husband)} Intrauterine insemination using sperm from the woman’s male partner; it is also called homologous insemination.

\medterm{Air bag} An inflatable vehicle safety device that rapidly deploys during a collision to reduce impact with the steering wheel, dashboard, or window; it supplements seat belts.

\medterm{Air-conditioner lung} Hypersensitivity pneumonitis caused by inhaled mold, fungus, or other contaminants from air-conditioning or humidifying systems.

\medterm{AK} Actinic keratosis, a rough, scaly precancerous skin lesion caused by chronic ultraviolet exposure.

\medterm{AKA (above the knee amputation)} Amputation of the lower limb through the thigh above the knee joint, usually performed for severe vascular disease, infection, trauma, or cancer.

\medterm{Akinetic} Characterized by little or no voluntary movement; akinesia may result from neurologic disease, medication effects, or severe motor impairment.

\medterm{ALA} An abbreviation for alpha-linolenic acid, an essential omega-3 fatty acid, or aminolevulinic acid, a heme-pathway compound used topically in some photodynamic treatments; context determines the meaning.

\medterm{Alb-} A prefix derived from the Latin albus, meaning white, as in albino and albinism.

\medterm{Albinism, oculocutaneous} An inherited disorder of melanin production affecting the skin, hair, and eyes, often causing light sensitivity, nystagmus, reduced visual acuity, and increased sun sensitivity.

\medterm{Albino} A person or animal with albinism; person-first language such as “person with albinism” is generally preferred.

\medterm{Albumen} The white of an egg, composed largely of water and proteins; it is distinct from albumin, the major protein in blood plasma.

\medterm{Alembic} A distillation apparatus historically used in chemistry and laboratory work to separate or purify substances by differences in volatility.

\medterm{algia} A word ending meaning pain, as in neuralgia, myalgia, and arthralgia.

\medterm{Alimentary} Relating to food, nourishment, or the organs and processes of digestion.

\medterm{Alkaloid} A naturally occurring nitrogen-containing organic compound, often produced by plants, with potentially potent physiologic or pharmacologic effects.

\medterm{Allelic} Relating to an allele, one of two or more alternative forms of a gene at a particular genomic location.

\medterm{Allergic contact dermatitis} A delayed, T-cell-mediated skin reaction after contact with a substance to which the person has become sensitized, causing itching, redness, swelling, and sometimes blisters or scaling.

\medterm{Allogeneic} Derived from a genetically different individual of the same species, as in an allogeneic blood, tissue, stem-cell, or organ transplant.

\medterm{Allopathic medicine} A historical term commonly used for conventional, evidence-based medical practice; it is not a precise scientific classification and may be used differently by different groups.

\medterm{Allopathy} A historical label for conventional medical treatment, often contrasted with homeopathy; modern clinical practice is more accurately described by its specific methods and evidence.

\medterm{Alongshan Virus (ALSV)} A tick-associated segmented RNA virus reported to cause a febrile illness, often with headache, fatigue, nausea, or neurologic symptoms; its epidemiology and clinical significance remain under study.

\medterm{Alprazolam} A short- to intermediate-acting benzodiazepine used mainly for anxiety and panic disorder; it can cause sedation, dependence, withdrawal, and dangerous respiratory depression with opioids or other depressants.

\medterm{ALT (alanine aminotransferase)} An enzyme concentrated in liver cells; an increased blood ALT level may indicate hepatocellular injury but must be interpreted with other tests and clinical findings.

\medterm{Alternative medicine} A nonconventional health practice used instead of standard medical treatment; evidence, safety, and interactions vary widely, and replacing effective care can be harmful.

\medterm{Altitude sickness} Illness caused by rapid ascent to high altitude without adequate acclimatization, ranging from acute mountain sickness to life-threatening pulmonary or cerebral edema.

\medterm{Alveolar} Relating to an alveolus, especially one of the tiny air sacs in the lungs where oxygen and carbon dioxide are exchanged.

\medterm{Alveolar hydatid disease} A serious parasitic infection caused by the larval stage of Echinococcus multilocularis, usually producing an infiltrative, tumor-like lesion in the liver that can spread to other organs.

\medterm{Alveolitis} Inflammation of the pulmonary alveoli, usually causing cough and breathlessness, or inflammation of a tooth socket after extraction, called alveolar osteitis or dry socket; the organ involved distinguishes the two meanings.

\medterm{Amastia} Congenital absence of one or both breasts, sometimes accompanied by absence of the nipple or pectoral muscles and other developmental anomalies.

\medterm{Amathophobia} An excessive or persistent fear of dust that causes significant anxiety, avoidance, or impairment.

\medterm{Ambidextrous} Able to use the right and left hands with approximately equal skill or preference.

\medterm{Ambient} Surrounding or present in the immediate environment, as in ambient temperature, light, noise, or air.

\medterm{AMC (arthrogryposis multiplex congenita)} A group of congenital conditions characterized by multiple joint contractures present at birth, often with muscle weakness or abnormal development of nerves, muscles, or connective tissue.

\medterm{Amelioration} Improvement or reduction in the severity of a symptom, disease, impairment, or other adverse condition.

\medterm{American Academy of Pediatrics} A professional organization of pediatricians that promotes the health and well-being of infants, children, adolescents, and young adults through clinical guidance, education, advocacy, and research.

\medterm{American Dental Association (ADA)} A professional organization representing dentistry and supporting oral-health education, standards, advocacy, and practice resources in the United States.

\medterm{AMI} Acute myocardial infarction, the sudden death of heart muscle caused by prolonged inadequate coronary blood flow.

\medterm{Amino acid symbols} Standard one-letter and three-letter abbreviations used to identify the 20 common protein-building amino acids in biochemical and genetic notation.

\medterm{Aminotransferase} An enzyme that transfers an amino group between an amino acid and an alpha-keto acid; clinically measured examples include ALT and AST.

\medterm{Amitriptyline} A tricyclic antidepressant that affects serotonin and norepinephrine signaling and is also used for selected chronic pain, migraine-prevention, and sleep-related indications.

\medterm{AML} Acute myeloid leukemia, an aggressive cancer of immature myeloid blood-cell precursors that crowds the bone marrow and impairs normal blood-cell production.

\medterm{Amok} A historical culture-related term for a sudden episode of uncontrolled, often violent behavior followed by exhaustion or amnesia; modern assessment considers psychiatric, neurologic, substance-related, and cultural factors.

\medterm{Amphetamine} A central nervous system stimulant that increases catecholamine signaling and is used in selected attention-deficit/hyperactivity and sleep disorders; misuse can cause dependence and cardiovascular or psychiatric harm.

\medterm{Ampulla} A widened portion of a tubular structure, such as the ampulla of the bile and pancreatic ducts or the ampulla of a fallopian tube.

\medterm{Amusia} An acquired or developmental inability to perceive, recognize, produce, or understand music despite otherwise adequate hearing and language ability.

\medterm{Amygdaloid nucleus} A group of nuclei in the medial temporal lobe involved in processing emotional significance, fear responses, learning, memory, and autonomic responses.

\medterm{Amyl-} A combining form referring to starch or a starch-like substance, as in amylase and amyloid.

\medterm{Amylo-} A combining form referring to starch-like material or amyloid protein deposits.

\medterm{An-} A prefix meaning without, lacking, or not, as in anoxia, anesthesia, and anencephaly.

\medterm{ANA} Antinuclear antibody, an autoantibody directed against components of cell nuclei; ANA testing can support evaluation of autoimmune disease but is not diagnostic by itself.

\medterm{Anaesthesia} Reversible loss of sensation, with or without loss of consciousness, produced by medicines or other techniques; also spelled anesthesia.

\medterm{Anal papilla} A small normal projection of mucosa at the anal canal’s dentate line, where columnar and squamous epithelium meet.

\medterm{Anal papillae} Multiple small mucosal projections at the dentate line of the anal canal; they are usually normal but may become enlarged or symptomatic.

\medterm{Analog} A structure, substance, or process that resembles another and performs a similar function but is not necessarily chemically or structurally identical.

\medterm{Analogous} Similar in function or purpose to another structure or process, despite having a different origin or structure.

\medterm{Analysis} Systematic examination or interpretation of a specimen, measurement, dataset, symptom pattern, or clinical problem.

\medterm{Anaphia} Loss or absence of the sense of touch or tactile sensation.

\medterm{Anaphylactoid purpura} An older term for IgA vasculitis, an immune-mediated small-vessel vasculitis causing palpable purpura, often with joint, abdominal, or kidney involvement.

\medterm{Anaptic} Lacking a normal sense of touch or tactile sensation; the term is rarely used in modern clinical writing.

\medterm{Anastrozole} An aromatase inhibitor that lowers estrogen production and is used mainly in hormone-receptor-positive breast cancer, particularly after menopause.

\medterm{Anatomic orientation terms} Standard words used to describe the position and relationships of body structures, including superior, inferior, anterior, posterior, medial, lateral, proximal, distal, superficial, and deep.

\medterm{Anatomy, gross} The study of body structures visible without a microscope, including organs, regions, bones, muscles, vessels, and their relationships.

\medterm{ANC} Absolute neutrophil count, the number of circulating neutrophils in a blood sample; a low ANC indicates increased risk of bacterial and fungal infection.

\medterm{Androgenic} Relating to androgens or producing masculine sexual development and other androgen-mediated effects.

\medterm{Android pelvis} A relatively heart-shaped or funnel-shaped pelvic configuration with a narrower midpelvis, which may increase the risk of obstructed labor compared with a gynecoid pelvis.

\medterm{Anemic} Having anemia, meaning reduced red-cell mass or hemoglobin and therefore reduced blood oxygen-carrying capacity.

\medterm{Anger} An emotional response involving displeasure or perceived threat, ranging from mild irritation to intense hostility; persistent or harmful anger may require clinical assessment.

\medterm{Angiogram} An image of blood vessels, usually obtained after contrast material is injected, used to identify narrowing, blockage, aneurysm, malformation, or bleeding.

\medterm{Angioneurotic edema, hereditary} Hereditary angioedema, an inherited bradykinin-mediated disorder causing recurrent swelling of the skin, gastrointestinal tract, or airway, usually without hives.

\medterm{Angiopathy} Disease or abnormality of blood vessels, including changes caused by diabetes, inflammation, atherosclerosis, trauma, or inherited disorders.

\medterm{Angiotensin} A family of peptides in the renin--angiotensin system that regulate blood pressure and fluid balance, with angiotensin II causing vasoconstriction and stimulating aldosterone release.

\medterm{Angiotensin converting enzyme} An enzyme that converts angiotensin I to vasoconstricting angiotensin II and also breaks down bradykinin; it is inhibited by ACE-inhibitor medicines.

\medterm{Angiotensin receptor blocker} A medicine that blocks angiotensin II at the AT1 receptor, reducing vasoconstriction and aldosterone effects; it is used for hypertension, heart failure, and selected kidney disease.

\medterm{Angle-closure glaucoma} Glaucoma caused by narrowing or closure of the drainage angle, which can abruptly raise eye pressure and threaten vision; acute attacks require emergency treatment.

\medterm{Anion} Anion is an ion with a net negative electrical charge, such as chloride, bicarbonate, or phosphate in body fluids.

\medterm{Aniso-} A combining form meaning unequal or unequal in size, number, or degree.

\medterm{Anisometropia} A difference in the refractive power of the two eyes, which can cause unequal image focus, blurred vision, or amblyopia.

\medterm{Ankle bone} A bone forming the ankle joint, especially the talus or the distal tibia and fibula that form the ankle mortise.

\medterm{Ankylose} To become stiff or immobile at a joint, sometimes because the joint surfaces become fused.

\medterm{Ankylosing} Causing or involving progressive stiffness and fixation of a joint, often through fibrous or bony fusion.

\medterm{Annulus} A ring-shaped structure or band; examples include the annulus fibrosus around an intervertebral disk and the fibrous rings around heart valves.

\medterm{Anogenital tract} The connected anatomic region comprising the anus, perianal tissues, genital structures, and related mucosal surfaces.

\medterm{Anomaly} A deviation from the usual structure, development, function, or measurement of the body; it may be harmless or clinically significant.

\medterm{Anorgasmy} Inability to achieve orgasm despite adequate sexual stimulation; possible contributors include medications, hormonal or neurologic disorders, psychological factors, and relationship difficulties.

\medterm{Antagonist} A substance, drug, muscle, or other factor that opposes or blocks the action of another; a receptor antagonist binds without activating the receptor.

\medterm{Antegrade} Moving or occurring in the normal forward direction of flow or conduction, such as blood flow away from the heart or electrical conduction through the usual pathway.

\medterm{Anterior cruciate ligament} A strong ligament inside the knee that runs from the femur to the tibia and limits forward movement and rotation of the tibia.

\medterm{Antero-} A combining form meaning anterior or toward the front.

\medterm{Anterolisthesis} Forward displacement of one vertebral body relative to the vertebra below it, usually in the spine.

\medterm{Anteroposterior} Directed from front to back; an anteroposterior radiographic view passes through the body from the anterior surface toward the posterior surface.

\medterm{Anteroposterior (AP)} Directed from the front of the body toward the back; an AP radiograph is made with the X-ray beam entering anteriorly and exiting posteriorly.

\medterm{Anti-} A prefix meaning against, opposed to, or preventing a specified process or condition.

\medterm{Anti-infective} A medicine or other agent used to prevent or treat infection caused by microorganisms, including bacteria, viruses, fungi, or parasites.

\medterm{Antibacterial} Destroying bacteria or inhibiting their growth; the term may describe a drug, disinfectant, surface treatment, or immune defense.

\medterm{Anticholinergic} Blocking the effects of acetylcholine, commonly at muscarinic receptors; these drugs can reduce secretions and smooth-muscle activity but may cause dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, or confusion.

\medterm{Anticoagulant agent} A drug that reduces blood clot formation by interfering with the coagulation cascade; examples include heparin, warfarin, and direct oral anticoagulants.

\medterm{Antidepressants} Medicines used mainly to treat depressive disorders; different classes alter serotonin, norepinephrine, dopamine, or other signaling and may also treat anxiety, pain, or related conditions.

\medterm{Antidiabetic agent} A medicine that lowers blood glucose or improves glucose regulation in diabetes, such as insulin, metformin, sulfonylureas, or GLP-1 receptor agonists.

\medterm{Antidiuretic hormone (ADH)} Vasopressin, a hormone released from the posterior pituitary that increases water reabsorption in the kidney and, at higher concentrations, constricts blood vessels.

\medterm{Antiestrogen} A drug that blocks estrogen effects or reduces estrogen production; it may be used in hormone-sensitive breast cancer or to alter reproductive hormone signaling.

\medterm{Antifungal agent} A drug or chemical that kills fungi or inhibits their growth, used to treat fungal infections of the skin, mucosa, or internal organs.

\medterm{Antifungal medication} A medicine used to treat fungal infection; the choice and route depend on the organism and site, and examples include azoles, terbinafine, polyenes, and echinocandins.

\medterm{Antihistamines} Medicines that block histamine receptors; H1 antihistamines relieve allergy symptoms and some cause sedation, while H2 antihistamines reduce gastric acid secretion.

\medterm{Antimalarial} A drug used to prevent or treat malaria caused by Plasmodium parasites; selection depends on species, disease severity, resistance, and geographic exposure.

\medterm{Antimetabolite} A compound resembling a substance needed for metabolism or DNA synthesis that disrupts cellular processes; many antimetabolites are used as anticancer or immunosuppressive drugs.

\medterm{Antimicrobial agent} A substance that kills microorganisms or inhibits their growth, including antibacterial, antiviral, antifungal, and antiparasitic agents.

\medterm{Antimicrobial drug} A medicine used to treat infection by bacteria, viruses, fungi, or parasites; the drug is selected according to the organism, infection site, and patient factors.

\medterm{Antimony} A metallic chemical element whose compounds can be toxic; selected antimony compounds, such as the pentavalent agents, have been used to treat leishmaniasis.

\medterm{Antiprotozoal} Destroying protozoa or inhibiting their growth; antiprotozoal medicines treat infections such as malaria, giardiasis, or amoebiasis.

\medterm{Antiprotozoal drug} A medicine used to prevent or treat disease caused by protozoa, including drugs directed against malaria, intestinal protozoa, or tissue parasites.

\medterm{Antiretroviral} Active against retroviruses; antiretroviral medicines inhibit different stages of HIV replication and are generally used in combination therapy.
