\medterm{Antithyroid drug} A medicine that reduces thyroid hormone production or release, used to treat hyperthyroidism; examples include methimazole, propylthiouracil, and radioactive iodine.

\medterm{Antrum} A cavity or chamber in an organ, especially the lower part of the stomach near the pylorus or the air-filled maxillary sinus.

\medterm{AOA} An abbreviation commonly used for the American Optometric Association; in other clinical contexts it may have a different expansion.

\medterm{Aortal} Relating to the aorta, the large artery that carries blood from the left ventricle to the systemic circulation.

\medterm{Aortic} Relating to the aorta, the main artery carrying blood from the heart to the body.

\medterm{AP} An abbreviation commonly meaning anteroposterior, or directed from front to back; in some contexts it may have another medical expansion.

\medterm{AP, X-ray} An anteroposterior radiographic projection in which the X-ray beam travels from the front of the body toward the back.

\medterm{APA} An abbreviation for American Psychiatric Association, American Psychological Association, or American Podiatry Association; it can also mean antiphospholipid antibody or alkaline phosphatase activity.

\medterm{APC} An abbreviation that may refer to an antigen-presenting cell, an argon plasma coagulation procedure, or another context-specific medical term.

\medterm{APC (immunology)} Antigen-presenting cell, an immune cell that processes antigens and displays peptide fragments on major histocompatibility complex molecules to activate T cells.

\medterm{APC (medication)} A combination of aspirin, phenacetin, and caffeine; phenacetin-containing APC products were associated with kidney injury and are no longer widely used.

\medterm{APECED (autoimmune polyendocrinopathy)} An inherited autoimmune syndrome, also called autoimmune polyendocrine syndrome type 1, characterized by chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis, hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency, and other autoimmune disorders.

\medterm{Apex} The tip or pointed end of a structure; examples include the apex of the heart, formed mainly by the left ventricle, and the upper tip of a lung.

\medterm{Aphrasia} Loss or impairment of the ability to speak, often used as an older or less precise term related to aphasia.

\medterm{Apical} Relating to or located at the apex, or tip, of a structure such as the lung, heart, or tooth.

\medterm{APLS (antiphospholipid antibody syndrome)} An autoimmune disorder in which antiphospholipid antibodies are associated with abnormal blood clotting, recurrent pregnancy loss, thrombocytopenia, and sometimes migraine or heart-valve abnormalities.

\medterm{Apneic} Relating to or characterized by apnea, meaning a temporary cessation of breathing.

\medterm{Apophenia} The perception of meaningful patterns or connections in unrelated or random events; marked apophenia may occur in some psychiatric or neurologic conditions.

\medterm{Appendiceal} Relating to the vermiform appendix, a narrow pouch projecting from the beginning of the large intestine.

\medterm{Appendiceal cancer} A malignant tumor arising in the appendix; types include neuroendocrine tumors and epithelial neoplasms, which can rupture or spread mucus-producing tumor cells within the abdomen.

\medterm{Appendiceal rupture} Tearing or perforation of an inflamed appendix, allowing intestinal contents to enter the abdomen and potentially causing peritonitis or an abscess.

\medterm{Apposition} Placement of two structures next to or against each other; in wound care, good apposition means that tissue edges are accurately aligned.

\medterm{APS (autoimmune polyglandular syndrome)} A group of disorders in which autoimmune disease affects multiple endocrine glands; manifestations vary by type and may include adrenal insufficiency, hypoparathyroidism, and thyroid disease.

\medterm{Aquaporin} A membrane water-channel protein that permits rapid movement of water across cells; aquaporin-2 in kidney collecting ducts is regulated by vasopressin.

\medterm{ARD} An abbreviation that may mean acute respiratory disease or acute respiratory distress; the precise expansion depends on the clinical context.


\medterm{Arm} The upper limb between the shoulder and elbow; in everyday use, the term may also include the entire upper extremity.

\medterm{Aromatase} An enzyme that converts androgens into estrogens in tissues such as the ovaries, adipose tissue, and breast; it is inhibited by drugs such as anastrozole.

\medterm{Aromatherapy} Use of essential oils or other plant-derived scents, usually by inhalation or topical application, for perceived relaxation or symptom relief; evidence and safety vary by oil and use.

\medterm{Arrector pili} A small involuntary smooth muscle attached to a hair follicle that contracts to raise the hair and produce gooseflesh.

\medterm{Arsenic} A naturally occurring metalloid that is toxic in many forms; exposure can cause gastrointestinal, neurologic, cardiovascular, and skin effects, while arsenic trioxide has specific therapeutic uses.

\medterm{ART (antiretroviral therapy)} Combination treatment with antiretroviral medicines that suppress HIV replication, preserve immune function, and reduce the risk of HIV transmission and disease progression.

\medterm{Artemisinin} A plant-derived antimalarial compound from Artemisia annua; artemisinin derivatives are used in combination regimens, especially for falciparum malaria.

\medterm{Arteria femoralis} The femoral artery, the major artery of the thigh and continuation of the external iliac artery below the inguinal ligament.

\medterm{Arterial blood gas (ABG)} A test of arterial blood that measures pH, oxygen, carbon dioxide, and usually bicarbonate or related values to assess oxygenation, ventilation, and acid--base balance.

\medterm{Arterial tension} The pressure or partial pressure measured in arterial blood; depending on context, it may refer to blood pressure or to the tension of an arterial gas such as oxygen or carbon dioxide.

\medterm{Arteriosclerotic retinopathy} Retinal vascular changes associated with thickening or hardening of small arteries, often occurring with chronic hypertension or atherosclerotic disease.

\medterm{Arteriovenous malformation (AVM)} An abnormal tangle of arteries and veins connected without the normal capillary bed; an AVM can bleed, cause seizures or neurologic symptoms, or produce high-flow complications.

\medterm{Arthro-} A combining form meaning joint.

\medterm{Arthrogram} An image of a joint obtained after contrast material is introduced into or around the joint, used to evaluate structures such as cartilage, ligaments, or the joint capsule.

\medterm{Arthrosis} Degenerative disease of a joint, characterized by progressive cartilage loss and changes in the underlying bone; osteoarthritis is the most common example.

\medterm{Articulation} A joint or connection between bones; the term also refers to the production and clarity of speech sounds by the vocal organs.

\medterm{Artificial abortion} An induced termination of pregnancy using medication, a procedure, or both, rather than a spontaneous miscarriage.

\medterm{Artificial insemination by husband (AIH)} Placement of sperm from a woman's husband or male partner into her reproductive tract to achieve pregnancy, usually by intrauterine insemination.

\medterm{ASA (drug caution code)} A medication caution code indicating that a product contains acetylsalicylic acid, or aspirin.

\medterm{Ascorbic acid} Vitamin C, a water-soluble vitamin needed for collagen formation, wound healing, antioxidant activity, and normal immune and connective-tissue function.

\medterm{Ascus} An abbreviation for atypical squamous cells of undetermined significance, a Pap-test finding in which cervical squamous cells are abnormal but not clearly precancerous.

\medterm{Aseptic} Free from disease-causing microorganisms, or performed using methods intended to prevent microbial contamination; it does not necessarily mean sterile.

\medterm{ASL} American Sign Language, a visual language used by many Deaf and hard-of-hearing people.

\medterm{ASOT} Antistreptolysin O titer, a blood test measuring antibodies to streptolysin O produced by group A Streptococcus.

\medterm{Asp} The standard amino-acid abbreviation for aspartic acid, an acidic amino acid incorporated into proteins and involved in cellular metabolism.

\medterm{Aspartame} A low-calorie artificial sweetener made from two amino-acid components; people with phenylketonuria must avoid it because it provides phenylalanine.

\medterm{Aspartate aminotransferase (AST)} An enzyme found mainly in the liver, heart, and skeletal muscle; increased blood AST can indicate injury to these tissues, especially liver injury.

\medterm{Aspirate} To withdraw fluid, cells, or tissue with suction, usually through a needle; to aspirate can also mean to inhale material into the airway.

\medterm{Aspirin} Acetylsalicylic acid, a salicylate that relieves pain and fever, reduces inflammation, and irreversibly inhibits platelet aggregation; it can cause gastrointestinal bleeding and is generally avoided in children with viral illness.

\medterm{Association} A relationship or co-occurrence between two variables, findings, or conditions; association alone does not prove that one causes the other.

\medterm{Asthenic} Characterized by reduced strength or a slender, lightly built body habitus; in clinical descriptions it may refer to physical weakness or, less precisely, an asthenic constitution and should be distinguished from asthenia as a symptom.

\medterm{Athelia} Congenital or acquired absence of one or both nipples.

\medterm{Atherosclerotic} Relating to atherosclerosis, the buildup of lipid-rich plaque and fibrous tissue in arterial walls that can narrow arteries or promote thrombosis.

\medterm{Atlas and axis joint} The atlantoaxial joint between the first cervical vertebra, the atlas, and the second, the axis; it permits much of the head's rotation.

\medterm{ATM} An abbreviation that may mean the temporomandibular joint or ataxia-telangiectasia mutated, a protein kinase involved in DNA-damage repair; context determines the meaning.

\medterm{Atom} The smallest unit of an element that retains that element's chemical properties, consisting of a nucleus surrounded by electrons.

\medterm{Atonic} Lacking normal muscle tone or contractile strength; an atonic bladder, for example, does not contract effectively to empty urine.

\medterm{Atopic} Relating to an inherited or acquired tendency to develop allergic reactions such as eczema, allergic rhinitis, or asthma.

\medterm{ATP} Adenosine triphosphate, the principal energy-transfer molecule in cells; hydrolysis of its phosphate bonds powers many cellular processes.

\medterm{Atresia} Congenital absence, closure, or severe narrowing of a normal body opening or passage, such as the intestinal, biliary, or pulmonary outflow tract.

\medterm{Atria} The two upper chambers of the heart, which receive blood from the systemic and pulmonary veins and pass it to the ventricles.

\medterm{Atrial} Relating to an atrium, especially one of the two upper chambers of the heart.

\medterm{Atrial fib} Atrial fibrillation, an irregular and often rapid atrial rhythm that can reduce coordinated heart contraction and increase the risk of blood clots and stroke.

\medterm{Atrial septum} The wall separating the right and left atria; an atrial septal defect is an abnormal opening in this wall.

\medterm{Atrioventricular (AV)} Relating to an atrium and ventricle, especially the atrioventricular valves or the conduction system connecting the atria and ventricles.

\medterm{Atropine} An anticholinergic drug that blocks muscarinic acetylcholine receptors; it can increase heart rate, reduce secretions, dilate the pupils, and treat selected poisonings or bradycardia.

\medterm{Attention} The ability to select, focus on, and sustain mental processing of relevant information while limiting distraction.

\medterm{Attention deficit disorder (ADD)} An older term for a condition now generally included under attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, especially a presentation dominated by persistent inattention that interferes with daily functioning.

\medterm{Attenuated} Weakened or reduced in force, severity, concentration, or biological activity; an attenuated vaccine contains a weakened form of a microorganism.

\medterm{Atypical} Not typical of the usual appearance, behavior, structure, or course of a condition; atypical does not by itself indicate whether a finding is benign or malignant.

\medterm{Atypical measles syndrome (AMS)} A rare severe illness formerly reported after exposure to measles virus in people who had received an inactivated measles vaccine; it can cause unusual rash, high fever, and pneumonia.

\medterm{Auditory acuity} The sensitivity or sharpness of hearing, commonly assessed by determining the softest sounds a person can detect at different frequencies.

\medterm{Auditory perception} The brain's process of detecting, recognizing, and interpreting sounds received through the auditory system.

\medterm{Autistic savant} A person with autism who has an unusually advanced, specific ability—such as in music, art, calculation, or memory—relative to overall functioning.

\medterm{Autoimmune} Relating to an immune response directed against the body's own cells, tissues, or molecules.

\medterm{Autoimmunity} An immune response against self-antigens; it can be harmless or can cause autoimmune disease when it damages tissues or disrupts their function.

\medterm{Autologous} Derived from or involving the same individual who will receive it, such as an autologous blood transfusion or stem-cell transplant.

\medterm{Autolysis} Self-digestion of cells or tissues by their own enzymes, commonly occurring after cell death and contributing to decomposition.

\medterm{Automated external defibrillator} A portable device that analyzes heart rhythm and delivers an electrical shock when it detects a shockable cardiac arrest rhythm such as ventricular fibrillation.

\medterm{Autonomy, patient} A patient's right and capacity to make informed, voluntary decisions about medical care, including accepting or refusing treatment.

\medterm{Autophobia} An excessive or irrational fear of being alone; it may occur as a specific phobia or alongside other anxiety conditions.

\medterm{AV (atrioventricular)} An abbreviation for atrioventricular, relating to an atrium and ventricle or to the conduction pathway between them.

\medterm{Avuncular} Relating to an uncle or resembling the kindly, familiar manner traditionally associated with an uncle.

\medterm{Axial tomography, computerized} Computed tomography, or CT, an imaging method that uses X-rays and computer processing to create cross-sectional images of the body.

\medterm{Ayurveda} A traditional system of medicine from India that emphasizes balance among bodily principles and uses individualized diet, lifestyle practices, and herbal or other treatments.

\medterm{Arteries} Blood vessels that carry blood away from the heart. Most arteries carry oxygenated blood, except the pulmonary and umbilical arteries; their walls contain elastic tissue and smooth muscle to withstand and regulate pressure.

\synonyms
arterial vessels

\medterm{ABO Blood Types} The major blood-group system based on the presence or absence of A and B antigens on red blood cells and corresponding antibodies in plasma.

\medterm{Atopic dermatitis (eczema)} A chronic inflammatory skin condition causing itchy, dry, irritated, or rash-covered skin, often associated with an atopic tendency.

\medterm{Actinic Keratosis (Solar Keratosis)} A rough, scaly precancerous skin lesion caused by long-term ultraviolet exposure and capable of progressing to squamous cell carcinoma.

\synonyms
Solar Keratosis

\medterm{Adolescent Obesity} Obesity during adolescence, assessed using age- and sex-specific growth charts rather than adult BMI thresholds alone. It may affect metabolic, cardiovascular, orthopedic, and psychological health.

\medterm{A \& E Medicine} Accident and emergency medicine provides rapid assessment, stabilization, diagnosis, and initial treatment for acute illness, injury, and other urgent conditions.

\medterm{Abdomen, Diseases of} Diseases of the abdomen affect abdominal organs, blood vessels, lining, or abdominal wall and may cause pain, vomiting, altered bowel function, bleeding, or distention.

\medterm{Abduct} To abduct a limb or body part is to move it away from the body’s midline; the opposite movement is adduction.

\medterm{ABO Blood Groups} The ABO blood-group system classifies red cells by A and B antigens and plasma antibodies; compatibility is essential for safe transfusion and transplantation.

\medterm{ABO System} The major red-cell blood-group system based on whether A and/or B antigens are present on the red-cell surface and the corresponding antibodies are present in plasma. The four principal phenotypes are A, B, AB, and O; correct ABO matching is critical for safe transfusion.

\medterm{Abreaction} Abreaction is the expression and re-experiencing of intense emotion linked to a distressing memory; it is a historical psychological term and is not itself a diagnosis.

\medterm{Abruptio Placenta} Abruptio placenta, more commonly called placental abruption, is premature separation of the placenta from the uterine wall before delivery, which can cause maternal bleeding and fetal compromise.

\medterm{Abstract} In medical literature, an abstract is a brief summary of a study or report describing its purpose, methods, main results, and conclusions.

\medterm{Acarus} Acarus is a mite genus; mites and related arthropods can cause skin infestation, allergy, or occupational exposure depending on the species.

\medterm{Accident and Emergency Medicine} Accident and emergency medicine is the specialty concerned with immediate evaluation and treatment of acute illness, injury, poisoning, and life-threatening emergencies.

\medterm{Accouchement} Accouchement is the process of childbirth, including labor, delivery of the infant, and expulsion of the placenta.

\medterm{Accretion} Accretion is gradual accumulation or growth of material; in medicine it may describe deposits such as dental calculus or mineral buildup around a lesion or device.

\medterm{Acebutolol} Acebutolol is a beta-adrenergic blocker with intrinsic sympathomimetic activity used in some settings for hypertension and cardiac rhythm disorders; it can slow heart rate and conduction.

\medterm{ACE Inhibitors} ACE inhibitors block angiotensin-converting enzyme, lowering angiotensin II and aldosterone; they treat hypertension, heart failure, and kidney protection in selected patients but can cause cough, hyperkalemia, or angioedema.

\medterm{Acetazolamide} Acetazolamide inhibits carbonic anhydrase and promotes bicarbonate loss in urine; it is used for glaucoma, altitude illness, selected edema, and some acid-base or neurologic disorders.

\medterm{Acetylsalicyclic Acid} Acetylsalicyclic acid is the commonly misspelled name for acetylsalicylic acid, or aspirin, a salicylate that reduces pain, fever, and inflammation and inhibits platelet aggregation.

\medterm{Achalasia of the Cardia} Achalasia of the cardia is failure of the lower esophageal sphincter to relax with loss of esophageal peristalsis, causing progressive swallowing difficulty, regurgitation, and food retention.

\medterm{Achromia} Achromia is loss or marked reduction of pigment in skin, hair, eye, or another tissue; causes include genetic disorders, autoimmune depigmentation, injury, and chemical exposure.

\medterm{Aciclovir} Aciclovir, also called acyclovir, is an antiviral drug that inhibits herpesvirus DNA polymerase and is used for infections such as herpes simplex and varicella-zoster.

\medterm{Aciduria} Aciduria means an abnormally acidic urine or the presence of excess organic acids in urine; it may reflect diet, dehydration, medication, or an inherited metabolic disorder.

\medterm{Acinus} An acinus is a small functional secretory unit of a gland, such as a pancreatic or salivary acinus, whose cells release their product into a duct.

\medterm{Acne Rosacea} Acne rosacea is a chronic inflammatory facial skin disorder causing flushing, persistent redness, visible vessels, papules, pustules, or eye symptoms; it is distinct from acne vulgaris.

\medterm{Acro-} Acro- is a medical word element meaning extremity, tip, or highest part, as in acromegaly and acrocyanosis.

\medterm{Acroparaesthesia} Acroparaesthesia is tingling, pins-and-needles, burning, or altered sensation in the hands or feet, commonly related to nerve compression, neuropathy, vascular disease, or metabolic causes.

\medterm{ACTH (Adrenocorticotrophic Hormone)} ACTH is a pituitary hormone that stimulates the adrenal cortex to produce cortisol and some adrenal androgens; testing helps evaluate pituitary and adrenal disorders.

\medterm{Actinomycin D} Actinomycin D, or dactinomycin, is an antineoplastic antibiotic that blocks DNA-dependent RNA synthesis and is used for selected cancers; myelosuppression and tissue injury are important risks.


\medterm{Acute Life-Threatening Event (ALTE)} ALTE is an older term for an unexpected infant event involving altered breathing, color, tone, or responsiveness; current practice generally uses the more specific term brief resolved unexplained event (BRUE).

\medterm{Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)} ARDS is acute inflammatory lung injury causing severe hypoxemia and diffuse pulmonary opacities that are not fully explained by heart failure or fluid overload.

\medterm{Adactyly} Adactyly is congenital absence of one or more fingers or toes, ranging from loss of a digit to more extensive limb malformation.

\medterm{Adaptation} Adaptation is the adjustment of a cell, organism, or person to a changing internal or external condition; in medicine it may involve physiologic compensation, behavior, or rehabilitation.

\medterm{Adduct} To adduct a limb or body part is to move it toward the body’s midline; the opposite movement is abduction.

\medterm{Adenolipoma} An adenolipoma is a usually benign tumor containing both glandular tissue and mature fatty tissue.

\medterm{Adenomatosis} Adenomatosis is the presence of multiple adenomas or diffuse adenomatous overgrowth in an organ or tissue; its implications depend on the site and underlying syndrome.

\medterm{Adenosine Triphosphate (ATP)} ATP is the cell’s principal immediately usable energy carrier; hydrolysis of its high-energy phosphate bonds powers transport, movement, synthesis, and signaling.

\medterm{Adenoviruses} Adenoviruses are non-enveloped DNA viruses that can cause respiratory, eye, gastrointestinal, urinary, or other infections, usually self-limited but sometimes severe in vulnerable people.

\medterm{Adiposis Dolorosa} Adiposis dolorosa, or Dercum disease, is a rare disorder characterized by painful fatty deposits, often with obesity, fatigue, and other systemic symptoms.

\medterm{Adoption} In healthcare and genetics, adoption may be relevant to family history, consent, psychosocial care, and assessment when biological family information is unavailable or differs from the caregiving family.

\medterm{Adrenocorticotrophic Hormone (ACTH)} Adrenocorticotrophic hormone, or ACTH, is released by the pituitary and stimulates the adrenal cortex, especially cortisol production; abnormal levels help localize endocrine disease.

\medterm{Advance Statements about Medical Treatment} Advance statements about medical treatment record a person’s preferences, values, and wishes for future care if illness later prevents effective communication; legal status varies by jurisdiction.

\medterm{Adverse Reactions to Drugs} Adverse drug reactions are harmful, unintended responses to medicines taken at normal doses, ranging from predictable side effects to allergic, organ-toxic, or idiosyncratic reactions.

\medterm{Aëdes Aegypti} Aëdes aegypti, now usually written Aedes aegypti, is a mosquito that transmits dengue, yellow fever, chikungunya, Zika, and other viruses.

\medterm{Aegophony} Aegophony is an altered “E” sound heard through a stethoscope as an “A,” classically suggesting lung consolidation or fluid-related change when present over the chest.

\medterm{Aerobic Bacterium} An aerobic bacterium grows using oxygen, although some species can tolerate or use alternative metabolic pathways; oxygen requirements help guide culture and infection assessment.

\medterm{Afibrinogenaemia} Afibrinogenaemia is severe or complete deficiency of fibrinogen, an essential clotting protein, causing a bleeding tendency; it is usually inherited but may be acquired.

\medterm{Agammaglobulinaemia} Agammaglobulinaemia is profound deficiency of immunoglobulins and mature B cells, leading to recurrent bacterial infections; X-linked disease is a classic form.

\medterm{Ageing} Ageing is the progressive biological and functional change over time, including altered repair, immunity, metabolism, organ reserve, and susceptibility to disease.

\medterm{Agglutinogen} An agglutinogen is an antigen, often on a red blood cell, that can trigger production of a corresponding agglutinin and cause visible cell clumping in a compatible reaction.

\medterm{AI} In medicine, AI means artificial intelligence: computer methods that analyze data or recognize patterns to support research, diagnosis, prediction, treatment, or clinical workflow; they require validation and oversight.

\medterm{Air Passages} The air passages are the conducting airways from the nose and mouth through the pharynx, larynx, trachea, bronchi, and bronchioles; obstruction can impair ventilation.

\medterm{Air-Sickness} Air-sickness is motion sickness triggered by aircraft movement or conflicting visual and vestibular signals, causing nausea, dizziness, sweating, and sometimes vomiting.

\medterm{Alastrim} Alastrim is a historical name for a generally milder form of smallpox, caused by variola minor; smallpox has been eradicated worldwide.

\medterm{Albendazole} Albendazole is a benzimidazole antiparasitic drug that disrupts parasite microtubules and is used for several intestinal and tissue helminth infections.

\medterm{Albumins} Albumins are water-soluble proteins, especially serum albumin made by the liver, that maintain oncotic pressure and transport hormones, fatty acids, drugs, and other substances.

\medterm{Alcuronium} Alcuronium is a nondepolarizing neuromuscular-blocking drug that causes skeletal-muscle paralysis during anesthesia; it requires airway and respiratory support.

\medterm{Alfacalcidol} Alfacalcidol is a vitamin-D analogue converted in the liver to an active form that increases calcium and phosphate absorption and is used in selected bone and mineral disorders.

\medterm{Alglucerase} Alglucerase is a modified form of the lysosomal enzyme glucocerebrosidase formerly used as enzyme-replacement therapy for Gaucher disease; newer preparations are more commonly used.

\medterm{Algorithm} A medical algorithm is a defined sequence of rules or calculations used to support diagnosis, risk assessment, treatment, triage, or data analysis; it does not replace clinical judgment.

\medterm{Alimentary Canal} The alimentary canal is the continuous digestive tube from mouth to anus, including the pharynx, esophagus, stomach, small intestine, and large intestine.

\medterm{Alkali} An alkali is a basic substance that accepts hydrogen ions or produces hydroxide in solution; caustic alkalis can cause severe chemical burns, while controlled bases have medical and laboratory uses.

\medterm{Alkaloids} Alkaloids are nitrogen-containing plant or microbial compounds with diverse biologic effects; examples include morphine, quinine, nicotine, and atropine.

\medterm{Alkylating Agents} Alkylating agents are drugs or chemicals that add alkyl groups to DNA or other molecules; anticancer agents exploit this to inhibit replication, while normal-tissue toxicity includes myelosuppression and mutagenic risk.

\medterm{Allantoin} Allantoin is a compound used in some topical products for its skin-protective, moisturizing, and keratolytic properties; formulation and indication determine its clinical role.

\medterm{Allantois} The allantois is an embryonic membrane involved in early waste handling, blood-vessel development, and formation of structures related to the umbilicus and urinary bladder.

\medterm{Azotaemia} Azotaemia is excess nitrogen-containing waste, especially urea and creatinine, in the blood, usually reflecting reduced kidney filtration, increased production, or altered renal blood flow.

\medterm{Acute Decompensated Heart Failure} A rapid onset or worsening of heart-failure symptoms and signs caused by increased cardiac filling pressures and/or inadequate cardiac output. It commonly presents with breathlessness, pulmonary or systemic congestion, edema, or fatigue and requires prompt assessment for precipitating causes and urgent treatment when respiratory failure, hypotension, or organ dysfunction is present.

\medterm{ANOCA} Angina with non-obstructive coronary arteries: angina symptoms without a flow-limiting obstructive stenosis in the epicardial coronary arteries. Causes may include coronary microvascular dysfunction or vasospastic angina, and further evaluation can help identify the mechanism.

\medterm{Appendageal} Relating to skin appendages or structures derived from the skin, including hair follicles, sweat glands, and sebaceous glands.

\medterm{Arciform} Having a curved or arch-like shape, used to describe the configuration of a skin lesion, rash, or other anatomical finding.

\medterm{Asymmetrical} Unequal in shape, size, distribution, or appearance between corresponding sides; asymmetry of a pigmented lesion can be a warning feature requiring assessment.
